🏠 Master Summary & Implementation RoadmapSTART HERE

🎯 What This Document Is — And How To Use It

This is your complete Grafana Certified Associate exam preparation system. It's not just a study guide — it's a training simulator built around a real Django application (anihpj/jobpost) monitored with Grafana + Prometheus + Loki + Tempo — the LGTM Stack. Every section is designed to build real observability skills through progressive difficulty: theory → troubleshooting → hands-on practice.

📝

200 MCQs
Build theory

Diagnostic patterns

100 Scenarios
Hands-on labs
🌳
DT + Appx
15 Trees + 13 Appx
Quick reference

📝 Part 1: 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Purpose: Build foundational knowledge across all 5 CKA domains. Every question has a detailed explanation with real-world context from anihpj/jobpost.

Domains covered: Storage (Q1-Q20), Troubleshooting (Q21-Q80), Workloads (Q81-Q110), Cluster Architecture (Q111-Q160), Networking (Q161-Q200).

How to use: Answer 20-30 questions per study session. Read the explanation even for correct answers — they contain deeper insights. Target: 85%+ correct before moving to Part 2.

🔧 Part 2: 97 Troubleshooting Issues

Purpose: Learn systematic diagnostic reasoning. Each issue follows the same pattern: Symptom → Terminal Output → 15 Causes (Most Likely / Less Likely / New Cluster) → Diagnostic Commands → Solution → Personal Advice.

Categories: ST1-ST10 (Storage), TR1-TR30 (Troubleshooting), WS1-WS15 (Workloads), CA1-CA22 (Cluster Architecture), SN1-SN20 (Networking).

How to use: Read the symptom first — try to diagnose BEFORE reading the causes. This builds the diagnostic muscle memory critical for 30% of your CKA score.

🧪 Part 3: 100 Hands-On Lab Scenarios

Purpose: Practice real CKA exam tasks on your 6-cluster lab. Each scenario deploys anihpj/jobpost with an intentional bug — you find and fix it.

Structure per scenario: APP STORY → FILE STRUCTURE → Deploy YAML (with bug) → Observe symptoms → Debug commands → Full answer with before/after outputs → Fix commands → Cleanup.

How to use: Run each scenario on the specified cluster (ksw → 1-6). Time yourself — aim for 5-7 minutes per scenario. Target: Complete all 100 scenarios before exam day.

🌳 Decision Trees + Appendices

Decision Trees (DT1-DT15): Quick diagnostic flowcharts for the 15 most common CKA issues. Start at the symptom, answer yes/no questions, arrive at the fix.

Appendices (A-M): Answer keys, top 50 commands, YAML templates, exam-day survival guide, cheat sheets, architecture diagrams, difficulty index, and the ⚡ Appendix M: 20 Exam Day Command Patterns — your last-30-minutes cram sheet.

How to use: Decision trees for quick reference during practice. Appendix M in the 30 minutes before exam. Cheat sheet (Appendix I) for last-minute review.

✅ Daily Implementation Checklist
  • Morning (30 min): Read 5-10 MCQs from Part 1 + review explanations. Focus on one domain per day.
  • Afternoon (45 min): Study 3-5 Part 2 issues. Read the symptom, try to diagnose, then read the solution. Note patterns you missed.
  • Evening (60 min): Run 3-5 Part 3 lab scenarios on your cluster. Type every command — don't copy-paste. Time each scenario.
  • Weekend (2-3 hours): Full review of the week's topics. Run 10 scenarios back-to-back. Review decision trees for any weak areas.
  • Day before exam: Read Appendix M (20 Command Patterns) twice. Type M.1, M.2, M.6, M.10 from memory. Get a good night's sleep.
  • Exam day morning: 30 minutes before: Read Appendix M one final time. Set up your environment: alias k=kubectl, export KUBECONFIG=..., verify context.

🏆 Skills You'll Master — Before vs After This Guide

❌ BEFORE — Common Beginner Pitfalls

  • ❌ "Why is my pod stuck in Pending?" — No idea where to look
  • ❌ "The Service works but returns 503" — Don't know about selectors
  • ❌ "I updated the ConfigMap but nothing changed" — Didn't know pods need restart
  • ❌ "kubectl commands take forever" — Never checked etcd or API server health
  • ❌ "The node is down but I can't drain it" — PDB concept unknown
  • ❌ "I ran commands on the wrong cluster" — No context discipline
  • ❌ "etcd backup? What's etcd?" — No disaster recovery plan

✅ AFTER — Skills This Guide Builds

  • ✅ Diagnose any pod failure in <2 min using systematic Tenet Flow
  • ✅ Fix Service/NetworkPolicy/DNS issues using endpoint & selector checks
  • ✅ Trigger rolling restarts, rollbacks, and canary deployments confidently
  • ✅ Monitor etcd health, compact & defrag, restore from snapshots
  • ✅ Cordon → Drain → Work → Uncordon with PDB awareness
  • ✅ Switch contexts safely and verify before every destructive command
  • ✅ Backup/restore etcd, renew TLS certs, upgrade clusters without downtime

🚨 Critical Do's & Don'ts During The Exam
  • ✅ DO: Use kubectl create ... --dry-run=client -o yaml to generate YAML in seconds — never type manifests from scratch.
  • ✅ DO: Run kubectl config use-context <given-context> as the FIRST command for every task. Wrong context = 0 points.
  • ✅ DO: Use kubectl explain <resource> --recursive when you forget a field name. It's faster than searching docs.
  • ✅ DO: Create a debug pod: kubectl run test --rm -it --image=busybox -- sh to test DNS, connectivity, and curl from inside the cluster.
  • ❌ DON'T: Spend more than 7 minutes on any single task. Flag it, move on, return with fresh eyes.
  • ❌ DON'T: Forget to set the namespace. Many tasks require -n flag. Use kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=<ns> to save typing.
  • ❌ DON'T: Leave any task blank. Even partial work (creating the namespace, writing partial YAML) may earn partial credit.
  • ❌ DON'T: Use kubectl delete pod on a static pod — you must delete the manifest file from /etc/kubernetes/manifests/.

📊 Exam Overview & StrategySTRATEGY

Grafana Certified Associate — Exam Format & Strategy

Exam Duration: 90 minutes | Format: Multiple choice + Multiple response | Passing Score: 70% | Cost: $99 | Certification Validity: 2 years | Questions: ~60

6 Official Domains: Grafana Fundamentals (15%), Data Sources & Queries (20%), Dashboards & Visualizations (25%), Alerting (15%), Administration & Security (15%), Troubleshooting (10%). Dashboards (25%) + Data Sources (20%) = 45% of your score — prioritize these!

Exam Strategy: The exam is multiple-choice based, taken online with a proctor. You have access to the Grafana documentation during the exam. Use the 3-pass method: Pass 1 — answer all easy questions (30 min), Pass 2 — medium difficulty (40 min), Pass 3 — review flagged + hard (20 min). Never leave a question blank — there's no penalty for wrong answers.

Your monitoring lab below runs anihpj/jobpost with Grafana + Prometheus + Loki on Docker & Kubernetes. Use the Explore mode and dashboards to verify concepts hands-on.

📚 Pre-Learning: Observability FoundationsF.1–F.5

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building Your Mental Model

F.1 What Is Observability & Why Does It Matter? — The "Dark Age" of monitoring: SSH + grep + tail. The Three Pillars: Metrics, Logs, Traces. MTTR from hours to minutes with Grafana.

F.2 Grafana vs The Competition — Grafana vs Datadog vs New Relic vs Kibana vs CloudWatch. The LGTM Stack explained. Grafana in the CNCF ecosystem with Prometheus & OpenTelemetry.

F.3 Grafana Data Flow — How a Query Becomes a Visualization — Step-by-step: Browser → Grafana Server → Data Source Plugin → Backend → Response → Render. The rendering pipeline explained.

F.4 The anihpj/jobpost Application Stack — Django Web + API + PostgreSQL + Redis + Celery. What we monitor: HTTP rates, error rates, latency, DB queries, Redis cache hits, Celery tasks, business metrics.

F.5 Setting Up Your Monitoring Lab — Docker & Kubernetes — docker-compose up → 7 containers (web, api, db, redis, grafana, prometheus, loki). Kubernetes: kubectl apply -f k8s/ → monitoring namespace with pre-built dashboards.

If you're new to Grafana: Read through F.1–F.5 before diving into Part 1. These foundations will give you the mental models needed to understand the MCQs, issues, and scenarios. The entire guide uses the anihpj/jobpost Django application monitored by Grafana + Prometheus + Loki as its running example.

🚀 How to Use This Guide — Implementation RoadmapSTRATEGY

200 MCQs · 6 Domains · LGTM Stack · anihpj/jobpost · From Zero to Grafana Certified Associate

📊 The 6 Domains — At a Glance

15% 🟠 1. Grafana Fundamentals Q1-Q30

What Grafana IS — architecture, navigation, dashboards, sharing, time ranges. The "control tower" mental model. 30 questions.

20% 🟢 2. Data Sources & Queries Q31-Q70

Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, SQL DS — connecting, querying, transforming data. PromQL, LogQL, SQL. 40 questions.

25% 🟡 3. Dashboards & Visualizations Q71-Q120

BIGGEST domain. 18 panel types, transformations, variables, library panels, provisioning, JSON model. 50 questions.

15% 🟣 4. Alerting Q121-Q150

Unified alerting, multi-dimensional alerts, notification policies, contact points, silences, templates. 30 questions.

15% 🔴 5. Administration & Security Q151-Q180

Auth (OAuth2, LDAP, SAML), RBAC, orgs, grafana.ini, provisioning, backup, TLS, grafana-cli. 30 questions.

10% ⚪ 6. Troubleshooting Q181-Q200

Systematic debugging — No Data, connection refused, auth failures, query timeouts, performance, OAuth loops. 20 questions.

⚡ Quick Reference — Key Concepts Per Domain

# Domain Weight Qs Must-Know Concepts Implementation
1 Fundamentals 15% 30 Architecture, navigation, dashboards, sharing, time ranges, keyboard shortcuts docker-compose up → grafana:3000
2 Data Sources & Queries 20% 40 PromQL, LogQL, SQL DS, Loki, Prometheus, PostgreSQL, data links Add DS → Save & Test → Explore
3 Dashboards & Viz 25% 50 18 panel types, transformations, variables, library panels, provisioning Dashboard → Add panel → Query → Save
4 Alerting 15% 30 Unified alerting, multi-dimensional, contact points, notification policies, silences Panel → Alert tab → Reduce → Threshold
5 Admin & Security 15% 30 OAuth2, LDAP, RBAC, orgs, grafana.ini, provisioning, TLS, backup grafana.ini → env vars → K8s ConfigMap
6 Troubleshooting 10% 20 No Data, connection refused, auth failures, timeouts, OAuth loops, logs Save & Test → Explore → grafana.log

🗄️ Core Data Sources — Deep Dive Comparison

  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │  PORT         │   :9090         │   :3100          │   :5432                  │
  │  QUERY LANG   │   PromQL        │   LogQL          │   SQL                    │
  │  DATA TYPE    │   Numeric       │   Text           │   Relational             │
  │  STORAGE      │   TSDB (disk)   │   Object Store   │   PostgreSQL tables       │
  │  RETENTION    │   15d default   │   Configurable   │   Permanent              │
  │  SCRAPE/PUSH  │   Pull (scrape) │   Push (Promtail)│   Push (app writes)      │
  │               │                 │                  │                          │
  │  ANIHPJ USE:  │                 │                  │                          │
  │  ──────────── │ HTTP req rate   │ Django error logs│ Active jobs count        │
  │               │ Error rate %    │ Nginx access logs│ Applications today       │
  │               │ Latency p95     │ PG slow queries  │ Users registered         │
  │               │ CPU/Mem/Disk    │ Redis cache logs │ Revenue metrics          │
  │               │ Celery tasks    │ Auth attempts    │ Job categories           │
  │               │                 │                  │                          │
  │  GRAFANA      │ Time Series     │ Logs panel       │ Table / Stat             │
  │  PANEL TYPE   │ Stat / Gauge    │ Explore          │ Time Series (with SQL)   │
  │               │ Heatmap         │                  │ Bar Gauge                │
  └───────────────┴─────────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

⚔️ Exam Day Battle Plan

1 First 5 Minutes
  • Read ALL questions (don't answer yet)
  • Flag the easy ones — do them FIRST
  • Bookmark hard ones for later
  • Check time: you have ~90 min for ~60 Qs
2 Answering Strategy
  • Process of elimination — remove 2 wrong answers
  • Look for ABSOLUTE words: "always", "never", "only"
  • Look for SPECIFIC words: "deprecated", "recommended"
  • Trust your first instinct (don't overthink)
3 Time Management
  • ~90 seconds per question max
  • If stuck > 2 min → bookmark, move on
  • Return to bookmarked Qs with remaining time
  • NEVER leave a question blank (no penalty)
4 Final Review
  • Review ALL flagged/bookmarked questions
  • Double-check answers with "always"/"never"
  • Make sure you didn't misread the question
  • Submit with confidence — 70% is PASSING!

Complete Grafana Associate MCQ Bank — Organized by the 6 official Grafana exam domains. Each question includes a detailed explanation, diagrams, and real-world context using anihpj/jobpost monitored with Grafana + Prometheus + Loki.

🟠 Category 1: Grafana Fundamentals (15%)Q1-Q30

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Understanding What Grafana IS

This category is about understanding Grafana at its core — what it is, how it works, and how to navigate it. Think of Grafana as a control tower at a busy port — it doesn't store the cargo (data), but it tells you exactly where every ship (data source) is, what it's carrying, and if any are in trouble. For anihpj/jobpost, Grafana connects to Prometheus (metrics), Loki (logs), and PostgreSQL (business data) to give you ONE unified view of your entire application.

Q1-Q30 30 Questions 🟢 Beginner

1.1 Grafana Architecture & Core ConceptsQ1-Q8

Fundamentals Q1 of 200
What is Grafana? Choose the BEST description.
A) Grafana is a time-series database that stores metrics, logs, and traces
B) Grafana is an open-source analytics and interactive visualization platform that connects to data sources and creates dashboards — it does NOT store data itself
C) Grafana is a monitoring agent that runs on every server to collect metrics
D) Grafana is a cloud service for log aggregation and analysis
✅ Correct Answer: B

Grafana is a visualization platform, not a data store. It connects to your existing data sources (Prometheus, PostgreSQL, Loki, Elasticsearch, 100+ more) and creates beautiful, interactive dashboards. It's the "one pane of glass" for all your observability data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Grafana Architecture — The Control Tower Analogy
  🟠 GRAFANA (Control Tower — Port 3000)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  📊 Dashboards  🔔 Alerts  🔍 Explore       │
  │  "I visualize data, I don't store it"      │
  └────────┬──────────┬──────────┬─────────────┘
           │          │          │
     ┌─────▼────┐ ┌───▼────┐ ┌─▼──────────┐
     │Prometheus│ │  Loki  │ │PostgreSQL  │
     │ Metrics  │ │  Logs  │ │Biz Data    │
     │ :9090    │ │ :3100  │ │ :5432      │
     └──────────┘ └────────┘ └────────────┘
  
  Grafana NEVER stores your data — it proxies
  queries to data sources and renders the results.

For anihpj/jobpost, Grafana connects to Prometheus (scraping Django /metrics), Loki (aggregating Django + PostgreSQL + Nginx logs), and PostgreSQL (business data like active jobs, user counts). All three appear in ONE dashboard — the "anihpj Production Overview."

Fundamentals Q2 of 200
Which components form Grafana's core architecture?
A) A Python backend with Angular frontend and a built-in time-series database
B) A monolithic Java application that stores dashboards in etcd
C) A React frontend (UI panels), a Go backend (server + API), and a plugin system that connects to external data sources
D) A desktop application that reads CSV files and generates PNG charts
✅ Correct Answer: C

Grafana has three architectural layers: the React-based frontend (what you see — panels, dashboards, settings), the Go backend server (business logic, authentication, API, query proxying), and the plugin system (data source plugins, panel plugins, app plugins). There is NO built-in database — Grafana relies entirely on external data sources.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Grafana's 3-Layer Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                  🟠 GRAFANA SERVER                       │
  │                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  │
  │  │  FRONTEND    │  │   BACKEND    │  │   PLUGINS    │  │
  │  │  (React)     │  │   (Go)       │  │   System     │  │
  │  │              │  │              │  │              │  │
  │  │ • Panels     │  │ • API        │  │ • 100+ Data  │  │
  │  │ • Dashboards │  │ • Auth       │  │   Sources    │  │
  │  │ • Settings   │──│ • Query      │──│ • 50+ Panels │  │
  │  │ • Navigation │  │   Proxy      │  │ • App Exts   │  │
  │  │              │  │ • Alerting   │  │              │  │
  │  │  Browser     │  │   Engine     │  │  Connects    │  │
  │  │  renders UI  │  │              │  │  to external │  │
  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  │  data stores │  │
  │                                      └──────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the React frontend renders your "anihpj Production Overview" dashboard. The Go backend proxies your queries to Prometheus (metrics), PostgreSQL (jobs/users), and Loki (logs). The plugin system makes all three data sources available as dropdown options when you create panels.

Fundamentals Q3 of 200
Your team needs Grafana for anihpj/jobpost monitoring. You want full control, self-hosting, and access to ALL 100+ data sources for free. Which Grafana edition should you choose?
A) Grafana Cloud Free — because "cloud" means everything is included at no cost
B) Grafana OSS (Open Source) — self-hosted, all data sources, all panel types, no usage limits, Apache 2.0 license
C) Grafana Enterprise — required for connecting to PostgreSQL and Loki data sources
D) Grafana Cloud Pro — the only edition that supports alerting and dashboard sharing
✅ Correct Answer: B

Grafana OSS (open-source) is the self-hosted, fully-featured edition. It includes ALL data sources, ALL panel types, alerting, dashboard sharing, and the complete plugin ecosystem — all under Apache 2.0 license with zero usage limits. Grafana Cloud adds managed hosting, but OSS has the exact same features.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Grafana OSS vs Cloud vs Enterprise — Feature Comparison
  ┌────────────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────────┐
  │   FEATURE      │  OSS     │  Cloud   │  Enterprise   │
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────────┤
  │ 100+ Data Srcs │    ✅    │    ✅    │     ✅        │
  │ 50+ Panels     │    ✅    │    ✅    │     ✅        │
  │ Alerting       │    ✅    │    ✅    │     ✅        │
  │ Dashboard JSON │    ✅    │    ✅    │     ✅        │
  │ Plugins        │    ✅    │    ✅    │     ✅        │
  │ Self-Hosted    │    ✅    │    ❌    │     ✅        │
  │ Managed Hosting│    ❌    │    ✅    │     ❌        │
  │ Free Tier      │  ALWAYS  │ 10K metrics│  ❌ (Paid)  │
  │ Usage Limits   │   NONE   │  Yes     │    NONE       │
  │ License        │ Apache 2.0│ SaaS   │ Commercial    │
  │ Best For       │Full Control│Quick Start│Enterprise  │
  │                │Self-Hosted │No Ops   │  Support     │
  └────────────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost on your 6-cluster lab, Grafana OSS is the perfect choice. You get full control, self-hosting on your kubeadm cluster, all data sources (Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL), and complete dashboard/alerting features — $0 cost. Grafana Cloud would be useful if you didn't want to manage the Grafana server yourself.

Fundamentals Q4 of 200
You open the "anihpj Production Overview" dashboard in your browser. Describe the exact path a query takes from your browser to the rendered panel.
A) Browser → Grafana Server → Data Source Plugin → External Data Store (e.g., Prometheus) → Data returns → Transformations → Panel Renderer → Browser displays
B) Browser reads data directly from Prometheus API → Browser renders the chart locally using JavaScript
C) Grafana Server stores all metrics in its internal database → Browser queries Grafana's DB → panel renders
D) Browser → Prometheus → Grafana → PostgreSQL → Panel renders in reverse order
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Grafana data flow is: Browser requests the dashboard → Grafana Server looks up the dashboard JSON → For each panel, Grafana proxies the query through the Data Source Plugin → The plugin calls the actual data store (Prometheus API, PostgreSQL, Loki, etc.) → Raw data returns as Data Frames → Optional Transformations reshape the data → Panel Plugin (React) renders SVG/Canvas → Browser displays the visualization.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Grafana Data Flow — Query to Visualization Pipeline
  STEP 1              STEP 2           STEP 3           STEP 4
  ┌────────┐      ┌─────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────┐
  │BROWSER │ ───▶ │ GRAFANA     │  │ DATA SOURCE   │  │ EXTERNAL │
  │        │      │ SERVER :3000│  │ PLUGIN (Go)   │  │ DATA     │
  │"Show   │      │             │  │               │  │ STORE    │
  │ anihpj │      │ GET /api/   │  │ translate     │  │          │
  │ dashbd"│      │ dashboards/ │──│ grafana query ─▶│Prometheus│
  │        │      │ uid/anihpj  │  │ → native API  │  │ :9090    │
  └────────┘      └─────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └────┬─────┘
       ▲                                                   │
       │         STEP 6              STEP 5               │
       │    ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐            │
       │    │ PANEL        │  │ TRANSFOR-    │◀───────────┘
       │    │ RENDERER     │◀─│ MATIONS      │  Data Frames
       │    │ (React/      │  │ (Optional)   │  returned
       └────│ SVG/Canvas)  │  │ Filter/Group │
            │ Rendered UI  │  │ /Join/Merge  │
            └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘

  KEY INSIGHT: Grafana is a PROXY, not a data store.
  It translates dashboard config → queries → forwards to
  data sources → transforms results → renders panels.
  At no point does Grafana permanently store your metrics.

For the anihpj/jobpost dashboard, when you see the "HTTP Request Rate" Time Series panel, the flow is: Browser → Grafana (port 3000) → Prometheus Data Source Plugin → Prometheus API (port 9090) → query: rate(django_http_requests_total[5m]) → Prometheus scans TSDB → returns time series data → Grafana's Time Series panel renders the line chart in your browser.

Fundamentals Q5 of 200
What is a Data Source in Grafana?
A) A file on disk where Grafana stores dashboard JSON definitions
B) A Grafana plugin that generates random sample data for testing dashboards
C) The SQLite database embedded inside Grafana that stores user accounts and preferences
D) A configured CONNECTION to an external data store (Prometheus, PostgreSQL, Loki, etc.) — the bridge between Grafana and where your data actually lives
✅ Correct Answer: D

A Data Source is Grafana's term for a configured connection to an external data store. It tells Grafana: "here is the URL, credentials, and type of database/API where your data lives." Grafana ships with built-in support for Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, MySQL, Elasticsearch, InfluxDB, CloudWatch, and 100+ more through plugins. A data source does NOT store data — it's just the connection configuration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Data Sources = Bridges, Not Warehouses
  🟠 GRAFANA (Control Tower)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Configuration → Data Sources → Connections       │
  │                                                   │
  │  ┌─────────────┐ ┌─────────────┐ ┌─────────────┐ │
  │  │Prometheus   │ │PostgreSQL   │ │Loki         │ │
  │  │DS Config:   │ │DS Config:   │ │DS Config:   │ │
  │  │URL: :9090   │ │Host: anihpj │ │URL: :3100   │ │
  │  │Type: Prom   │ │DB: jobpost  │ │Type: Loki   │ │
  │  │Access:Proxy │ │User: reader │ │MaxLines:1000│ │
  │  └──────┬──────┘ └──────┬──────┘ └──────┬──────┘ │
  └─────────┼───────────────┼───────────────┼────────┘
            │               │               │
     ┌──────▼──────┐ ┌──────▼──────┐ ┌──────▼──────┐
     │ Prometheus  │ │ PostgreSQL  │ │    Loki     │
     │  (Metrics)  │ │ (Business)  │ │   (Logs)   │
     │  ACTUAL     │ │  ACTUAL     │ │  ACTUAL    │
     │  DATA STORE │ │  DATA STORE │ │ DATA STORE │
     └─────────────┘ └─────────────┘ └─────────────┘

  The Data Source is the CONFIGURATION (bridge).
  The data store is where data ACTUALLY lives.
  Grafana stores the connection config, NOT the data.

For anihpj/jobpost, you configure three data sources in Grafana: Prometheus (for Django HTTP metrics and system metrics at port 9090), PostgreSQL (for business data like number of active jobs, user counts, application stats at port 5432), and Loki (for Django, PostgreSQL, and Nginx logs at port 3100). All three appear in the "anihpj Production Overview" dashboard simultaneously.

Fundamentals Q6 of 200
What is a Dashboard in Grafana?
A) A Grafana plugin that connects to external APIs for data retrieval
B) A collection of panels organized into rows — it's the main container for visualizing data from one or more data sources
C) A configuration file that tells Grafana how to authenticate users via LDAP
D) The backend database where Grafana stores all user preferences and settings
✅ Correct Answer: B

A Dashboard is the main organizational unit in Grafana. It's a collection of panels (visualizations) arranged in rows, each panel pulling data from one or more data sources. A dashboard answers a specific set of questions — for example, "Is anihpj healthy right now?" Think of a dashboard as a canvas where you arrange different visualizations (panels) to tell a complete story about your system.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Dashboard = Canvas of Panels
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  📊 DASHBOARD: "anihpj Production Overview"             │
  │                                                         │
  │  ═══════════ ROW 1: HTTP Overview ═══════════════════   │
  │  ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐    │
  │  │ Time Series  │ │    Stat      │ │    Stat      │    │
  │  │ Request Rate │ │  200 OK/min  │ │  5xx Errors  │    │
  │  │ (Prometheus) │ │ (Prometheus) │ │ (Prometheus) │    │
  │  └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘    │
  │                                                         │
  │  ═══════════ ROW 2: Business Metrics ═════════════════  │
  │  ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐                     │
  │  │    Stat      │ │    Table     │                     │
  │  │ Active Jobs  │ │ Recent Apps  │                     │
  │  │ (PostgreSQL) │ │ (PostgreSQL) │                     │
  │  └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘                     │
  │                                                         │
  │  ═══════════ ROW 3: Logs ════════════════════════════  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │               Logs Panel (Loki)                  │  │
  │  │  {app="anihpj-api"} |= "ERROR"                  │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the "anihpj Production Overview" dashboard has 3 rows: Row 1 shows HTTP metrics (request rate, errors) from Prometheus, Row 2 shows business metrics (active jobs, applications) from PostgreSQL, and Row 3 shows live error logs from Loki. One dashboard, three data sources, complete operational picture.

Fundamentals Q7 of 200
What is a Panel in Grafana, and which of the following is NOT a standard Grafana panel type?
A) A Panel is the smallest visualization unit in a dashboard; "Spreadsheet Editor" is NOT a real Grafana panel type
B) A Panel is a user account type that controls dashboard permissions; "Time Series" is not a real panel
C) A Panel is a data source configuration file; "Stat" is not a real panel type
D) A Panel is the Grafana server process; "Gauge" is not a real panel type
✅ Correct Answer: A

A Panel is the smallest visualization unit in a Grafana dashboard — it's one chart, one number, one table, or one gauge. Each panel queries a data source and renders the result visually. Grafana ships with 50+ panel types including Time Series (line/bar charts), Stat (big number + sparkline), Gauge (value in range), Bar Gauge, Table, Heatmap, Geomap, Pie Chart, State Timeline, Logs, Traces, Node Graph, Text/Markdown, and Canvas. There is NO "Spreadsheet Editor" panel.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Panel = One Visualization Tile in a Dashboard
  DASHBOARD ──── contains ──▶ ROWS ──── contain ──▶ PANELS
                                                      │
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  │
  │  Each PANEL has:
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  │  ① Data Source  →  Where to query          │
  │  │  ② Query        →  What to ask             │
  │  │  ③ Panel Type   →  How to visualize        │
  │  │  ④ Field Config →  Units, colors, thresholds│
  │  │  ⑤ Transform    →  Reshape data (optional)  │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  OFFICIAL PANEL TYPES (subset of 50+):
  📈 Time Series  📊 Histogram   📉 Bar Gauge
  🔢 Stat         🗺️ Geomap      🥧 Pie Chart
  🎚️ Gauge        📜 Logs        🔍 Traces
  📋 Table        🕸️ Node Graph  📝 Text
  🔥 Heatmap      ⏱️ State Timeline

For anihpj/jobpost, the dashboard uses: Time Series panels for HTTP request rates over time (Prometheus), Stat panels for "247 Active Jobs" and "12 Open Applications" (PostgreSQL), a Table panel for recent job applications, and a Logs panel for Django error logs (Loki). Each panel answers ONE specific question.

Fundamentals Q8 of 200
What is an Organization in Grafana, and why would anihpj/jobpost use one?
A) An Organization is a user group for email notifications — anihpj would use it to send alerts to the on-call team
B) An Organization is a dashboard folder that groups panels by team — anihpj uses it to separate web and API dashboards
C) An Organization is a complete tenant boundary — it isolates users, dashboards, data sources, and teams from other organizations. anihpj could have separate orgs for "Production" and "Staging" with different data sources and permissions
D) An Organization is the same thing as a Team — a group of users with shared dashboard permissions
✅ Correct Answer: C

An Organization (Org) in Grafana is the highest-level tenant boundary. Each Org has its OWN set of users, dashboards, data sources, teams, and settings — completely isolated from other Orgs. One Grafana instance can host multiple Orgs. This is different from Teams (groups within an Org) and Folders (dashboard groupings within an Org).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Organization vs Team vs Folder — Hierarchy
  🟠 GRAFANA INSTANCE (one server)
  │
  ├── 🏢 ORG: "anihpj Production"
  │   ├── 👥 Team: Platform-Engineering (Editor)
  │   │   └── 📁 Folder: Infrastructure Dashboards
  │   ├── 👥 Team: Developers (Viewer)
  │   │   └── 📁 Folder: Application Dashboards
  │   ├── 📡 Data Sources: prometheus-prod, postgresql-prod
  │   └── 👤 Users: alice@company.com (Admin), bob@c.com (Editor)
  │
  └── 🏢 ORG: "anihpj Staging"
      ├── 👥 Team: QA-Engineers (Editor)
      ├── 📡 Data Sources: prometheus-staging, postgresql-staging
      └── 👤 Users: alice@company.com (Viewer) ← SAME user, DIFFERENT role!

  KEY: Alice is Admin in Production Org but only Viewer in Staging.
  The two Orgs share NOTHING — separate dashboards, data sources, teams.

For anihpj/jobpost on your 6-cluster lab, you could create two Organizations: "Production" (connected to kubeadm cluster's Prometheus on port 9090) and "Staging" (connected to kind-cka-lab's Prometheus). The same user can switch between orgs from their profile menu. This keeps production dashboards completely isolated from staging.

1.2 Core Navigation & User InterfaceQ9-Q14

Fundamentals Q9 of 200
You just logged into Grafana at http://localhost:3000. The left sidebar shows several icons. What does EACH core sidebar icon represent?
A) All icons are the same — they're just shortcuts to your favorite dashboards
B) The sidebar only shows data source connection status — green dot = connected, red dot = broken
C) The sidebar is customizable widget area — you drag-and-drop panels onto it for quick access
D) Dashboards (browse/manage dashboards), Explore (ad-hoc queries), Alerting (alert rules + silences), Administration (users, orgs, plugins, settings), and your Profile (preferences, org switch, sign out)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Grafana sidebar is your main navigation hub with 5 core sections: Dashboards (📊 browse, search, create, manage folders and playlists), Explore (🔍 ad-hoc query playground for PromQL, LogQL, SQL), Alerting (🔔 alert rules, contact points, notification policies, silences), Administration (⚙️ users, teams, organizations, plugins, data sources, settings), and your Profile (👤 preferences, theme, organization switch, sign out).

Fundamentals Q10 of 200
What is the difference between "Starred" and "Recent" dashboards in Grafana?
A) Starred dashboards are manually bookmarked by YOU for quick access (click the ⭐ icon); Recent dashboards are automatically tracked by Grafana based on what you've viewed, and they appear in the "Recent" section without any action on your part
B) Starred dashboards are public dashboards anyone can see; Recent dashboards are private to your user account only
C) Starred and Recent are the same thing — Grafana automatically stars dashboards you visit frequently
D) Starred dashboards are created by Admins for the whole org; Recent dashboards are created by individual users
✅ Correct Answer: A

Starred dashboards are your personal bookmarks — you manually click the ⭐ icon on any dashboard to add it to your Starred list for quick access from the sidebar. Recent dashboards are automatically tracked by Grafana — they show the last ~20 dashboards you viewed, in reverse chronological order, without any user action. Both lists are per-user (not shared across the organization).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Starred vs Recent — Quick Reference
  ┌────────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │   FEATURE      │    STARRED       │       RECENT            │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ How added?     │ YOU click ⭐     │ Grafana auto-tracks     │
  │ Where shown?   │ Sidebar section  │ Sidebar section         │
  │ Shared?        │ Per-user (yours) │ Per-user (yours only)   │
  │ Max items?     │ Unlimited        │ ~20 most recent         │
  │ Persistent?    │ Yes (until      │ Rotates automatically   │
  │                │  you un-star)    │                          │
  │ Best for...    │ Dashboards you   │ Quickly returning to    │
  │                │ use EVERY DAY    │ dashboards you just saw │
  └────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  TIP: Star your "anihpj Production Overview" and "anihpj
  Business Metrics" dashboards — they'll always be 1 click away.
  Use Recent to jump back to dashboards you were viewing earlier.

For anihpj/jobpost monitoring, star "anihpj Production Overview" (your daily SRE dashboard) and "anihpj Business Metrics" (for stakeholder reviews). They'll always appear at the top of your sidebar. The Recent list will automatically show dashboards you visited during incident response — no need to search for them again.

1.3 Explore Mode & Ad-Hoc QueryingQ15-Q18

Fundamentals Q15 of 200
What is Explore mode in Grafana, and how is it fundamentally different from a Dashboard?
A) Explore mode is the same as a Dashboard but with a different color scheme — no functional difference
B) Explore is an interactive query playground for ad-hoc investigation — you write queries and see results immediately without building a dashboard. Dashboards are for saved, repeatable monitoring views
C) Explore mode is the Grafana mobile app that lets you view dashboards on your phone
D) Explore mode only works with Prometheus — other data sources must use Dashboards
✅ Correct Answer: B

Explore mode is Grafana's interactive query workbench. Unlike Dashboards (which are saved, repeatable views), Explore is for ad-hoc investigation: you type a query (PromQL, LogQL, SQL), see results instantly, refine, and iterate. It's like a REPL for your data sources. No dashboard JSON is saved — when you close Explore, your queries are gone (though recent queries are remembered).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Explore vs Dashboard — Different Tools for Different Jobs
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                   🔍 EXPLORE MODE                       │
  │  • Ad-hoc investigation ("Is anihpj down RIGHT NOW?")   │
  │  • Write a query → see results → refine → iterate       │
  │  • Not saved — temporary, exploratory                   │
  │  • Split view: compare two queries side-by-side         │
  │  • Live log tailing (Loki)                              │
  │  • Use during INCIDENTS to diagnose issues quickly      │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                   📊 DASHBOARD                          │
  │  • Saved, repeatable monitoring ("Is anihpj healthy?")  │
  │  • Multiple panels with pre-configured queries          │
  │  • Saved as JSON — version controlled, shared           │
  │  • Variables for environment switching (prod/staging)   │
  │  • Used for ONGOING visibility and alerting             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: During an incident, open Explore and run rate(django_http_errors_total{job="anihpj-api"}[5m]) to see the error spike immediately. Once you understand the issue, create a Dashboard panel from that query for long-term monitoring. Explore = investigation. Dashboard = monitoring.

Fundamentals Q16 of 200
Which of the following is NOT a feature of Grafana Explore mode?
A) Split view — compare results from two different queries or data sources side-by-side
B) Logs live tailing — stream real-time Loki logs as they arrive, like `tail -f`
C) Dashboard JSON export — Explore mode can save your query as a shareable dashboard template
D) Metrics drill-down — click on a time series label to filter or add it to the query
✅ Correct Answer: C

Explore mode's key features include: Split view (compare two queries side-by-side), Live log tailing (real-time Loki log streaming), Metrics drill-down (click labels to filter), Query history (recent queries remembered), and Log context (see surrounding log lines). However, Explore does NOT export dashboard JSON — to save a query from Explore, you manually copy it and paste into a new Dashboard panel.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Explore Features — What You CAN Do
  🔍 EXPLORE MODE CAPABILITIES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ Split View        Compare 2 queries side-by-side │
  │  ✅ Live Tail         Stream logs in real-time       │
  │  ✅ Drill-Down        Click label → filter/add       │
  │  ✅ Query History     Recall recent queries          │
  │  ✅ Log Context       See surrounding log lines      │
  │  ✅ Time Range Sync   Left & Right share time range  │
  │  ✅ Table/Graph       Toggle between views           │
  │  ✅ Data Links        Click through to dashboards    │
  │                                                      │
  │  ❌ Export JSON       NOT available (copy manually)  │
  │  ❌ Save Dashboard    NOT available (use Dashboard)  │
  │  ❌ Alerting          NOT available (use Dashboard)  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Use Split View to compare error rates (Prometheus, left) with error logs (Loki, right) for the same time range during an incident. Use Live Tail to watch Django logs in real-time as you deploy a fix. When you find the right query, copy it to a Dashboard panel for ongoing monitoring.

Fundamentals Q17 of 200
When should you use Explore mode instead of building a Dashboard?
A) During an active incident when you need to run ad-hoc queries to diagnose a problem quickly — Explore is faster than creating a dashboard panel
B) When you need a permanent view that your entire team will reference daily — Explore queries persist forever
C) When you want to set up alerting — Explore mode is the only place to create alert rules
D) When you're presenting to stakeholders — Explore mode has better visualization options than Dashboards
✅ Correct Answer: A

Use Explore for temporary, investigative work: incident response ("is the error rate spiking right now?"), query experimentation ("what does this PromQL function return?"), ad-hoc log analysis ("show me errors from the last 10 minutes"), and data exploration ("what labels does this metric have?"). Use Dashboards for everything permanent: ongoing monitoring, team-wide visibility, alerting, and presentations.

  "I need to look at some data..."
              │
     ┌────────┴────────┐
     ▼                 ▼
  One-time?         Ongoing?
     │                 │
     ▼                 ▼
  🔍 EXPLORE        📊 DASHBOARD
     │                 │
     ├─ Incident        ├─ Daily monitoring
     ├─ Debugging       ├─ Team visibility
     ├─ Experimenting   ├─ Alerting rules
     ├─ Exploring data  ├─ Stakeholder views
     └─ Log diving      └─ Wall displays (NOC)

For anihpj/jobpost: At 3 AM when PagerDuty wakes you up, open Explore immediately — run {app="anihpj-api"} |= "ERROR" in Loki, check Prometheus for metric anomalies. Diagnose in Explore. Fix the issue. THEN, after the incident, create or update a Dashboard panel to monitor whatever broke, so you catch it BEFORE the next 3 AM page.

Fundamentals Q18 of 200
You suspect anihpj-api is returning HTTP 500 errors. How do you use Explore mode to quickly confirm this?
A) Open Explore → select PostgreSQL data source → run a SQL query on the error_logs table

C) Explore mode can't access Prometheus — you must create a Dashboard panel first
D) Open Explore → it automatically detects anomalies and shows a red banner if errors are above 5%
✅ Correct Answer: B

Explore mode is the fastest way to check a hypothesis. For anihpj-api errors: Select the Prometheus data source, enter a PromQL query, and click "Run query" (or Shift+Enter). You'll see the error rate graph immediately. Then use Split View to open Loki on the right side and search for {app="anihpj-api"} |= "ERROR" to see the actual error messages at the same time points. This takes ~30 seconds — far faster than building a dashboard.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Explore Workflow: "Is anihpj-api returning errors?"
  STEP 1: Open Explore (🔍 sidebar icon or press 'x' key)
  STEP 2: Select Prometheus data source from dropdown
  STEP 3: Enter query:
          rate(django_http_errors_total{job="anihpj-api"}[5m])
  STEP 4: Shift+Enter → See graph immediately
          ┌────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  12 ┤                    ╭─╮       │
          │  8  ┤              ╭─────╯ ╰──     │
          │  4  ┤─────────────╯               │
          │  0  ┼────────────────────────────  │

          └────────────────────────────────────┘
          ✅ Confirmed: Error rate spiked at 14:25!

  STEP 5: Split View → Loki → {app="anihpj-api"} |= "ERROR"
          → See the ACTUAL error messages at 14:25
          → "django.db.utils.OperationalError: could not connect"
          → ROOT CAUSE: Database connection pool exhausted!

This exact workflow saves minutes during anihpj/jobpost incidents. Instead of guessing or building dashboards, you confirm the problem in Explore, identify the root cause, and fix it — all within Grafana.

1.4 Plugins — Extending GrafanaQ19-Q22

Fundamentals Q19 of 200
What are Grafana plugins, and why are they essential to Grafana's architecture?
A) Plugins are optional themes that change Grafana's appearance — purely cosmetic
B) Plugins are paid add-ons that unlock premium features like alerting and dashboard sharing
C) Plugins are extensions that add functionality to Grafana — they enable new data source connections (Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL), new panel types (Pie Chart, Geomap), and full applications (Synthetic Monitoring). Without plugins, Grafana can't connect to ANY data source
D) Plugins are command-line tools that run alongside the Grafana server for log processing
✅ Correct Answer: C

Plugins are Grafana's extensibility mechanism — they add capabilities that aren't in Grafana's core. There are three types: Data Source plugins (connect to Prometheus, PostgreSQL, Loki, Elasticsearch, CloudWatch, etc.), Panel plugins (Time Series, Stat, Pie Chart, Geomap, etc.), and App plugins (full applications like Synthetic Monitoring, Incident Response Management). Grafana's core is intentionally small — plugins provide the 100+ data source and 50+ panel options.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Grafana Plugin Ecosystem
  🟠 GRAFANA CORE (small, intentional)
  │
  ├── 📡 DATA SOURCE PLUGINS (100+)
  │   ├── Built-in: Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, MySQL
  │   │            Elasticsearch, InfluxDB, CloudWatch
  │   │            Azure Monitor, Google Cloud Monitoring
  │   ├── Community: GitHub, GitLab, Jira, ServiceNow
  │   │              Splunk, Datadog, New Relic, Zabbix
  │   └── Custom: Your own plugin for proprietary data
  │
  ├── 📊 PANEL PLUGINS (50+)
  │   ├── Built-in: Time Series, Stat, Gauge, Table
  │   │            Heatmap, Geomap, Pie Chart, Logs
  │   ├── Community: Plotly, D3, FlowCharting, Apache ECharts
  │   └── Custom: Your own visualization plugin
  │
  └── 📦 APP PLUGINS
      └── Synthetic Monitoring, IRM, OnCall, Incident

For anihpj/jobpost, you rely on THREE data source plugins: Prometheus plugin (connects to :9090 for metrics), PostgreSQL plugin (connects to :5432 for business data), and Loki plugin (connects to :3100 for logs). Without these plugins, Grafana would be an empty shell — plugins ARE the connectivity layer.

Fundamentals Q20 of 200
What are the three types of Grafana plugins, and how do they differ?
A) Free plugins, Paid plugins, and Enterprise plugins — differing by license cost
B) Frontend plugins, Backend plugins, and Database plugins — differing by where they run
C) Core plugins, Bundled plugins, and External plugins — differing by how they're shipped
D) Data Source plugins (connect to external data stores), Panel plugins (add visualization types), and App plugins (full applications with their own UI) — differing by WHAT they add to Grafana
✅ Correct Answer: D

Grafana has three plugin types: Data Source plugins add new data source types (e.g., "connect to MongoDB"), Panel plugins add new visualization types (e.g., "FlowChart panel"), and App plugins are full applications with their own UI, pages, and navigation inside Grafana (e.g., Synthetic Monitoring app). Each serves a completely different purpose.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Three Plugin Types — Comparison
  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │    TYPE      │   WHAT IT ADDS   │     EXAMPLES         │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Data Source  │ New data         │ Prometheus, Loki     │
  │ Plugin       │ connection type  │ PostgreSQL, MySQL    │
  │              │                  │ CloudWatch, InfluxDB │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Panel Plugin │ New visualization│ Pie Chart, Geomap    │
  │              │ type             │ State Timeline,      │
  │              │                  │ Node Graph, Canvas   │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ App Plugin   │ Full application │ Synthetic Monitoring │
  │              │ (UI + logic)     │ Incident Response    │
  │              │                  │ OnCall, Performance  │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: You use Data Source plugins (Prometheus, PostgreSQL, Loki), Panel plugins (Time Series, Stat, Table, Logs), and could add an App plugin like Synthetic Monitoring to proactively test your job board's API endpoints from multiple geographic locations.

Fundamentals Q21 of 200
How do you install a new Grafana plugin (e.g., the Pie Chart panel) on your self-hosted Grafana OSS instance?
A) Download a .deb package from grafana.com and run `dpkg -i plugin.deb` — all plugins are distributed as OS packages
B) Use grafana-cli plugins install <plugin-id> (e.g., grafana-cli plugins install grafana-piechart-panel), then restart Grafana. Or on Grafana Cloud, install from the plugin catalog with one click
C) Navigate to Administration → Plugins → type the plugin URL → Grafana downloads and installs it automatically without restart
D) Plugins are compiled into Grafana at build time — you must recompile Grafana from source to add a new plugin
✅ Correct Answer: B

On self-hosted Grafana OSS, install plugins using the grafana-cli tool: grafana-cli plugins install <plugin-id>. The plugin ID is found on the plugin's page at grafana.com/plugins. After installation, restart the Grafana server for the plugin to load. On Grafana Cloud, plugins are installed from the catalog with one click — no CLI needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Plugin Installation — Self-Hosted vs Cloud
  SELF-HOSTED (Grafana OSS):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

    set allow_loading_unsigned_plugins in grafana.ini

For anihpj/jobpost on your kubeadm cluster, install additional panel plugins like the Pie Chart panel using grafana-cli plugins install grafana-piechart-panel inside the Grafana pod or through Dockerfile customization. Restart the Grafana pod to load the new plugin.

Fundamentals Q22 of 200
What is the difference between signed and unsigned Grafana plugins, and why does it matter?
A) Signed plugins are cryptographically verified by Grafana Labs — their signature proves the plugin hasn't been tampered with. Unsigned plugins (typically community-developed) have no signature, and Grafana blocks them by default for security. You must explicitly allow them via grafana.ini
B) Signed plugins cost money (monthly subscription); unsigned plugins are free and open-source
C) Signed plugins work with Grafana Cloud; unsigned plugins only work with self-hosted Grafana OSS
D) The difference is purely cosmetic — signed plugins show a green checkmark in the UI, unsigned show a warning icon, but both work identically
✅ Correct Answer: A

Signed plugins have a digital signature from Grafana Labs that verifies authenticity — the code you download is exactly what the developer published, with no tampering. Unsigned plugins (mostly community-developed) lack this signature. By default, Grafana refuses to load unsigned plugins as a security measure. To use them, you must add the plugin ID to allow_loading_unsigned_plugins in grafana.ini.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Signed vs Unsigned — Security Implications
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SIGNED PLUGINS (✅ Trusted)                             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ grafana.com verifies: who built it? is it intact? │  │
  │  │ Grafana Labs signs with private key                │  │
  │  │ Grafana server verifies with public key            │  │
  │  │ → Guaranteed: plugin = exactly what dev published │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                          │
  │  UNSIGNED PLUGINS (⚠️ Warning)                           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ No signature = no tamper-proof guarantee           │  │
  │  │ Grafana blocks by default (won't load)             │  │
  │  │ To allow: grafana.ini →                            │  │
  │  │   [plugins]                                        │  │
  │  │   allow_loading_unsigned_plugins = plugin-id       │  │
  │  │ RISK: Modified plugin could exfiltrate your data   │  │
  │  │ MITIGATION: Audit plugin source code on GitHub     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost on your 6-cluster lab: The Prometheus, PostgreSQL, and Loki data source plugins are all SIGNED and built into Grafana — no security concern. If you ever install a community panel plugin, check its GitHub repository, review the source, and add it to the allow list only if you trust it.

Fundamentals Q23 of 200
What are Annotations in Grafana, and how would anihpj/jobpost use them?
A) Annotations are code comments in dashboard JSON — they're only visible when editing the raw JSON
B) Annotations are visual markers overlaid on graphs to mark events — deployments, incidents, releases, config changes. They help correlate "what changed" with "what happened to the metrics"
C) Annotations are alert notifications that appear as pop-up banners in Grafana
D) Annotations are a data source type that imports calendar events from Google Calendar
✅ Correct Answer: B

Annotations are event markers that appear as vertical lines or regions on your graphs. They answer the critical question: "What changed when the metrics changed?" For example, if error rates spike at 14:30, an annotation showing "Deployed v2.3.1 at 14:28" immediately suggests the deploy caused the spike.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Annotations — Connecting Events to Metrics
  📈 HTTP Error Rate (anihpj-api)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  15┤                    ╭─╮                          │
  │  10┤              ╭─────╯ ╰──                        │
  │   5┤─────────────╯                                   │
  │   0┼────────────────────────────────────────────     │
  │    14:00    14:15    14:30    14:45    15:00        │
  │              │         │         │                   │
  │        ┌─────┘    ┌────┘    ┌───┘                   │
  │        │ 🚀       │ 🔴     │ 🔧                    │
  │        │ Deploy   │Incident│ Rollback               │
  │        │ v2.3.1   │ #1423  │ to v2.3.0              │
  │        │ 14:12    │ 14:29  │ 14:52                  │
  │        └──────────┴────────┴────────────────────    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  STORY: Deploy v2.3.1 at 14:12 → errors spike at 14:29
  → Rollback at 14:52 → errors normalize. Clear correlation!

For anihpj/jobpost, create annotations for every deployment (GitLab CI/CD can push annotations via Grafana API), every incident (PagerDuty integration), and every configuration change. When investigating a production issue, annotations immediately show what changed around the time the problem started.

Fundamentals Q24 of 200
Where can Grafana annotations come from? Select the MOST complete answer.
A) Only from the Grafana UI — you manually click on the graph to add an annotation
B) Only from the Grafana REST API — external systems push annotations programmatically
C) From FOUR sources: Grafana UI (manual click on graph), Grafana HTTP API (programmatic from CI/CD), data source queries (auto-pull events from Prometheus alerts, Loki log lines, or PostgreSQL deployment tables), and tags for filtering
D) From Grafana alerts only — every time an alert fires, an annotation is automatically created
✅ Correct Answer: C

Annotations can come from four sources: Grafana UI (manual click to mark an event), HTTP API (POST /api/annotations from CI/CD pipelines or scripts), Data source queries (Grafana queries a data source for events — e.g., querying Loki for "DEPLOYED" log lines or querying PostgreSQL for deployment records), and Tags for organizing and filtering annotations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Annotation Sources — The Four Ways
  ANNOTATION SOURCES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① UI Manual      Click on graph → Add annotation   │
  │     Best for: Incident notes during investigation    │
  │                                                      │
  │  ② HTTP API       POST /api/annotations             │
  │     Best for: CI/CD pipelines, GitLab/GitHub         │
  │     Example: curl --user api_key: -X POST           │
  │       -d '{"text":"Deploy v2.3.1","tags":["deploy"]}'│
  │                                                      │
  │  ③ Data Source    Query Prometheus/PostgreSQL/Loki  │
  │     Best for: Auto-discovering events from your data │
  │     Example: Loki query {app="anihpj"} |= "DEPLOYED"│
  │                                                      │
  │  ④ Tags           Filter annotations by tag         │
  │     Best for: Showing only "deploy" or "incident"   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost automation: Configure your GitLab CI/CD pipeline to POST an annotation to Grafana after every successful deploy. Configure a Loki-based annotation query to automatically pull "DEPLOYED" log lines as annotations. Now every deploy appears on your graphs automatically — zero manual work.

Fundamentals Q25 of 200
What time range options does Grafana provide, and how do absolute and relative time ranges differ?
A) Grafana only shows the last 6 hours — time ranges are fixed for performance reasons
B) Absolute time (e.g., "Jan 1 2025 00:00 to Jan 1 2025 23:59") is the only option; relative time doesn't exist in Grafana
C) All time ranges are relative — you can only pick "Last 5 minutes" or "Last 1 hour" from a dropdown
D) Grafana supports both: Relative ranges ("Last 5 min", "Last 7 days") that always show data relative to NOW, and Absolute ranges (specific calendar dates/times) for historical analysis. Quick ranges are preset buttons for common durations
✅ Correct Answer: D

Grafana's time picker (top-right corner) supports Relative time ranges like "Last 5 minutes", "Last 1 hour", "Last 7 days", "Today so far", "Yesterday" — these always calculate relative to the current time. Absolute time ranges let you pick exact calendar dates/times, e.g., "January 15, 2025 09:00 to January 15, 2025 17:00" for post-mortem analysis. Quick ranges are one-click preset buttons for common durations.

Fundamentals Q28 of 200
You accidentally deleted a critical panel from the anihpj dashboard. How can you recover it using Grafana's version history?
A) Grafana doesn't have version history — once deleted, panels are gone forever
B) Check your browser's localStorage — Grafana stores dashboard history in the browser cache
C) Go to Dashboard Settings → Versions — Grafana automatically saves a version every time you save the dashboard. You can view any previous version, compare differences (diff view), and restore to any earlier version with one click
D) Check the Grafana server's recycle bin at /var/lib/grafana/trash/ — deleted panels are stored there for 30 days
✅ Correct Answer: C

Grafana automatically creates a new version every time you save a dashboard (Ctrl+S or the Save button). Dashboard Settings → Versions shows the full history with timestamps, version numbers, and who made the change. You can diff any two versions (JSON diff view showing exactly what changed) and restore any previous version with one click. This is a built-in feature — no plugins needed.

Fundamentals Q29 of 200
Your team is distributed across London (UTC+0), New York (UTC-5), and Tokyo (UTC+9). How does Grafana handle time zones?
A) Each user can set their OWN timezone in Preferences (browser local, UTC, or specific timezone). Dashboards can also have a dashboard-level timezone override. Data is always stored in UTC — timezone is a display preference only
B) Grafana uses UTC only — there is no way to change the timezone display, and all users see the same times
C) Grafana automatically detects the user's location via IP geolocation and adjusts the display timezone
D) Timezone is set globally in grafana.ini — all users on the same Grafana instance share the same timezone
✅ Correct Answer: A

Grafana handles time zones at two levels: User Preferences (each user sets their own timezone — Browser local, UTC, or a specific timezone like "America/New_York") and Dashboard Settings (a dashboard can override the timezone for ALL viewers — useful for NOC screens that should always show UTC). The underlying data from Prometheus/PostgreSQL is always in UTC — timezone only affects how timestamps are DISPLAYED.

🟡 Intermediate: Connecting Grafana to Your Data

This category is about connecting Grafana to WHERE your data lives and asking the right questions. Think of it like knowing which ship is carrying what cargo and how to radio them. Without data sources, Grafana is just an empty canvas — data sources make it come alive. For anihpj/jobpost, this means configuring Prometheus (metrics), PostgreSQL (business data), and Loki (logs) as data sources, and mastering PromQL, SQL, and LogQL to extract exactly the data you need.

Q31-Q70 40 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

2.1 Data Source FundamentalsQ31-Q38

Data Sources Q31 of 200
What is a Data Source in Grafana?
A) A file on disk where Grafana stores dashboard JSON definitions
B) A configured connection to an external data store — it's the bridge between Grafana and where your data actually lives (Prometheus, PostgreSQL, Loki, etc.)
C) A Grafana plugin that generates sample data for testing dashboards
D) The SQLite database embedded inside Grafana that stores user accounts
✅ Correct Answer: B

A Data Source is Grafana's term for a configured connection to an external data store. It tells Grafana: "here's the URL, credentials, and type of database/API where your data lives." Grafana ships with built-in support for Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, and 100+ more through plugins. A data source does NOT store data — it's just the connection bridge.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Data Source Configuration — The Bridge
  🟠 GRAFANA → Data Source Config → External Data Store
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Data Source: "anihpj-prometheus"                │
  │  Type: Prometheus                                │
  │  URL: http://prometheus.anihpj.svc:9090          │
  │  Access: Server (proxy through Grafana backend)  │
  │  Auth: None (internal cluster)                   │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────│
  │  [Save & Test] → "Data source is working ✅"     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, you configure THREE data sources in Grafana: Prometheus (:9090 for Django metrics), PostgreSQL (:5432 for job/user/application counts), and Loki (:3100 for Django + Nginx + PostgreSQL logs).

Data Sources Q32 of 200
How does a Grafana data source plugin actually query an external database?
A) The browser directly connects to the database — Grafana only provides the UI
B) Grafana imports all data into its internal database first, then queries locally
C) The data source plugin runs in Grafana's Go backend → it receives the query from the frontend → translates it to the data store's native protocol (HTTP API, SQL, etc.) → forwards the request → receives the response → converts to Data Frames → returns to the frontend
D) Grafana downloads a native database driver into the browser via WebAssembly
✅ Correct Answer: C

Data source plugins operate in Grafana's Go backend, acting as a proxy between the React frontend and the external data store. When you run a query: the frontend sends the query to Grafana's backend API → the backend routes it to the appropriate plugin → the plugin translates it (e.g., to a Prometheus HTTP API call or a PostgreSQL wire-protocol query) → the plugin calls the external store → converts the raw response into Grafana's internal Data Frame format → returns it to the frontend for rendering.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  DATA SOURCE PLUGIN FLOW:
  ┌──────────┐    ┌─────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐
  │ Frontend │───▶│ Backend API │───▶│ DS Plugin    │
  │ (React)  │    │ POST /api/  │    │ (Go code)    │
  │          │    │ ds/query    │    │              │
  └──────────┘    └─────────────┘    └──────┬───────┘
                                            │ translates to
                                            ▼
                                ┌───────────────────────┐
                                │ External Data Store   │
                                │ Prometheus API :9090  │
                                │ PostgreSQL :5432      │
                                │ Loki :3100            │
                                └───────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Prometheus plugin translates your Grafana query into the Prometheus HTTP API format (GET /api/v1/query_range), the PostgreSQL plugin sends a native PostgreSQL query over a TCP connection, and the Loki plugin calls Loki's HTTP API. All three plugins run inside Grafana's backend — the browser never talks directly to your databases.

Data Sources Q33 of 200
What is the difference between built-in and community data source plugins?
A) Built-in plugins ship WITH Grafana (Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, MySQL, Elasticsearch, InfluxDB, CloudWatch, etc.) and are maintained by Grafana Labs. Community plugins are developed by third parties and must be installed separately via grafana-cli or the plugin catalog
B) Built-in plugins are free; community plugins require a paid Grafana Enterprise license
C) There is no difference — all plugins are community-developed and must be installed manually
D) Built-in plugins only work with Grafana Cloud; community plugins only work with self-hosted Grafana
✅ Correct Answer: A

Built-in data sources come pre-installed with every Grafana instance and are maintained directly by Grafana Labs: Prometheus, Loki, PostgreSQL, MySQL, Elasticsearch, InfluxDB, CloudWatch, Azure Monitor, Google Cloud Monitoring, Graphite, OpenTSDB, Jaeger, Tempo, and more. Community plugins (GitHub, GitLab, Jira, Splunk, etc.) are built by third parties and must be installed separately.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  BUILT-IN DATA SOURCES (shipped with Grafana):
  • Prometheus     • Loki          • PostgreSQL
  • MySQL          • Elasticsearch • InfluxDB
  • CloudWatch     • Azure Monitor • Google Cloud
  • Graphite       • OpenTSDB      • Tempo
  • Jaeger         • Zipkin        • TestData DB

  POPULAR COMMUNITY DATA SOURCES (install separately):
  • GitHub         • GitLab        • Jira
  • ServiceNow     • Splunk        • Datadog
  • New Relic      • Zabbix        • Salesforce

For anihpj/jobpost, all three of your data sources (Prometheus, PostgreSQL, Loki) are BUILT-IN — no additional installation needed. They appear in the data source type dropdown immediately after installing Grafana.

Data Sources Q34 of 200
Who can query, edit, and administer a data source in Grafana?
A) Any logged-in user can query any data source — there are no data source permissions
B) Only Grafana Admins can query data sources; Editors and Viewers cannot use data sources at all
C) Data source permissions are inherited from dashboard permissions — you can't set them independently
D) Data sources have three permission levels: Query (users who can use this DS in dashboards/Explore), Edit (users who can modify the DS configuration), and Admin (users who can change permissions and delete the DS). These are set per user or per team
✅ Correct Answer: D

Data sources have granular permissions: Query (can use the data source in dashboards and Explore — typical for developers), Edit (can modify the data source configuration — typical for platform engineers), and Admin (can manage permissions and delete — typical for Grafana admins). These are set per user or team in the data source's Permissions tab.

Data Sources Q35 of 200
What is the difference between Server (Proxy) and Browser (Direct) access mode when configuring a data source?
A) Server mode is faster but less secure; Browser mode is slower but encrypted
B) Server/Proxy mode: Grafana's backend proxies ALL queries to the data source — the browser never contacts it directly (works even if the data source is on a private network). Browser/Direct mode: the browser directly calls the data source API (requires CORS and public network access)
C) Server mode works only with SQL databases; Browser mode works only with HTTP APIs
D) There is no difference — both modes function identically in all scenarios
✅ Correct Answer: B

Server (Proxy) mode (default, recommended): Grafana's Go backend proxies all queries to the data source. The browser sends the query to Grafana → Grafana forwards it to the data source → returns the result. This works even if the data source is on a private network (only Grafana needs network access). Browser (Direct) mode: The browser JavaScript calls the data source API directly. This requires the data source to be publicly accessible, support CORS headers, and the user's browser to have network access.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  SERVER (PROXY) MODE ✅ RECOMMENDED:
  Browser → Grafana:3000 → Prometheus:9090
  Prometheus only needs to be reachable from Grafana.

  BROWSER (DIRECT) MODE ⚠️ LIMITED USE:
  Browser → Prometheus:9090 (direct)
  Prometheus must be PUBLIC + support CORS.

For anihpj/jobpost: Always use Server/Proxy mode. Your Prometheus, PostgreSQL, and Loki run inside the Kubernetes cluster on private IPs — only Grafana (running in the same cluster) can reach them. Browser/Direct mode would fail because your laptop browser can't reach cluster-internal services.

Data Sources Q36 of 200
How can you authenticate Grafana to a data source that requires credentials?
A) Grafana cannot authenticate to data sources — they must be open and unauthenticated
B) Only username/password (Basic Auth) is supported
C) Multiple methods: Basic Auth (username/password), TLS Client Certificates (mutual TLS), API Keys (bearer token in header), OAuth 2.0 (delegated auth), AWS SigV4 (IAM-based for CloudWatch), and custom HTTP headers with secrets
D) Data sources must use the same authentication as Grafana itself — SSO is the only option
✅ Correct Answer: C

Grafana supports multiple authentication methods for data sources: Basic Auth (username + password), TLS Client Certificates (mutual TLS — Grafana presents its own cert to the data source), API Keys (sent as a custom header or bearer token), OAuth 2.0 (for cloud services), AWS SigV4 (IAM-based authentication for CloudWatch and AWS data sources), and Custom HTTP Headers (for proprietary auth schemes).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  AUTH METHODS PER DATA SOURCE:
  ┌────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Prometheus    │ None (internal), or Basic Auth   │
  │  PostgreSQL    │ Username + Password (SCRAM-SHA)  │
  │  Loki          │ None (internal), or Basic Auth    │
  │  Elasticsearch │ Basic Auth, API Key               │
  │  CloudWatch    │ AWS Access Key + Secret, or IAM  │
  │  InfluxDB      │ Token (Authorization header)     │
  └────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Prometheus and Loki on your internal cluster don't need auth (same network). PostgreSQL uses username/password authentication. If you were using AWS CloudWatch for monitoring, you'd configure AWS SigV4 with IAM credentials or use the EC2 instance role.

Data Sources Q37 of 200
What are custom HTTP headers used for in Grafana data source configuration?
A) They add extra HTTP headers to every request Grafana makes to that data source — useful for multi-tenant systems (X-Scope-OrgID for Loki/Mimir), custom authentication tokens, or routing headers for API gateways
B) They modify the response headers that Grafana sends back to the browser — for CORS configuration
C) They are for debugging only — headers are logged but not sent with requests
D) They control caching behavior — how long Grafana caches query results from that data source
✅ Correct Answer: A

Custom HTTP Headers are added to every outgoing request Grafana makes to that specific data source. Common uses: X-Scope-OrgID (multi-tenant Loki/Mimir/Cortex — routes queries to the correct tenant), Authorization (bearer tokens), X-API-Key (proprietary API authentication), and custom routing headers for API gateways. They are configured in the data source settings under "Custom HTTP Headers."

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  Custom HTTP Headers (Loki data source example):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Header: X-Scope-OrgID                           │
  │  Value:  anihpj-production                       │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────│
  │  Every Loki query now includes:                  │
  │  GET /loki/api/v1/query_range                    │
  │  X-Scope-OrgID: anihpj-production               │
  │  → Loki returns ONLY "anihpj-production" logs   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost multi-tenancy: If you use a shared Loki instance with multiple teams, set X-Scope-OrgID to "anihpj" to ensure queries only return anihpj's logs and not other teams' data.

Data Sources Q38 of 200
What does the "Save & Test" button do when configuring a data source?
A) It saves the configuration and runs a benchmark query to measure the data source's performance
B) It saves the configuration AND validates: network connectivity (can Grafana reach the URL?), authentication (are credentials valid?), and protocol compatibility (can Grafana successfully query this data source type?). If all checks pass, you see "Data source is working ✅"
C) It exports the data source configuration as a JSON file for backup purposes
D) It tests the data source in a temporary Explore session without saving the configuration
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Save & Test button performs three validations: Network connectivity (can Grafana's backend reach the URL? — tests for DNS resolution + TCP connection), Authentication (are the provided credentials accepted?), and Protocol compatibility (can Grafana successfully execute a basic query against this data source type? — e.g., Prometheus: a simple /api/v1/query, PostgreSQL: SELECT 1). If all pass, you see the green "Data source is working" banner.

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │     Can Grafana query this data source type?   │

  └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When adding your Prometheus data source, Save & Test verifies Grafana can reach http://prometheus.anihpj.svc:9090 inside the Kubernetes cluster. If it fails, check: is the Prometheus pod Running? Is the Service DNS resolving correctly? Is the port correct?

2.2 Prometheus Data Source & PromQLQ39-Q48

Data Sources Q39 of 200
What is Prometheus, and why is it the most common Grafana data source?
A) Prometheus is a logging system similar to Loki — it stores application log files
B) Prometheus is a visualization tool that competes with Grafana — they serve the same purpose
C) Prometheus is a relational database optimized for time-series data — you query it with SQL
D) Prometheus is an open-source time-series database (TSDB) and monitoring system that scrapes /metrics endpoints, stores numeric time-series data efficiently, and provides a powerful query language (PromQL). It's the most common Grafana data source because it's the CNCF standard for metrics
✅ Correct Answer: D

Prometheus is a time-series database designed for metrics. It scrapes (pulls) data from /metrics endpoints exposed by applications, stores it in a highly efficient TSDB, and provides the PromQL query language. It's the de facto standard for Kubernetes monitoring and a CNCF graduated project. Grafana + Prometheus is the most popular monitoring stack because Prometheus stores the data and Grafana visualizes it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  PROMETHEUS ARCHITECTURE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-api (Django)                             │
  │  exposes /metrics endpoint (Prometheus client)   │
  │  → django_http_requests_total{method="GET"} 5230 │
  │  → django_http_errors_total{status="500"} 12    │
  └───────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘
                      │ scrape every 15s
                      ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Prometheus Server (:9090)                        │
  │  • TSDB (stores time-series data on disk)        │
  │  • PromQL query engine                           │
  │  • Alerting rules evaluation                     │
  └───────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘
                      │ PromQL query
                      ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🟠 Grafana (:3000)                              │
  │  Data Source → Prometheus → Dashboard panels     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Django exposes /metrics using the django-prometheus package. Prometheus scrapes it every 15 seconds. Grafana queries Prometheus with PromQL to create dashboards showing request rates, error rates, latency percentiles, and system metrics.

Data Sources Q40 of 200
How do you connect Grafana to Prometheus running on your Kubernetes cluster?
A) Add a Prometheus data source → set URL to http://prometheus-server.<namespace>.svc:9090 → set Access to Server (proxy) → Save & Test. Grafana will proxy all PromQL queries through its backend to Prometheus
B) Install the Prometheus plugin from grafana-cli — Prometheus support is not built-in
