Data Sources Q41 of 200
What is the difference between an Instant Vector and a Range Vector in PromQL?
A) Instant Vectors are faster but less accurate; Range Vectors are slower but more precise — they're functionally equivalent
B) Instant Vectors work with gauges; Range Vectors work with counters — the data type determines which you use
C) An Instant Vector returns a single value per series at ONE point in time (e.g., current CPU usage). A Range Vector returns multiple values per series over a TIME RANGE (e.g., CPU usage over the last 5 minutes), enabling rate(), increase(), and avg_over_time() calculations
D) Instant Vectors are for dashboards; Range Vectors are for alerting rules only
✅ Correct Answer: C

In PromQL: An Instant Vector (e.g., django_http_requests_total) returns the current value of each matching time series at the query evaluation moment — a single number per series. A Range Vector (e.g., django_http_requests_total[5m]) returns ALL samples from the last 5 minutes for each series — a list of (timestamp, value) pairs. Range vectors are used with functions like rate(), increase(), avg_over_time() that need multiple data points to calculate.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  INSTANT VECTOR: http_requests_total
  → {job="api"} = 5230  (one value at query time)

  RANGE VECTOR: http_requests_total[5m]
  → {job="api"} = [(t1,5210), (t2,5215), (t3,5220), ...]
  (all values from last 5 minutes)

  TOGETHER: rate(http_requests_total[5m])
  → range vector feeds into rate() → instant vector out
  → {job="api"} = 1.5 requests/second

For anihpj/jobpost: django_http_requests_total (instant vector) shows the current total request count. rate(django_http_requests_total[5m]) (range vector → rate → instant vector) shows the per-second request rate over the last 5 minutes — much more useful for dashboards.

Data Sources Q42 of 200
Which PromQL function would you use to calculate the per-second rate of HTTP requests from a counter metric?
A) sum(http_requests_total) — this adds up all request counts across all labels
B) rate(http_requests_total[5m]) — rate() calculates the per-second average rate of increase over the specified time window. For HTTP request rates, 5m is a common window that smooths out short spikes
C) count(http_requests_total) — this counts how many time series match, not the values
D) http_requests_total / 300 — manually dividing by seconds is not how PromQL works
✅ Correct Answer: B

The rate() function calculates the per-second average rate of increase for a counter metric over a specified time window. It takes a range vector and returns an instant vector. Key PromQL functions: rate() — per-second average rate (smooths), irate() — per-second instant rate (uses only last 2 samples, more responsive to spikes), increase() — total increase over the window, sum() — aggregate across dimensions, avg() — average across dimensions, histogram_quantile() — calculate percentile from histogram buckets.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  COMMON PromQL FUNCTIONS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  rate(counter[5m])       → per-second avg rate   │
  │  irate(counter[5m])      → per-second instant    │
  │  increase(counter[1h])   → total increase in 1h  │
  │  sum(rate(counter[5m]))  → sum across all labels │
  │  avg(metric) by (pod)    → average per pod       │
  │  topk(5, metric)         → top 5 highest values  │
  │  histogram_quantile(0.95, hist) → P95 latency   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost dashboard: Use rate(django_http_requests_total{job="anihpj-api"}[5m]) for the request rate panel. Use sum(rate(django_http_errors_total[5m])) by (status) to see error rates broken down by HTTP status code. Use histogram_quantile(0.95, rate(django_http_latency_seconds_bucket[5m])) for the P95 latency panel.

Data Sources Q43 of 200
What is the critical difference between rate() and irate() in PromQL?
A) rate() works with counters; irate() works with gauges — they're for different metric types
B) rate() is deprecated in Prometheus 3.0; irate() is the modern replacement
C) There is no functional difference — irate() is just an alias for rate()
D) rate() calculates the average rate across ALL samples in the window (smooths graphs, better for alerting). irate() uses ONLY the last two samples (more responsive to sudden spikes, better for visualizing rapid changes, but can be jumpy)
✅ Correct Answer: D

rate() averages all samples in the window → smoother graphs, better for alerting (less noisy). irate() uses only the last two samples → more responsive to sudden spikes, better for visualizing rapid changes, but can be "jumpy." Use rate() for alerting (you want sustained issues, not momentary spikes). Use irate() for dashboards showing quick-response metrics.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  rate() vs irate() — Visual Comparison:
  
  rate(counter[5m]):   ───────────╱────── (smooth)
  irate(counter[5m]):  ───╲  ╱──╲──╱─ (responsive)
                       spike     spike

  WHEN TO USE:
  • rate():  Alerting (don't wake SRE for a 1-second blip)
  • irate(): Diagnostic dashboards (see every spike)

For anihpj/jobpost: Use rate(django_http_errors_total[5m]) > 5 for your alert rule — it prevents false alarms from momentary error spikes. Use irate(django_http_requests_total[5m]) on your diagnostic dashboard to see traffic bursts instantly.

Data Sources Q44 of 200
How do you filter Prometheus metrics to show only HTTP requests from the anihpj-api job with the GET method?
A) Use label selectors: django_http_requests_total{job="anihpj-api", method="GET"} — labels are Prometheus's key=value metadata attached to every time series
B) Use a SQL WHERE clause: SELECT * FROM metrics WHERE job='anihpj-api'
C) Filter in the Grafana panel settings after querying all data — PromQL doesn't support filtering
D) Use a regular expression in the metric name: django_http_requests_total_anihpj_api_get
✅ Correct Answer: A

Prometheus metrics are identified by labels — key=value pairs attached to every time series. You filter using {label="value"} syntax: metric{job="anihpj-api", method="GET"}. Operators include: = (exact match), != (not equal), =~ (regex match), and !~ (regex not match). Multiple selectors are ANDed together.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  LABEL SELECTOR OPERATORS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  =     exact match    {job="api"}                 │
  │  !=    not equal      {status!="200"}             │
  │  =~    regex match    {method=~"GET|POST"}        │
  │  !~    regex not match {path!~"/health.*"}        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: rate(django_http_requests_total{job="anihpj-api"}[5m]) shows the API request rate. rate(django_http_requests_total{job="anihpj-api", method=~"POST|PUT|DELETE"}[5m]) shows only mutating requests. django_http_errors_total{status=~"5.."} shows only 5xx server errors using regex.

Data Sources Q45 of 200
How do you aggregate Prometheus metrics — for example, showing total error rate across all API pods?
A) PromQL cannot aggregate — each pod's metrics must be shown separately on the graph
B) Use aggregation operators: sum(rate(django_http_errors_total[5m])) adds all pods together. Use by (label) to group: sum by (status) (...) groups by HTTP status code. Use without (label) to aggregate across specified labels
C) Use the Grafana Transform function — PromQL returns raw data, and Grafana aggregates it in the panel settings
D) Aggregation is done by Prometheus automatically — you don't need explicit operators
✅ Correct Answer: B

PromQL aggregation operators: sum() (add all), avg() (average), min()/max() (extremes), count() (number of series), topk(N, ...) (top N values), bottomk(N, ...) (bottom N), stddev()/stdvar() (statistical). Use by (label) to group/break down results, and without (label) to aggregate across everything except specified labels.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  AGGREGATION EXAMPLES:
  sum(rate(errors[5m]))              → total errors/s
  sum by (pod) (rate(errors[5m]))    → errors/s per pod
  avg by (endpoint) (rate(latency[5m])) → avg latency per endpoint
  topk(3, rate(errors[5m]))          → top 3 highest error rates

For anihpj/jobpost: sum(rate(django_http_errors_total[5m])) gives the total error rate across all API pods for alerting. sum by (pod) (rate(django_http_errors_total[5m])) breaks it down per pod to identify which specific pod is misbehaving.

Data Sources Q46 of 200
What are $__interval and $__range in Grafana's Prometheus query editor?
A) They're Grafana dashboard variables that the user fills in manually from a dropdown
B) $__interval is the data source refresh rate; $__range is the dashboard time range in seconds — both are fixed at configuration time
C) They're Grafana's built-in "magic variables": $__interval dynamically calculates the optimal step based on the dashboard time range and panel width (≈ time_range / max_data_points). $__range equals the dashboard's current time range duration. They auto-adjust as you zoom in/out
D) $__interval is for Prometheus only; $__range is for Loki only — they can't be used together
✅ Correct Answer: C

$__interval and $__range are Grafana's auto-calculating variables. $__interval = optimal scrape/step interval based on the dashboard's time range divided by the maximum data points (configured per panel). As the user zooms in (Last 5 min), $__interval shrinks; as they zoom out (Last 30 days), it grows. $__range = the current dashboard time range as a PromQL duration. These make dashboards zoom-responsive without manual configuration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  $__interval AUTO-CALCULATION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Dashboard: Last 1 hour, 1100px wide panel       │
  │  → $__interval = 1h ÷ 1100 ≈ 15s                 │
  │                                                  │
  │  Dashboard: Last 30 days, 1100px wide panel      │
  │  → $__interval = 30d ÷ 1100 ≈ 30m                │
  │                                                  │
  │  Use in queries:                                 │
  │  rate(metric[$__interval])                       │
  │  → Auto-adjusts as user zooms in/out!            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Always use rate(django_http_requests_total[$__interval]) instead of hardcoded [5m]. This ensures your dashboard performs well whether the user is viewing the last 5 minutes (incident mode) or last 30 days (trend analysis).

Grafana's Prometheus query editor has two modes. What are they and when should you use each?
A) Light mode and Dark mode — purely visual preference
B) Simple mode and Advanced mode — Simple only supports rate(), Advanced supports all functions
C) Dashboard mode and Explore mode — they're the same editor in different contexts
D) Code mode (free-text PromQL editor with syntax highlighting and autocomplete — for experienced users) and Builder mode (visual query builder with dropdowns for metric, labels, and functions — for beginners or quick queries). You can switch between them freely
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Prometheus query editor has Code mode (type PromQL directly with syntax highlighting, autocomplete, and function documentation) and Builder mode (visual interface: pick the metric from a dropdown, add label filters, choose aggregation functions — without typing PromQL). Builder mode is great for beginners or when you don't remember exact function names. Code mode is faster for experienced users. You can toggle between them and your query is translated both ways.

For anihpj/jobpost: Use Builder mode when onboarding new team members — they can build PromQL queries without memorizing syntax. Switch to Code mode for complex queries with multiple functions and aggregations.

Data Sources Q48 of 200
What are Exemplars in Grafana + Prometheus, and how do they connect metrics to traces?
A) Exemplars are example dashboard JSON files that ship with Grafana for learning purposes
B) Exemplars are traceID references attached to specific Prometheus data points. When you see a latency spike on a graph, you can click the exemplar to jump directly to the exact Tempo/Jaeger trace that caused it — connecting metrics to traces in one click
C) Exemplars are Prometheus alert templates — pre-written alert rules you can import
D) Exemplars are Grafana dashboard annotations auto-generated from Prometheus alerts

In Grafana, this appears as a small dot on your graph — click it to jump directly to that specific trace in Tempo/Jaeger. It closes the loop: "This P99 latency spike → this exact slow request trace."

  METRICS + TRACES = EXEMPLARS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │                                                  │
  │  In Grafana:                                     │
  │  📈 Latency graph shows spike at 14:30           │
  │  ● Click exemplar dot → opens Tempo trace        │
  │  → See EXACT slow DB query that caused it        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost with OpenTelemetry: Configure Django's OTel SDK to emit both metrics and traces. When latency spikes on your dashboard, click the exemplar to see the exact database query, external API call, or code path that caused the slowdown.

2.3 SQL Data Sources — PostgreSQL & MySQLQ49-Q54

Data Sources Q49 of 200
What kind of data can you query from PostgreSQL in Grafana for anihpj/jobpost?
A) Business metrics that Prometheus doesn't have — number of active jobs, users registered today, applications per job category, revenue per skill type. These are stored in PostgreSQL tables and queried directly with SQL
B) Only system metrics like CPU and memory usage — PostgreSQL is a monitoring database
C) PostgreSQL cannot be used as a Grafana data source — only time-series databases like Prometheus are supported
D) Only log data — PostgreSQL stores application logs the same way Loki does
✅ Correct Answer: A

PostgreSQL brings business context to your Grafana dashboards — data that Prometheus doesn't have. While Prometheus has technical metrics (request rates, error counts, latency), PostgreSQL has business metrics: "How many jobs were posted today?", "What's the application-to-hire conversion rate?", "Which skills are in highest demand?" By adding PostgreSQL as a data source, you combine technical AND business observability in one dashboard.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  PROMETHEUS (Technical):         POSTGRESQL (Business):
  ┌──────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ HTTP requests/sec     │        │ Active job listings  │
  │ Error rate %          │        │ Users registered     │
  │ P95 latency           │        │ Applications today   │
  │ Memory usage          │        │ Revenue per category │
  │ Pod restarts          │        │ Top skills in demand │
  └──────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────┘

  ONE DASHBOARD: Both sides = Complete operational picture

For anihpj/jobpost: Query PostgreSQL directly from Grafana panels — SELECT COUNT(*) FROM jobpost WHERE status='active' shows live job count. SELECT skill_name, COUNT(*) FROM applications GROUP BY skill_name ORDER BY 2 DESC LIMIT 10 shows the top 10 in-demand skills.

Data Sources Q50 of 200
How do you connect Grafana to your anihpj/jobpost PostgreSQL database?
A) PostgreSQL is not supported as a Grafana data source — you must export data to Prometheus first
B) Install the PostgreSQL plugin from grafana-cli — it's a community plugin, not built-in
C) PostgreSQL is a built-in data source. Configuration: Host (anihpj-db.anihpj.svc:5432), Database (jobpost), User (grafana_reader), Password (***), TLS/SSL Mode (disable for internal, require for external). Use a read-only database user with SELECT-only permissions for security
D) Use a connection string URL like postgresql://user:pass@host/db in the URL field — same format as Prometheus
✅ Correct Answer: C

PostgreSQL is a built-in Grafana data source — no plugin installation needed. Configure it with: Host (host:port), Database name, User and Password (credentials), and TLS/SSL Mode (disable/require/verify-ca/verify-full). Best practice: create a dedicated read-only PostgreSQL user (e.g., grafana_reader) with SELECT-only permissions on the specific tables Grafana needs. Never use the application's database user or a superuser account.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  POSTGRESQL DATA SOURCE CONFIG:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Name: anihpj-postgresql                         │
  │  Host: anihpj-db.anihpj.svc:5432                 │
  │  Database: jobpost                               │
  │  User: grafana_reader  (READ-ONLY!)              │
  │  Password: **********                            │
  │  TLS/SSL Mode: disable (internal K8s network)    │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────│
  │  [Save & Test] → "✅ Database Connection OK"     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CREATE READ-ONLY USER (run in PostgreSQL):
  CREATE USER grafana_reader WITH PASSWORD 'xxx';
  GRANT CONNECT ON DATABASE jobpost TO grafana_reader;
  GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA public TO grafana_reader;
  GRANT SELECT ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA public TO grafana_reader;

For anihpj/jobpost: Create the grafana_reader user with SELECT-only permissions on the jobpost, user, and application tables. This follows the principle of least privilege — even if the Grafana data source config is exposed, the attacker can only SELECT data, not modify or delete it.

2.3 SQL Data Sources — Query Editor & VariablesQ51-Q54

Data Sources Q51 of 200
In Grafana's PostgreSQL query editor, what is the difference between Table format and Time Series format?
A) Table format is for dashboards; Time Series format is for Explore mode only — they can't be mixed
B) Table format returns rows and columns as-is (for Stat, Table, and Gauge panels). Time Series format expects a time column + numeric columns (for Time Series and Graph panels) — Grafana uses the time column for the X-axis
C) Table format is deprecated; all queries should use Time Series format
D) Table format works only with PostgreSQL; Time Series format works only with MySQL
✅ Correct Answer: B

The SQL query editor's Format dropdown tells Grafana how to interpret the query results. Table format: returns columns as-is — use for Stat panels (single values like "247 active jobs"), Table panels, and Gauge panels. Time Series format: expects a time column (timestamp) + one or more numeric columns — use for Time Series graphs, where the time column maps to the X-axis.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  TABLE FORMAT (returns raw rows):
  SELECT status, COUNT(*) FROM jobpost GROUP BY status
  ┌──────────┬───────┐
  │ status   │ count │
  ├──────────┼───────┤
  │ active   │  247  │  → Stat panel: "247 Active Jobs"
  │ closed   │ 1523  │
  │ draft    │   42  │
  └──────────┴───────┘

  TIME SERIES FORMAT (requires time column):
  SELECT date_trunc('hour', created_at) AS time,
         COUNT(*) FROM jobpost GROUP BY 1
  ┌─────────────────────┬───────┐
  │ time                │ count │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ 2025-01-15 09:00    │   12  │  → Time Series panel:
  │ 2025-01-15 10:00    │   18  │     jobs posted over time
  │ 2025-01-15 11:00    │   25  │
  └─────────────────────┴───────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Use Table format for "Active Jobs" (Stat panel showing one number) and "Applications by Skill" (Bar Gauge). Use Time Series format for "Jobs Posted per Hour" (Time Series graph showing trends).

Data Sources Q52 of 200
What are Grafana SQL macros like $__timeFilter() and when must you use them?
A) Macros are optional shortcuts — you can write raw SQL dates instead without any issue
B) Macros are only needed for MySQL — PostgreSQL has native time-range support
C) Macros like $__timeFilter(column) automatically add the dashboard's time range as a WHERE clause. Without them, your SQL query ignores the time picker and returns ALL data regardless of the selected time range
D) Macros are Grafana dashboard variables — they create dropdowns for users to pick time ranges
✅ Correct Answer: C

$__timeFilter(column) is the most important SQL macro — it auto-generates a WHERE clause based on the dashboard's current time range. Example: $__timeFilter(created_at) becomes created_at BETWEEN '2025-01-01' AND '2025-01-15'. Other macros: $__timeGroup(column, interval) for GROUP BY time buckets, $__unixEpochFilter(column) for Unix timestamp columns, $__timeFrom() / $__timeTo() for individual time boundaries.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  WITHOUT $__timeFilter (WRONG):
  SELECT COUNT(*) FROM jobpost
  → Returns ALL jobs ever, ignoring time picker → WRONG!

  WITH $__timeFilter (CORRECT):
  SELECT COUNT(*) FROM jobpost
  WHERE $__timeFilter(created_at)
  → Grafana auto-adds: WHERE created_at BETWEEN '...' AND '...'
  → Respects the dashboard time picker → CORRECT!

For anihpj/jobpost dashboard: Always include WHERE $__timeFilter(created_at) in your PostgreSQL queries. Without it, your "Jobs Posted Today" Stat panel would show all jobs ever posted, not just today's. The macro ensures your dashboard responds to the time picker.

Data Sources Q53 of 200
How do you create a dropdown variable in Grafana that lists all job categories from the PostgreSQL database?
A) You can't — variables only work with Prometheus, not SQL databases
B) Create a Text box variable and manually type the category names separated by commas
C) Use the grafana-cli to import a CSV file of categories into Grafana's variable store
D) Dashboard Settings → Variables → New → Type: Query → Data source: PostgreSQL → Query: SELECT category_name FROM job_categories ORDER BY 1 → This creates a dynamic dropdown that automatically updates when categories change in the database
✅ Correct Answer: D

Query variables from SQL data sources dynamically populate dropdowns from database queries. Every time the dashboard loads (or on time range change), Grafana runs the query and updates the dropdown options. This means your variable options are always in sync with the actual database — no manual maintenance needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  VARIABLE CONFIGURATION:
  Name: category
  Type: Query
  Data source: PostgreSQL (anihpj-postgresql)
  Query: SELECT category_name AS __text,
                category_id AS __value
         FROM job_categories ORDER BY 1

  RESULT: Dropdown in dashboard
  ┌─────────────────────┐
  │ Category: [All ▼]   │
  │  ├─ Engineering     │
  │  ├─ Design          │
  │  ├─ Marketing       │
  │  └─ Sales           │
  └─────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Create a $category variable from the job_categories table. Panels use it as SELECT * FROM jobpost WHERE category_id = $category. Users can filter the entire dashboard by job category — one variable, dashboard-wide filtering.

Data Sources Q54 of 200
How do you enable multi-value selection and the "All" option for a Grafana query variable?
A) Multi-value and "All" are always enabled — Grafana variables support them by default
B) In the variable settings: check "Multi-value" (allows selecting multiple values) and check "Include All option" (adds an "All" entry). Then use ${variable:csv} in your SQL query to pass comma-separated values
C) Multi-value requires Grafana Enterprise — it's not available in OSS
D) Create separate variables for each value — Grafana doesn't support multi-value selection natively
✅ Correct Answer: B

Enable Multi-value and Include All option in the variable settings. When multi-value is enabled, users can select multiple dropdown values. The "All" option selects everything. In SQL queries, use ${category:csv} to pass values as a comma-separated list. For PostgreSQL, pair this with WHERE category_id IN (${category:csv}).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  VARIABLE SYNTAX FORMATS:
  $variable              → single value:  "Engineering"
  ${variable}            → same as above
  ${variable:csv}        → csv:  "Engineering,Design"
  ${variable:json}       → json:  ["Engineering","Design"]
  ${variable:doublequote} → "Engineering","Design"
  ${variable:pipe}       → Engineering|Design
  ${variable:sqlstring}  → 'Engineering','Design'

For anihpj/jobpost: Enable multi-value for the $category variable. Query: WHERE category_id IN (${category:csv}). Users can now compare "Engineering" AND "Design" in the same dashboard view — perfect for cross-category analysis.

2.4 Loki Data Source & LogQLQ55-Q60

Data Sources Q55 of 200
What is Loki, and how does it differ from Prometheus?
A) Loki is a time-series database for metrics — it replaces Prometheus entirely
B) Loki is Grafana's built-in database for storing dashboard JSON and user preferences
C) Loki is a log aggregation system — "Prometheus for logs." It indexes metadata (labels) but NOT the log content itself, making it far more cost-effective than Elasticsearch. Prometheus stores metrics (numbers); Loki stores logs (text)
D) Loki is the old name for Grafana Cloud — they rebranded it in 2024
✅ Correct Answer: C

Loki is Grafana Labs' log aggregation system inspired by Prometheus. Key design principle: Loki indexes ONLY metadata (labels like app="anihpj-api") but NOT the full log text — it stores logs in compressed chunks on object storage (S3, GCS, MinIO). This makes Loki orders of magnitude cheaper than Elasticsearch for log storage. Query with LogQL: {app="anihpj-api"} |= "ERROR".

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  PROMETHEUS (Metrics)              LOKI (Logs)
  ┌──────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ Stores: Numbers       │        │ Stores: Text logs     │
  │ Query: PromQL         │        │ Query: LogQL          │
  │ Indexes: Labels       │        │ Indexes: Labels only  │
  │ Scrapes: /metrics     │        │ Pushes: Promtail      │
  │ DB: TSDB on disk      │        │ DB: Object storage    │
  └──────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Prometheus stores request counts and latencies (numbers). Loki stores the actual Django error logs, PostgreSQL slow queries, and Nginx access logs (text). Together in Grafana, you see the metrics spike AND the exact log messages that caused it.

Data Sources Q56 of 200
How do you connect Grafana to Loki?
A) Loki is a built-in data source. Add data source → Loki → URL: http://loki.monitoring:3100 → Save & Test. Configure optional settings: Maximum lines (limits query results), Derived fields (extract structured data from log lines)
B) Install the Loki plugin via grafana-cli first — it's a community plugin
C) Loki must run on the same server as Grafana — remote Loki instances aren't supported
D) Loki only works with Grafana Cloud — self-hosted Grafana can't connect to Loki
✅ Correct Answer: A

Loki is a built-in Grafana data source. Configure it with the Loki URL (typically port 3100). Important settings: Maximum lines (limits Explore query results — default 1000, increase for deeper log analysis), Derived fields (extracts structured data like traceID from log lines to enable trace linking). Save & Test verifies connectivity.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  LOKI DATA SOURCE CONFIG:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Name: anihpj-loki                               │
  │  URL: http://loki.monitoring.svc:3100            │
  │  Max lines: 5000                                 │
  │  Derived fields:                                 │
  │    Name: traceID  Regex: trace_id=(\w+)          │
  │    → Links logs to Tempo traces automatically   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Loki runs alongside Prometheus in your monitoring namespace. Grafana connects to http://loki.monitoring:3100. Promtail (Loki's log collector) runs as a DaemonSet on every node, tailing Django, PostgreSQL, and Nginx log files.

Data Sources Q57 of 200
What is the basic structure of a LogQL query?
A) LogQL is identical to SQL — you write SELECT statements against log tables
B) LogQL has two parts: a Log Stream Selector {app="anihpj-api"} (which log streams to query) + an optional Log Pipeline |= "ERROR" (filter/transform the log lines). The stream selector is REQUIRED, the pipeline is optional
C) LogQL uses a JSON query format — you can't write queries as text, only as JSON objects
D) LogQL queries are written in YAML — the same format as Kubernetes manifests
✅ Correct Answer: B

A LogQL query has two parts: Log Stream Selector — wrapped in {}, selects which log streams to query using label matchers (e.g., {app="anihpj-api", env="prod"}). Log Pipeline — appended after |, filters and transforms the log lines (e.g., |= "ERROR" for contains, | json to parse JSON, | line_format to reformat). The stream selector is mandatory; the pipeline is optional.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  LogQL ANATOMY:
  {app="anihpj-api"} |= "ERROR" | json | line_format "{{.msg}}"

  └─ Stream Selector ─┘└─ Pipeline ─────────────────────────────┘
     (REQUIRED)            (optional, chainable)

  Stream: Which logs?     Pipeline: Filter + transform
  {app="api"}             |= "ERROR"     → contains
                          != "debug"     → not contains
                          |~ "5\\d{2}"   → regex match
                          | json         → parse JSON
                          | logfmt       → parse logfmt

For anihpj/jobpost: {app="anihpj-api"} |= "ERROR" shows all Django error logs. {app="anihpj-api"} |~ "5\\d{2}" shows only 5xx HTTP errors. {job="nginx"} | json | status =~ "5.." parses Nginx JSON logs and filters for 5xx responses.

Data Sources Q58 of 200
Match each LogQL operator to its function: |= , != , |~ , !~
A) All four operators are aliases — they all mean "contains this string"
B) |= (JSON parse), != (logfmt parse), |~ (regex extract), !~ (label format)
C) These are numeric operators for metrics queries — |= (equals), != (not equals), etc.
D) |= (line CONTAINS string), != (line does NOT contain string), |~ (line matches REGEX), !~ (line does NOT match regex). These filter log lines in the pipeline after the stream selector
✅ Correct Answer: D

The four LogQL filter operators are case-sensitive and operate on the log line text: |= (line contains — substring match), != (line does NOT contain), |~ (line matches regex — RE2 syntax), !~ (line does NOT match regex). Multiple filters can be chained: {app="api"} |= "ERROR" != "timeout" — show ERROR lines that don't mention timeout.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  LogQL FILTER OPERATORS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  |= "substring"    line contains "substring"      │
  │  != "substring"    line does NOT contain          │
  │  |~ "regex"        line matches RE2 regex         │
  │  !~ "regex"        line does NOT match regex      │
  │                                                   │
  │  Chaining:                                        │
  │  {app="api"} |= "ERROR" != "heartbeat"           │
  │  → Show ERROR lines, exclude heartbeat checks    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: {app="anihpj-api"} |= "OperationalError" |~ "connection.*refused" finds database connection errors specifically. {app="anihpj-api"} != "200" |~ "[45]\\d{2}" shows only non-200 HTTP responses.

Data Sources Q59 of 200
How do you turn Loki logs into metrics using LogQL?
A) Loki can't generate metrics — you must export logs to Prometheus first
B) Add a "Metrics" panel to the Loki data source configuration
C) Use LogQL metric queries: rate({app="api"} |= "ERROR" [5m]) counts errors per second. Functions like rate(), count_over_time(), bytes_rate(), and sum by() let you create graphs and alerts from log data
D) Use the Loki-to-Prometheus exporter plugin that converts logs to Prometheus metrics
✅ Correct Answer: C

LogQL metric queries wrap a log query in an aggregation function: rate({app="api"} |= "ERROR" [5m]) gives the per-second error rate from logs. Key functions: rate() (per-second rate), count_over_time() (total count in range), bytes_rate() (bytes per second), sum by() (aggregate by label). This lets you create Time Series panels and alerts directly from log data — no Prometheus needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  LOGQL METRIC QUERIES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  count_over_time({app="api"}|="ERROR"[1h])       │
  │  → Total errors in the last hour                 │
  │                                                  │
  │  rate({app="api"}|="ERROR"[5m])                  │
  │  → Error rate per second (for Time Series panels)│
  │                                                  │
  │  sum by (pod) (rate({app="api"}|="ERROR"[5m]))   │
  │  → Error rate broken down per pod                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost alerting: Create an alert rule using a Loki metric query: rate({app="anihpj-api"} |= "ERROR" [5m]) > 2 fires when Django errors exceed 2 per second for 5 minutes. This alerts from LOGS — no Prometheus metric needed.

Grafana Explore mode has a rich Loki integration: Live tailing (real-time log streaming — click "Live" button), Log context (click a log line → "Show context" to see lines before and after), Log details (expand any log line to see parsed labels and JSON fields), Split view (compare Loki logs with Prometheus metrics side-by-side for the same time range), and Derived fields (click a traceID to jump directly to Tempo).

2.5 Other Data Sources & Multi-Source QueriesQ61-Q65

Data Sources Q61 of 200
How does Grafana's Elasticsearch data source differ in query syntax from Kibana?
A) Grafana doesn't support Elasticsearch — you must use Kibana for ES data
B) Grafana uses Lucene query syntax by default (same as older Kibana versions), while modern Kibana defaults to KQL (Kibana Query Language). Grafana also supports PPL (Piped Processing Language) for newer Elasticsearch versions
C) Grafana requires SQL queries to Elasticsearch — it translates SQL to ES queries internally
D) Elasticsearch queries in Grafana are written in YAML, unlike Kibana's text-based queries
✅ Correct Answer: B

Grafana's Elasticsearch data source supports Lucene query syntax (field:value, AND/OR/NOT, range queries) and PPL (Piped Processing Language for newer ES versions with SQL plugin). Kibana defaults to KQL (field:value with autocomplete). If you're migrating from Kibana, you may need to adjust your query syntax. Grafana's ES editor also has a visual Builder mode.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  ELASTICSEARCH QUERY SYNTAX IN GRAFANA:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Lucene:  status:500 AND app:api                 │
  │  PPL:     source=logs | where status=500         │
  │  Builder: Choose fields from dropdowns           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: If you're using the ELK stack alongside Grafana, add Elasticsearch as a data source to bring your existing application logs into Grafana dashboards alongside Prometheus metrics and Loki logs.

Data Sources Q62 of 200
What query languages does Grafana's InfluxDB data source support?
A) Only SQL — InfluxDB is a SQL-compatible database
B) Only PromQL — InfluxDB uses the same query language as Prometheus
C) Both InfluxQL (SQL-like, for InfluxDB 1.x and 2.x) and Flux (functional data scripting language, InfluxDB 2.x+). You choose which language in the data source settings
D) Grafana doesn't support InfluxDB — you must use Chronograf for InfluxDB visualization
✅ Correct Answer: C

Grafana's InfluxDB data source supports InfluxQL (SQL-like syntax: SELECT mean("value") FROM "cpu" WHERE time > now() - 1h GROUP BY time(5m)) and Flux (functional pipeline: from(bucket:"telegraf") |> range(start: -1h) |> filter(fn: (r) => r._measurement == "cpu")). Select the language per query or set a default in the data source configuration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  INFLUXQL (SQL-like):
  SELECT mean("usage_idle") FROM "cpu"
  WHERE "host" = 'server01'
  AND $timeFilter GROUP BY time($__interval)

  FLUX (functional):
  from(bucket: "telegraf/autogen")
  |> range(start: v.timeRangeStart)
  |> filter(fn: (r) => r._measurement == "cpu")

For anihpj/jobpost: If you use InfluxDB for infrastructure metrics (via Telegraf), add it as a data source alongside Prometheus. Use InfluxQL for simple queries, Flux for complex data transformations.

What is a Mixed data source in Grafana and why is it powerful?
A) Mixed allows a SINGLE panel to query MULTIPLE data sources simultaneously — e.g., one line from Prometheus (request rate) and another line from PostgreSQL (jobs created) on the SAME graph. This correlates data from completely different systems in one visualization
B) Mixed combines two Grafana instances into one — it's for federation, not individual panels
C) Mixed is a data source type that merges Prometheus and Loki into a single query language
D) Mixed data sources are for testing only — they shouldn't be used in production dashboards

A Mixed data source lets a single panel pull data from multiple data sources. In panel edit mode, select "-- Mixed --" as the data source, then add multiple queries — each with its own data source. This enables cross-system correlation that's impossible with a single data source.

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Create a Mixed panel showing HTTP requests (Prometheus) overlaid with job postings created (PostgreSQL) — does traffic correlate with job creation? This kind of cross-source insight is impossible with single-source panels.

Data Sources Q65 of 200
What are Data Links in Grafana panels?
A) Data Links connect two Grafana instances for cross-instance dashboard sharing
B) Data Links make panel values clickable — clicking a value opens another dashboard, an external URL, or a drill-down view. They can include template variables from the clicked data point (e.g., clicking a pod name opens that pod's detail dashboard)
C) Data Links are API endpoints that external systems use to push data into Grafana
D) Data Links are database foreign keys that Grafana uses to JOIN tables across data sources
✅ Correct Answer: B

Data Links turn any panel value into a clickable link. Configure them in panel settings → Data Links. The URL can include template variables like ${__data.fields.pod} (the value from the clicked row) or dashboard variables like $environment. Common uses: clicking a pod name in a table opens that pod's detail dashboard, clicking an error count opens the Loki log view filtered to that error, clicking a user ID opens the user's profile in your admin app.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  DATA LINK EXAMPLES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  URL: /d/pod-detail?var-pod=${__data.fields.pod} │
  │  → Click pod name → opens pod detail dashboard   │
  │                                                  │
  │  URL: /explore?left={"datasource":"Loki",        │
  │       "queries":[{"expr":"{pod=\"${__data...}\}"}]}│
  │  → Click pod name → opens Loki logs for that pod │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: In your "Recent Errors" Table panel, add a Data Link that opens Loki Explore filtered to that specific error message. One click from "what errors exist" → "show me the full log context of this error." Seamless drill-down.

2.6 Query Variables & Template VariablesQ66-Q70

Data Sources Q66 of 200
What are Grafana Variables and why are they essential?
A) Variables are environment variables set in grafana.ini that control server behavior
B) Variables are JavaScript variables used in custom panel plugins for data processing
C) Variables are dynamic dropdowns (or text inputs) at the top of a dashboard that let users filter and customize what data is shown. One dashboard can work for ALL environments (prod/staging/dev), ALL services, or ALL time ranges — without duplicating dashboards
D) Variables are Prometheus recording rules that pre-compute expensive queries
✅ Correct Answer: C

Variables make dashboards interactive and reusable. Instead of creating separate dashboards for production, staging, and development, create ONE dashboard with an $environment variable. Users select "production" from the dropdown, and all panels update to show production data. Variables can be used in queries (job="$service"), panel titles, and even to repeat panels/rows.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  WITHOUT VARIABLES:             WITH VARIABLES:
  3 dashboards (prod/staging/dev) → 1 dashboard + $environment dropdown
  
  ■ Production Overview           ■ anihpj Overview
  ■ Staging Overview              ┌──────────────────┐
  ■ Dev Overview                  │ Env: [production▼]│
                                  │ Svc: [api    ▼]   │
                                  └──────────────────┘
                                  → 1 dashboard = infinite combinations

For anihpj/jobpost: Create one "anihpj Overview" dashboard with $environment (production/staging) and $service (web/api/db) variables. One dashboard covers all 6 combinations — no duplication, easy maintenance.

Data Sources Q67 of 200
How many variable types does Grafana support?
A) Two: Text box and Dropdown — that's all Grafana variables can do
B) Three: Custom, Query, and Constant — these are the only variable types
C) Grafana doesn't have native variables — you must use template JSON for dynamic dashboards
D) Seven: Query (from data source), Custom (manual list), Text box (free text input), Constant (hidden fixed value), Data source (switch between data sources), Interval (time interval picker), and Ad hoc filters (dynamic key=value filters applied to all queries)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Grafana supports 7 variable types: Query (values from a data source query), Custom (manual comma-separated list), Text box (free-text input), Constant (hidden fixed value — not shown to users), Data source (switch between configured data sources), Interval (time interval picker for query step), and Ad hoc filters (dynamic key=value filters applied to ALL queries on the dashboard).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  7 VARIABLE TYPES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Query:    SELECT name FROM services (dynamic)   │
  │  Custom:   production, staging, development      │
  │  Text box: Free-form input                       │
  │  Constant:  us-east-1 (hidden, always set)       │
  │  Data src: Switch between Prometheus instances   │
  │  Interval: 1m, 5m, 15m, 1h (time step picker)   │
  │  Ad hoc:    tag=value filters (auto-discovered)  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Use Query variables for dynamic service lists from Prometheus labels, Custom variables for environment selection, Constant variables for cloud region, and Data source variables to switch between production and staging Prometheus instances.

Data Sources Q68 of 200
What is the difference between $variable and ${variable:csv} syntax?
A) $variable returns a single value. ${variable:csv} returns all selected values as a comma-separated list (for multi-value variables). Other formats: :json (JSON array), :doublequote, :pipe, :sqlstring, :percentencode, :queryparam
B) $variable is deprecated; ${variable:csv} is the modern replacement for all use cases
C) ${variable:csv} exports the variable to a CSV file — it doesn't affect query formatting
D) There is no difference — they are aliases that produce the same output
✅ Correct Answer: A

Grafana's variable syntax supports multiple formatting options. $variable returns a single value. ${variable} is the same but allows adjacent text. Advanced formats: :csv (comma-separated), :json (JSON array), :doublequote, :pipe (|), :sqlstring (SQL-safe single-quoted), :percentencode (URL encoding), :queryparam (var=val&var=val2). These are essential when passing multi-value variables to SQL IN clauses or API URLs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  VARIABLE FORMAT SYNTAX (if user selects "api" and "web"):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $service           → api                        │
  │  ${service}         → api                        │
  │  ${service:csv}     → api,web                    │
  │  ${service:json}    → ["api","web"]              │
  │  ${service:pipe}    → api|web                    │
  │  ${service:sqlstring} → 'api','web'              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost SQL queries: Use WHERE service IN (${service:sqlstring}) when the user selects multiple services. For Prometheus: {job=~"${service:pipe}"} matches multiple jobs using regex.

Data Sources Q69 of 200
How do you chain variables so that selecting "Production" in one dropdown filters the available options in the next dropdown?
A) Variable chaining requires a custom JavaScript plugin — it's not built into Grafana
B) Use Grafana's "Dependent Variable" plugin from the plugin catalog
C) In the child variable's Query, reference the parent variable: label_values(up{env="$environment"}, service). Grafana automatically re-queries the child variable when the parent changes, filtering its options
D) Chain them in grafana.ini using the variable_chain configuration option
✅ Correct Answer: C

Variable chaining (cascading/dependent variables) works by referencing the parent variable in the child variable's query. Example: $environment variable (parent) → $service variable (child) with query label_values(up{env="$environment"}, service). When the user selects "production" from the environment dropdown, Grafana automatically re-runs the service query filtered to env="production", showing only production services. Common chain: Region → Cluster → Namespace → Service.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  VARIABLE CHAINING:
  $environment (parent)
      │ user selects "production"
      ▼
  $cluster (child)
      Query: label_values(kube_node_info{env="$environment"}, cluster)
      → Shows only production clusters: "kubeadm", "eks-prod"
      │ user selects "kubeadm"
      ▼
  $namespace (grandchild)
      Query: label_values(kube_namespace_labels{cluster="$cluster"}, ns)
      → Shows only kubeadm namespaces

For anihpj/jobpost: Chain $environment → $cluster → $service. Users first pick production/staging, then the cluster narrows, then services narrow. Three clicks to zoom into exactly the data they need — all from one dashboard.

Data Sources Q70 of 200
How do you auto-generate one panel per value of a variable (e.g., one graph per service)?
A) Manually create a panel for each service — Grafana cannot auto-generate panels
B) Enable "Repeat by variable" in the panel or row settings. Grafana will clone the panel/row once per variable value — e.g., if $service has 3 values, you get 3 identical panels, each filtered to one service. Use $service in the panel title to identify each
C) Use the "Clone Panel" button and manually update each clone's query — it's semi-automated
D) Repeat requires Grafana Enterprise — it's a premium feature not available in OSS
✅ Correct Answer: B

The "Repeat by variable" feature (in panel or row settings → Repeat options) auto-generates one copy of the panel/row per value of the selected variable. If the variable changes (user selects different values), panels are automatically created/destroyed. This enables "dynamic dashboards" that adapt to your infrastructure. Tip: use $variable in the panel title so each copy is labeled with its value.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  REPEAT BY VARIABLE:
  Panel: HTTP Request Rate
  Query: rate(http_requests{job="$service"}[5m])
  Title: $service — Request Rate
  Repeat: by variable $service (values: api, web, worker)

  RESULT (3 panels auto-generated):
  ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ api — Request Rate   │
  │ 📈 ───╱──────        │
  ├──────────────────────┤
  │ web — Request Rate   │
  │ 📈 ──────╱───        │
  ├──────────────────────┤
  │ worker — Request Rate│
  │ 📈 ──╱────────       │
  └──────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Create one "Request Rate" panel with Repeat by $service. Grafana auto-generates separate panels for anihpj-api, anihpj-web, and anihpj-worker. When you add a new service, it automatically appears — no dashboard editing needed.

Q71-Q120 50 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

3.1 Dashboard FundamentalsQ71-Q78

Dashboards Q71 of 200
How do you create a new dashboard in Grafana from scratch?
A) Write a JSON file defining the dashboard and upload it via the Grafana API — there's no UI for dashboard creation
B) Click "+" → "Dashboard" → "Add new panel" (or "Add row" first). This opens the panel editor where you select a data source, write a query, and choose a visualization — then save the dashboard
C) Use grafana-cli dashboard create — dashboards are only created through the CLI
D) Import a dashboard from grafana.com — you can't create dashboards from scratch in the UI
✅ Correct Answer: B

Creating a dashboard is simple: Click the "+" icon in the sidebar → Dashboard → you'll see an empty canvas. Click "Add visualization" (or "Add panel") to create your first panel. Select a data source, write a query, pick a visualization type, configure the panel, and click Apply. Repeat for more panels, organize into rows, and Save the dashboard with a name, folder, and tags. That's it — your dashboard is live.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Detailed Illustration
  DASHBOARD CREATION WORKFLOW:
  + → Dashboard → Add visualization →
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Select data source (Prometheus)   │
  │  ② Write query (PromQL/SQL/LogQL)    │
  │  ③ Choose visualization (Time Series)│
  │  ④ Configure (units, thresholds)     │
  │  ⑤ Apply → Save dashboard            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Create your first dashboard with a Prometheus Time Series panel showing rate(django_http_requests_total[5m]). Add a Stat panel from PostgreSQL showing active job count. Save as "anihpj Overview" in the Production folder with tags "anihpj, overview."

Dashboards Q72 of 200
What does the Dashboard JSON model contain?
A) Only the panel titles and their grid positions — everything else is stored in the Grafana database separately
B) The raw metric data displayed on the dashboard — JSON is Grafana's data storage format
C) The complete dashboard definition: panels (with queries, visualizations, and configurations), rows/layout, template variables, time settings, annotations, links, tags, and version history — all in a single JSON file that's portable between Grafana instances
D) Only the dashboard name and folder — panels are stored as separate JSON files
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Dashboard JSON is the complete, portable definition of a dashboard. It contains everything: panels (queries, visualizations, field configs), templating (variables), time (time range, refresh), annotations, links, tags, and layout (grid positions). This makes dashboards version-controllable in Git, shareable via grafana.com, and portable between Grafana instances.

Detailed Illustration
  DASHBOARD JSON STRUCTURE:
  {
    "dashboard": {
      "title": "anihpj Overview",
      "uid": "anihpj-overview",
      "templating": { "list": [...] },  // Variables
      "panels": [...],                   // All panels
      "time": { "from": "now-1h" },     // Time defaults
      "tags": ["anihpj", "production"]
    }
  }

For anihpj/jobpost: Export your dashboard JSON and store it in Git alongside your Kubernetes manifests. This gives you dashboard-as-code — version history, code review, and disaster recovery for your dashboards.

Dashboards Q73 of 200
How many ways can you import a dashboard into Grafana?
A) Only one: paste the dashboard JSON into the Import text box
B) Two: upload a JSON file or paste JSON text
C) Import is not available in Grafana OSS — it's an Enterprise feature
D) Three: Upload a JSON file, paste JSON text directly, or import by ID from grafana.com/dashboards (browse thousands of community dashboards and import with one click)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Grafana supports three import methods: Upload JSON file (drag-and-drop or file picker), Paste JSON (paste the raw dashboard JSON), and Import via grafana.com ID (enter a dashboard ID from grafana.com/dashboards — e.g., ID 315 for "Kubernetes cluster monitoring"). After import, you map the dashboard's data source placeholders to your actual data sources.

Detailed Illustration
  IMPORT METHODS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Upload .json file (drag & drop)               │
  │  ② Paste raw JSON text                           │
  │  ③ grafana.com ID (e.g., 1860 for Node Exporter)│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Browse grafana.com/dashboards for "Django" or "PostgreSQL" community dashboards. Import one with ID and map the Prometheus data source to your anihpj-prometheus instance. Instantly get pre-built monitoring — customize from there.

Dashboards Q74 of 200
How do dashboard variables make one dashboard work for multiple environments?
A) Create a $environment variable (Query type from Prometheus label) and use job=~"anihpj-.*${environment}.*" in all panel queries. Users select "prod" or "staging" from a dropdown, and all panels instantly switch to the selected environment's data
B) Clone the dashboard and change the data source for each environment — variables can't switch environments
C) Set an environment variable in grafana.ini — it's a server-wide setting, not per-dashboard
D) Use the Data source variable type — switch the entire dashboard's data source at once
✅ Correct Answer: A

Dashboard variables make a single dashboard environment-aware. Create a $environment variable (e.g., Custom type with values "prod, staging") and reference it in queries: {job=~"anihpj-.*${environment}.*"}. When a user selects "prod" from the dropdown, ALL panels on the dashboard automatically update to show production data. One dashboard, infinite environments.

Detailed Illustration
  BEFORE: 3 separate dashboards (prod/staging/dev)
  AFTER:  1 dashboard + $environment dropdown

  Dashboard: "anihpj Overview"
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐

  │  All panels now show production data │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: One "anihpj Overview" dashboard with $environment set to "production" by default. During a staging deployment, switch to "staging" to verify metrics. No need for duplicate dashboards.

Dashboards Q75 of 200
What are Dashboard tags used for?
A) Tags are internal metadata that Grafana uses for performance optimization — not visible to users
B) Tags are user-defined labels for categorizing and searching dashboards. You can filter the dashboard browser by tag (e.g., show all dashboards tagged "production" and "anihpj") and tag-based access control is supported in Grafana Enterprise
C) Tags are only used for grafana.com dashboard discovery — they don't work on self-hosted instances
D) Tags are automatically generated from dashboard content — users cannot manually set them
✅ Correct Answer: B

Dashboard tags are user-defined labels (set in Dashboard Settings → General → Tags) that help organize, search, and filter dashboards. In the dashboard browser, you can filter by one or more tags to instantly narrow down hundreds of dashboards. Tags are also used by the Dashboard List panel and can be used for tag-based access control.

Detailed Illustration
  TAG FILTERING:
  Dashboards → Browse → Filter by tag
  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Tags: [anihpj ×] [production ×]   │
  │  Showing 4 of 150 dashboards      │
  └────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Tag all anihpj dashboards with "anihpj" and the environment ("production"/"staging"). Team members filter by "anihpj" to see only relevant dashboards. Add "incident" tag to dashboards used during on-call for quick access.

Dashboards Q76 of 200
How do Grafana folders differ from tags for organizing dashboards?
A) Folders and tags are the same — they both just label dashboards for search
B) Tags control permissions; folders are only for visual grouping
C) Folders are hierarchical containers with permissions (Editor/Viewer/Admin per team or user) — dashboards in a folder inherit those permissions. Tags are flat metadata labels for search/filtering. Folders = access control; Tags = discovery
D) Folders are deprecated in favor of tags in Grafana 10+
✅ Correct Answer: C

Folders and Tags serve different purposes: Folders provide hierarchical organization and access control (you can grant a team Editor access to one folder and Viewer access to another). Tags are flat metadata for search and filtering. A dashboard belongs to exactly ONE folder but can have MANY tags. Use both: folders for permissions, tags for discovery.

Detailed Illustration
  FOLDERS vs TAGS:
  ┌────────────┬─────────────────┬────────────────┐
  │            │    FOLDERS      │     TAGS       │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Structure  │ Hierarchical    │ Flat           │
  │ Permissions│ Yes (per folder)│ No             │
  │ One-to-many│ 1 folder/dash   │ Many tags/dash │
  │ Search     │ Filter by folder│ Filter by tag  │
  │ Best for   │ Access control  │ Discovery      │
  └────────────┴─────────────────┴────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Use folders for team-based access control (Platform-Eng = Editor on Production folder, Developers = Viewer). Use tags for discovery (tag all anihpj dashboards with "anihpj" so anyone can find them regardless of folder).

Dashboards Q77 of 200
How does Grafana's dashboard version history work?
A) Every time you save a dashboard (Ctrl+S), Grafana creates a new version. Dashboard Settings → Versions shows the full history with timestamps, user, and optional message. You can diff any two versions (JSON diff) and restore any previous version with one click
B) Version history must be enabled per-dashboard in Settings — it's off by default
C) Only the last 5 versions are kept — older versions are automatically deleted
D) Version history is a Grafana Cloud exclusive feature — not available in OSS
✅ Correct Answer: A

Grafana automatically creates a new version on every save — no configuration needed. Go to Dashboard Settings → Versions to see the complete history. You can compare (diff) any two versions to see exactly what changed (JSON diff view), and restore any version with one click. This is a built-in safety net — if you break a dashboard, you're never more than one click away from recovery.

Detailed Illustration
  VERSION HISTORY:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  v12  Jan 15 14:30  anihpj  "Fixed alerts"  │
  │  v11  Jan 15 11:00  anihpj  "Added DB panel"│
  │  v10  Jan 14 16:45  jane    "Updated query" │
  │  ...                                         │
  │  [Compare v11 ↔ v10]  [Restore v10]          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Before making major dashboard changes, save with a descriptive message like "Pre-cleanup baseline." If the changes cause issues, diff to see what went wrong and restore the baseline in seconds.

Dashboards Q78 of 200
What is dashboard provisioning and why use it?
A) Dashboard provisioning is the process of manually creating dashboards through the Grafana UI
B) Provisioning means purchasing dashboards from the Grafana marketplace
C) Provisioning is only for Grafana Cloud — self-hosted instances can't provision dashboards
D) Provisioning lets you define dashboards (and data sources) as YAML/JSON files that Grafana automatically loads on startup. This enables GitOps for dashboards — store them in Git, deploy via CI/CD, and never manually create dashboards in the UI
✅ Correct Answer: D

Dashboard provisioning allows defining dashboards as files that Grafana automatically loads. Place JSON files in /etc/grafana/provisioning/dashboards/ (or configure a different path in a provisioning YAML file), and Grafana imports them on startup and watches for changes. This enables GitOps for dashboards: store dashboard JSON in Git, deploy via CI/CD, never manually create dashboards in the UI. Changes go through code review.

Detailed Illustration
  DASHBOARD PROVISIONING (GitOps):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  /etc/grafana/provisioning/dashboards/           │
  │  ├── dashboard-provider.yaml  (config)           │
  │  └── anihpj-overview.json     (dashboard)        │
  │                                                  │
  │  Grafana watches this directory → auto-imports   │
  │  → Dashboard appears in UI → read-only in UI     │
  │  → Changes via Git → CI/CD → Grafana reloads     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Store dashboard JSON in your anihpj/monitoring/grafana/dashboards/ directory alongside your Kubernetes manifests. Deploy via your GitOps pipeline (ArgoCD/Flux). Dashboards are version-controlled, reviewed, and auditable — just like application code.

3.2 Panel Types Deep Dive — Visualizations GuideQ79-Q94

Dashboards Q79 of 200
What is the Time Series panel and what makes it Grafana's most-used visualization?
A) The Time Series panel visualizes data over time as lines, bars, or points. It supports gradients, stacking, multiple Y-axes, thresholds, annotations, and legend customization — making it the most versatile panel for 90% of monitoring use cases
B) Time Series panel is a premium Grafana Enterprise feature for financial charting
C) Time Series is the old name for the Graph panel (deprecated) — it's being replaced by the Stat panel
D) Time Series panels only work with Prometheus data — they can't use other data sources
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Time Series panel is Grafana's flagship visualization — it displays numeric data over time with extensive customization: line/bar/point styles, gradient fill (area below line), stacking (cumulative display), multiple Y-axes, thresholds (color-coded regions), annotations overlay, and full legend control. It works with any data source that returns time-value pairs. For HTTP request rates, CPU usage, or any metric over time, Time Series is the panel you want.

Detailed Illustration
  TIME SERIES OPTIONS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Style: Lines | Bars | Points                    │
  │  Fill: Solid | Gradient | None                   │
  │  Stack: Off | Normal | 100%                      │
  │  Axes: Left Y | Right Y | Dual                   │
  │  Thresholds: Red > 100, Yellow > 50, Green < 50  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Use Time Series panels for HTTP request rates, error rates, latency percentiles, database query rates, and Redis cache hit ratios — essentially any metric that changes over time.

Dashboards Q80 of 200
What is the Stat panel best used for?
A) Displaying time-series graphs showing trends over time
B) Showing a single large number with optional sparkline — perfect for "Active Jobs: 247", "Error Rate: 2.3%", "Users Online: 1,203". The most common KPI visualization
C) Displaying tabular data with sortable columns
D) Showing geographic data on a world map
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Stat panel displays a single value with optional sparkline, thresholds, and value mappings. Ideal for KPIs: active users, error count, revenue. Sparkline adds trend without taking space.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Stat Panel — KPI Visualization
  STAT PANEL ANATOMY:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │           247                      │      │
  │  │        Active Jobs                 │      │
  │  │      ╱╲   ╱╲  (sparkline)          │      │
  │  │     ╱  ╲_╱  ╲___                  │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │  🟢 Green (< 50) 🟡 Yellow (50-100) 🔴 (100+)│

For anihpj/jobpost: "Active Jobs: 247" with sparkline showing 24-hour trend.

Dashboards Q81 of 200
What is the Gauge panel and when should you use it vs a Stat panel?
A) Gauge and Stat are identical — the Gauge is just a circular Stat panel
B) Gauge panels can only display percentages, Stat panels can display any value
C) Gauge shows a value within a min-max range with a colored arc/bar — best when you care about "how full" something is (CPU 72% of 100%, disk 85% of 1TB). Stat panel shows a standalone value with sparkline — best when the range isn't relevant
D) Gauge panels are deprecated in favor of Stat panels in Grafana 10+
✅ Correct Answer: C

Gauge visualizes a value within a defined range (min-max) with colored thresholds along the arc/bar. Perfect for "How full is X?": disk usage (850GB / 1TB = 85%), memory (6.4GB / 8GB = 80%), request queue depth (23 / 100 = 23%). The Stat panel is better when the range doesn't matter — you just need the number (e.g., "247 active jobs" — there's no "max jobs").

Detailed Illustration
  GAUGE:                       STAT:
  ┌──────────┐                 ┌──────────┐
  │    🟡    │                 │   247    │
  │   72%    │                 │  Active  │
  │   CPU    │                 │   Jobs   │
  │ 0────100 │                 │  ───╱──  │
  └──────────┘                 └──────────┘
  Range matters                Range irrelevant

For anihpj/jobpost: Use Gauge for system resources (CPU %, memory %, disk %) where "100% = full" is meaningful. Use Stat for business metrics (active jobs, users online, applications today) where there's no natural maximum.

Dashboards Q82 of 200
What visualization would you use to show "Disk Usage Across 10 Servers" at a glance?
A) 10 individual Stat panels — one per server
B) A Pie Chart showing each server's share of total disk
C) A Time Series line chart with 10 overlapping lines
D) A Bar Gauge panel — horizontal or vertical bars, one per server, with thresholds (green <70%, yellow <85%, red >=85%), showing all 10 servers in a single compact visualization
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Bar Gauge panel displays multiple values as horizontal or vertical bars within a min-max range, with threshold coloring. It's ideal for comparing a single metric across multiple entities: disk usage per server, memory usage per pod, error rate per endpoint. Each entity gets one bar — easy to spot the over-utilized server at a glance.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Bar Gauge — Multi-Entity Comparison at a Glance
  BAR GAUGE — Disk Usage Across 5 Servers:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  server-01  🟩🟩🟩🟩🟩🟩🟩⬜⬜⬜  72%     │
  │  server-02  🟩🟩🟩🟩🟩⬜⬜⬜⬜⬜  48%     │
  │  server-03  🟨🟨🟨🟨🟨🟨🟨🟨⬜⬜  83% ⚠  │
  │  server-04  🟥🟥🟥🟥🟥🟥🟥🟥🟥⬜  94% 🚨 │
  │  server-05  🟩🟩🟩🟩⬜⬜⬜⬜⬜⬜  35%     │
  └────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Thresholds: Green <70% | Yellow <85% | Red ≥85%

  Best for: "N entities, 1 metric" comparisons
  ─ Disk usage per node
  ─ Memory per pod  ─ Error rate per endpoint

For anihpj/jobpost: Use a Bar Gauge to show "Error Rate per API Endpoint" — one bar per endpoint (/api/jobs, /api/apply, /admin) — instantly spot which endpoint is misbehaving.

A) Sorting (click column headers), filtering (per-column filters), cell coloring (based on thresholds or value mappings), column hiding/showing, pagination, and cell links (clickable values that navigate to other dashboards)
B) The Table panel is a basic data grid with no advanced features
C) Table panels can only display static data — they can't use variables or time filters
D) The Table panel supports pivot tables and Excel-like formulas for data processing

The Table panel is Grafana's most feature-rich panel for tabular data: sortable columns, filterable columns, cell coloring (conditional formatting via thresholds or value mappings), column visibility control, pagination, cell links (click values to navigate), and footer calculations (sum, avg, count).

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Features:                   Cell coloring:
  ① Column sorting (click)    🟢 = good/approved
  ② Per-column filters        🟡 = warning/pending
  ③ Cell color by threshold   🔴 = critical/rejected
  ④ Pagination (100+ rows)
  ⑤ Footer: Sum, Avg, Count   Cell links:
  ⑥ Cell links → drill down   Click name → detail dashboard

Dashboards Q84 of 200
What does the Heatmap panel visualize?
A) Geographic data shown as hot/cold regions on a world map
B) 2D density — X-axis is time, Y-axis is a value dimension (e.g., latency), and color intensity shows frequency. Perfect for "Request latency distribution over time" or "API endpoint response times per hour"
C) Server room temperature data — requires physical temperature sensors
D) A Heatmap is the same as a Bar Gauge but with different color options
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Heatmap panel visualizes the distribution of values over time in two dimensions: X-axis = time, Y-axis = value buckets, color intensity = frequency. It answers "Where do most requests fall in latency, and how does that change over time?" Darker colors = more data points in that bucket. Essential for latency distribution analysis and identifying the "long tail" of slow requests.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Heatmap — Latency Distribution Over Time
  HEATMAP: API Request Latency (darker = more requests)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Y: Latency Buckets                          │
  │  500ms+  ░░░░░░░░▒▒░░░░░░░░░▓▓░░░░           │
  │  200ms   ░░░░▒▒▒▒▒▒▓▓░░░░▒▒▒▒▒▒░░░           │
  │  100ms   ░░▒▒▒▒▓▓▓▓▓▓░░▒▒▒▒▓▓▓▓░░░           │
  │   50ms   ░▒▒▒▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▒▒▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▒░░           │
  │   10ms   ▒▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▒░ ← Normal │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
            08:00    10:00    12:00    14:00
            X: Time →

  Reading the heatmap: At 10:00, there's a dark band
  at 500ms+ = latency spike! Most requests normally
  cluster at 10-50ms (bottom band).

For anihpj: "API Latency Distribution" heatmap — spot when the latency profile shifts from fast (10-50ms) to slow (200ms+) during load spikes.

Dashboards Q88 of 200
What does the Traces panel display?
A) Network packet traces — it shows raw TCP/IP packet data for debugging
B) Application stack traces — it shows Java/Python exception call stacks
C) Distributed traces from Tempo/Jaeger/Zipkin — waterfall diagrams showing how a request flows through multiple services (anihpj-web → anihpj-api → anihpj-db), with timing for each span. Identifies bottlenecks in microservice architectures
D) Traces panel requires Grafana Enterprise — not available in OSS
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Traces panel visualizes distributed traces — waterfall diagrams showing a request's journey through multiple services with timing for each span. It integrates with Tempo, Jaeger, and Zipkin. Each span shows the service name, operation, and duration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Traces Panel — Distributed Tracing Waterfall
  TRACE: Job Application Request (waterfall view)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TOTAL: 2,030ms                                  │
  │                                                  │
  │  Browser ─────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  Nginx    █ 10ms                                 │
  │  Django   ██████████ 200ms                       │
  │  PG-DB    ██████████████████████████████ 1800ms  │
  │  Redis    ██ 20ms                                │
  │           │    │    │    │    │    │    │         │
  │          0ms  400  800  1200 1600 2000ms         │
  │                                                  │
  │  🔴 BOTTLENECK: PostgreSQL query = 88% of time!  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Each bar = a "span" (single operation in one service)
  Width = duration. Parent→child shows call hierarchy.

For anihpj: Trace a job application request — Browser → Nginx (10ms) → Django API (200ms) → PostgreSQL query (1800ms). The DB query dominates — time to add an index or cache!

Dashboards Q93 of 200
What does the Histogram panel show that a Time Series panel cannot?
A) The DISTRIBUTION of values — how many requests fall into each latency bucket (0-10ms, 10-50ms, 50-100ms, etc.). Time Series shows values over time; Histogram shows the shape of your data's distribution
B) The Histogram panel is just a Time Series panel with bars instead of lines — they show the same data
C) Histogram panels are for financial data only — stock price distributions
D) The Histogram panel replaces the Heatmap — they're functionally identical in Grafana 10
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Histogram panel visualizes the statistical distribution of values — it groups data into buckets (bins) and shows how many values fall into each. This reveals the SHAPE of your data: is it normally distributed? Skewed? Bimodal? While a Time Series shows latency at each point in time, a Histogram shows "most requests take 10-30ms, but there's a long tail at 500ms+."

📝 Detailed Explanation
Histogram — Distribution Shape Analysis
  HISTOGRAM: Request latency distribution
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  300┤  ▄▄▄                            │
  │  200┤  ███▄▄                          │
  │  100┤▄▄██████▄▄                      │
  │   50┤████████████▄▄    ▄              │
  │    0┼──────────────────▀────────────  │
  │     10ms  50ms  100ms  500ms  1s+    │
  │                                       │
  │  → Most requests: 10-50ms (good!)     │
  │  → Long tail: some at 500ms+ (investigate!)
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Use a Histogram panel to analyze API latency distribution. If the P95 is high but the histogram shows 95% of requests are fast, you have a long-tail problem — a few very slow requests, not a systemic slowdown.

3.3 Panel Configuration & CustomizationQ95-Q102

Dashboards Q95 of 200
How do you configure a panel's title, description, and transparent background?
A) These settings are only available in the dashboard JSON — not in the UI
B) In Panel Edit → Panel options (right sidebar): Title (text displayed above the panel), Description (tooltip shown when hovering over the panel's 'i' icon), and Transparent background (makes the panel background transparent to show the dashboard background)
C) Title is set in the query editor; description and transparency require the Text panel
D) Panel titles are auto-generated from the query — manual titles aren't supported

  │  │                         dashboard   │     │
  │  │                         background  │     │

  │                                              │

  │  Description: Tooltip on ℹ️ hover            │
  │  Transparent: Remove panel background        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
