Dashboards Q96 of 200
What are Panel Links and how are they configured?
A) Panel Links connect two panels so they share the same data source query
B) Panel Links are automatically generated by Grafana to link panels to their data sources
C) Panel Links appear in the top-left corner of a panel (link icon). Clicking navigates to another dashboard, an external URL, or a specific panel. They can include dashboard variables and time range parameters. Configured in Panel Edit → Links
D) Panel Links require Grafana Enterprise — not available in OSS
✅ Correct Answer: C

Panel Links add navigation to any panel — click the link icon in the panel's top-left corner to jump to a related dashboard, documentation URL, or external system. Configured in Panel Edit → Links. URLs support template variables: /d/detail-dashboard?var-service=$service.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Panel Links — Dashboard-to-Dashboard Navigation
  PANEL LINKS (top-left corner of any panel):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🔗 ▼   Active Jobs: 247                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────┐        │
  │  │ → Job Details Dashboard          │        │
  │  │ → Documentation: API Reference   │        │
  │  │ → External: PagerDuty            │        │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────┘        │
  │                                              │
  │  Link URL template example:                  │
  │  /d/job-detail?var-job=$job&from=$__from     │
  │                    ↑              ↑          │
  │             panel variable    time range     │
  │                                              │
  │  Types: Dashboard | Absolute URL | Panel     │
  │  Pass: Variables, time range, series name    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Add a Panel Link from the "Active Jobs" Stat panel to the "Job Details" dashboard, passing the selected time range so the detail view shows the same period.

Dashboards Q97 of 200
How do Thresholds work in Grafana panels?
A) Thresholds define color-coded value boundaries — Green (OK), Yellow/Orange (Warning), Red (Critical). They change the panel's background, gauge color, or time series region color based on the current value. Configurable in absolute or percentage mode
B) Thresholds are only available in the Gauge panel — other panels don't support them
C) Thresholds trigger Grafana alerts — they're the same as alert rules
D) Thresholds require manual refreshing — they don't update automatically with new data
✅ Correct Answer: A

Thresholds define value boundaries with associated colors. In Stat/Gauge panels: the background or bar changes color as the value crosses thresholds. In Time Series: threshold lines or colored regions appear on the graph. Modes: Absolute (fixed values) or Percentage (% of min-max range).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Thresholds — Color-Coded Value Boundaries
  THRESHOLD MODES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ABSOLUTE:                  PERCENTAGE:       │
  │  Red:    > 90               Red:    > 90%    │
  │  Orange: > 70               Orange: > 70%    │
  │  Green:  ≤ 70  (base)       Green:  ≤ 70%    │
  │                                              │
  │  STAT PANEL:                TIME SERIES:      │
  │  ┌──────────┐               ╱╲                │
  │  │   🟡     │              ╱  ╲  ─── Red     │
  │  │   72%    │             ╱    ╲ ─── Orange  │
  │  │   CPU   │            ╱______╲─── Green    │
  │  └──────────┘                                │
  │  Bg color = threshold      Regions = colored  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Color scheme: 🟢 Green=OK | 🟡 Yellow=Warn | 🔴 Red=Crit

For anihpj: Set thresholds on your error rate Stat panel — Green <1%, Yellow 1-5%, Red >5% — instantly visible severity at a glance without reading numbers.

Dashboards Q98 of 200
What are Value Mappings in Grafana?
A) Value Mappings convert metric units (bytes→GB, seconds→hours) — they're the same as Units
B) Value Mappings replace raw values with display text — 0→"Down", 1→"Up", null→"No Data", or map numeric ranges to labels like 80-100→"Healthy". They make technical values human-readable without changing the underlying data
C) Value Mappings are SQL JOIN operations that map foreign keys to display names
D) Value Mappings are used only in the Table panel — other panels can't use them
✅ Correct Answer: B

Value Mappings translate raw values into human-readable display text. Types: Value (exact match — 1 → "Running"), Range (0-49 → "Low", 50-79 → "Medium", 80-100 → "High"), Regex (pattern matching), Special (null → "N/A", NaN → "Error"). They only affect DISPLAY — the underlying data is unchanged.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Value Mappings — Numbers → Human-Readable Labels
  VALUE MAPPING TYPES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  RAW VALUE          DISPLAYS AS              │
  │  ─────────────────  ───────────────────       │
  │  0              →   🔴 Down                  │
  │  1              →   🟡 Degraded              │
  │  2              →   🟢 Healthy               │
  │  null           →   No Data                  │
  │                                              │
  │  RANGE MAPPING:                              │
  │  0–49           →   Low                      │
  │  50–79          →   Medium                   │
  │  80–100         →   High                     │
  │                                              │
  │  REGEX MAPPING:                              │
  │  /error.*timeout/ → ⏱️ DB Timeout            │
  │                                              │
  │  💡 Display only — data is unchanged!        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Map status codes — 0→"Down", 1→"Degraded", 2→"Healthy" — your dashboard shows words, not cryptic numbers.

Dashboards Q99 of 200
What are Field Overrides and how do they differ from standard field options?
A) Field Overrides are the same as standard options — just a different name in the UI
B) Field Overrides change the underlying data; standard options only change the display
C) Field Overrides are global settings applied to all panels; standard options are per-panel
D) Standard options apply to ALL fields/series equally. Field Overrides target SPECIFIC fields by name, regex, or type — e.g., make the "errors" series red with 2 decimal places, while keeping other series at default settings
✅ Correct Answer: D

Field Overrides let you customize individual fields/series differently from the defaults. Target a field by Name (exact match), Name regex (pattern match), Type (numeric vs string), or Data link presence. Then override any property: color, unit, decimals, display name, thresholds, min/max, mappings.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Field Overrides — Per-Field Customization
  STANDARD OPTIONS (apply to ALL fields):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Unit: bytes | Decimals: 2 | Color: blue     │
  │  → ALL series get same unit, decimals, color │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  FIELD OVERRIDES (target SPECIFIC fields):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Override 1: [Name = "errors"]               │
  │    → Color: RED | Decimals: 0 | Unit: count  │
  │                                              │
  │  Override 2: [Name regex = "/latency.*/"]    │
  │    → Unit: ms | Decimals: 1 | Color: ORANGE  │
  │                                              │
  │  Override 3: [Type = "string"]               │
  │    → Display name: "Status"                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Target methods: Name | Name regex | Type | Data link
  Override any: Color, Unit, Decimals, Display name,
  Thresholds, Min/Max, Mappings, No value

For anihpj: In a multi-series Time Series panel, override the "errors" field to always display in RED with 0 decimals and "requests" in BLUE — instant visual distinction between error and success metrics.

3.4 Transformations — Molding Data Before DisplayQ103-Q108

Dashboards Q103 of 200
What are Transformations in Grafana?
A) Transformations change the underlying data in the database — they're SQL UPDATE operations
B) Transformations are a pre-processing pipeline applied to query results BEFORE rendering — they reshape, filter, merge, or aggregate the Data Frames without modifying the source data. Think of them as "Grafana's data processing layer"
C) Transformations are panel type conversions — changing a Time Series into a Stat panel
D) Transformations are only available in Grafana Enterprise for data compliance purposes
✅ Correct Answer: B

Transformations are operations applied to Data Frames between the query result and the panel renderer. They let you reshape data without modifying the source or the query. Common transformations: Reduce, Merge, Filter, Group by, Sort by, Join by field, Rename, Series to rows. Transformations are chainable — apply multiple in sequence.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Transformations Pipeline — Grafana's Data Processing Layer
  QUERY → TRANSFORMATIONS (chain) → RENDER
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Data Source (Prometheus/PostgreSQL/Loki)           │
  │  │                                                  │
  │  ▼  Raw Data Frame (10,000 rows)                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │  ① Filter by value: keep only errors     │      │
  │  │  ② Group by: aggregate per service       │      │
  │  │  ③ Reduce: calculate Mean                │      │
  │  │  ④ Sort by: descending                   │      │
  │  │  ⑤ Rename: "Value" → "Error Rate"       │      │
  │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────┤      │
  │  │  ↳ 100 rows of clean, shaped data        │      │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │  │                                                  │
  │  ▼  Panel receives clean data → renders instantly   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Key: Transformations don't modify source data.
  They reshape Data Frames in Grafana's memory.

For anihpj: Use Merge to combine Prometheus error rates with PostgreSQL error counts into a single panel — cross-source correlation without changing any query.

Dashboards Q104 of 200
What does the Reduce transformation do?
A) It reduces the number of panels on a dashboard to improve performance
B) It compresses query results to save network bandwidth between Grafana and the data source
C) It aggregates multiple time series values into single values per series — calculations like Min, Max, Mean, Sum, Count, Last, First. Perfect for turning "10,000 data points" into "one average value per endpoint" for a Stat or Table panel
D) It reduces the time range of the query to improve performance on slow data sources
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Reduce transformation collapses a time series (many data points) into a single calculated value per series: Min, Max, Mean, Sum, Count, Last, First, Range, StdDev, Variance, Delta, Difference, Diff percent. Use it to feed time-series data into panels that expect single values (Stat, Gauge, Table).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Reduce — Many Points → One Value Per Series
  REDUCE TRANSFORMATION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  INPUT: Time Series (100 data points)        │
  │  Time    Value                               │
  │  08:00   42                                  │
  │  08:01   45                                  │
  │  08:02   38                                  │
  │  ...     ...                                 │
  │  10:00   51                                  │
  │                                              │
  │  REDUCE (calculation = Mean)                 │
  │         ↓                                    │
  │  OUTPUT: Single value = 44.3                 │
  │                                              │
  │  Calculations: Min | Max | Mean | Sum        │
  │  Count | Last | First | Range | StdDev        │
  │  Delta | Diff | Diff% | Variance             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Use case: Prometheus 24h data → Reduce(Mean)
  → Stat panel showing "Avg: 44.3 req/s"

For anihpj: Query Prometheus for request rate over 24h → Reduce to "Mean" → display as a Stat panel showing average request rate for the day.

Dashboards Q105 of 200
What does the Merge transformation do?
A) It merges two dashboards into one — combining all their panels
B) It merges two Grafana instances for high availability — a clustering feature
C) It merges two data sources into a single connection for simplified configuration
D) It combines results from multiple queries (potentially from different data sources) into a single Data Frame. Essential for Mixed data source panels where you want to show Prometheus metrics and PostgreSQL data in one table or graph
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Merge transformation combines multiple query results (from same or different data sources) into one unified Data Frame. Modes: Merge (append rows), Outer join (preserve all), Inner join (matching only).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Merge — Combine Queries into One Data Frame
  MERGE TRANSFORMATION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Query A (Prometheus):     Query B (PostgreSQL):
  │  ┌─────────────────┐      ┌─────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Endpoint  Errors │      │ Endpoint  Total  │ │
  │  │ /api/jobs   23  │      │ /api/jobs  1500 │ │
  │  │ /api/users   5  │      │ /api/users  800 │ │
  │  └─────────────────┘      └─────────────────┘ │
  │              ↓ MERGE ↓                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ Endpoint  Errors  Total  ErrorRate   │     │
  │  │ /api/jobs   23    1500    1.53%     │     │
  │  │ /api/users   5     800    0.63%     │     │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                              │
  │  Now one Table shows both sources together!  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Merge Prometheus errors with PostgreSQL totals → one table per endpoint with error count, request count, and calculated error rate.

Dashboards Q106 of 200
How do Filter transformations work in Grafana?
A) Filter by values removes rows where a field's value doesn't meet a condition (>, <, =, contains, regex). Filter by name includes/excludes series by name pattern (regex or glob). Both act BEFORE the panel renderer sees the data
B) Filter transformations modify the database query — they add WHERE clauses to SQL
C) Filters only work with the Table panel — other panels can't use filter transformations
D) Filters are global dashboard settings — they apply to all panels simultaneously
✅ Correct Answer: A

Filter by values removes rows based on field conditions (equals, contains, regex, is null, greater/less than). Filter by name includes/excludes entire series by name pattern (regex). These are client-side filters — data is filtered in Grafana, not in the data source.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Filter Transformations — Client-Side Data Filtering
  FILTER BY VALUES:                FILTER BY NAME:
  ┌────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────┐
  │ INPUT: All rows    │          │ INPUT: All series  │
  │ ┌──────────────┐   │          │ ┌──────────────┐   │
  │ │ 200 OK   1200│   │          │ │cpu_usage     │   │
  │ │ 404 NF     23│   │          │ │mem_usage     │   │
  │ │ 500 ERR    45│   │          │ │disk_usage    │   │
  │ │ 502 GW      8│   │          │ │net_io        │   │
  │ └──────────────┘   │          │ └──────────────┘   │
  │ FILTER: code >= 500│          │ KEEP: /.*usage$/   │
  │        ↓           │          │        ↓           │
  │ ┌──────────────┐   │          │ ┌──────────────┐   │
  │ │ 500 ERR    45│   │          │ │cpu_usage     │   │
  │ │ 502 GW      8│   │          │ │mem_usage     │   │
  │ └──────────────┘   │          │ │disk_usage    │   │
  └────────────────────┘          └────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Query all HTTP status codes → Filter by values: code >= 500 → display only server errors in a focused table.

Dashboards Q107 of 200
What does the Group by transformation do?
A) It groups dashboard panels into rows for better organization
B) It groups rows by one or more field values and applies aggregation (sum, avg, min, max, count) to the grouped rows — similar to SQL GROUP BY. Use it to aggregate per-URL error counts into per-service totals
C) It groups users into teams for RBAC permission management
D) Group by only works with Prometheus data — other data sources aren't supported
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Group by transformation groups rows sharing the same field value(s) and calculates aggregations (Sum, Average, Min, Max, Count, First, Last) for each group — like SQL GROUP BY applied to Grafana Data Frames.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Group By — Aggregate Rows by Field
  GROUP BY TRANSFORMATION: (like SQL GROUP BY)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  INPUT: Per-URL errors                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────┐        │
  │  │ URL              Service   Errors│        │
  │  │ /api/jobs/list   jobs       12  │        │
  │  │ /api/jobs/create jobs        8  │        │
  │  │ /api/users/me    users       3  │        │
  │  │ /api/users/all   users       2  │        │
  │  │ /api/apply       apply      15  │        │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────┘        │
  │  GROUP BY: Service | CALC: Sum(Errors)       │
  │              ↓                               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐              │
  │  │ Service   Errors (Sum)     │              │
  │  │ jobs      20               │              │
  │  │ users      5               │              │
  │  │ apply     15               │              │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘              │
  │  Now you see per-SERVICE totals!            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Per-URL error data → Group by "service" → Sum errors → total errors per microservice, not per individual URL.

Dashboards Q108 of 200
What does the Join by field transformation do?
A) It joins two Grafana instances into a federated multi-instance setup
B) It concatenates panel titles to create longer, more descriptive names
C) It performs an SQL-like JOIN between two query results based on a matching field. Use it to combine data from different data sources — e.g., join Prometheus error counts with PostgreSQL service names on the "service_id" field
D) Join requires Grafana Enterprise — it's a premium transformation feature
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Join by field transformation combines two Data Frames based on a shared field — like an SQL JOIN. Modes: Outer join (all rows from both), Inner join (matching only), Left join (all from left + matching from right).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Join By Field — Cross-Source Data Fusion
  JOIN BY FIELD: (like SQL JOIN)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Frame A (Prometheus):   Frame B (PostgreSQL):│
  │  ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐  │
  │  │ job      errors  │   │ job_name  owner  │  │
  │  │ api        23    │   │ api       Alice  │  │
  │  │ web         5    │   │ web       Bob    │  │
  │  │ worker     12    │   │ worker    Carol  │  │
  │  └──────────────────┘   └──────────────────┘  │
  │          JOIN ON job = job_name               │
  │              ↓                                │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ job      errors   owner               │    │
  │  │ api        23     Alice    ← on-call! │    │
  │  │ web         5     Bob                 │    │
  │  │ worker     12     Carol               │    │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │  Errors + ownership in ONE view!             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Join Prometheus error counts with PostgreSQL service ownership — one table shows errors AND who owns each service for instant escalation.

3.5 Variables & Dynamic DashboardsQ109-Q114

Dashboards Q109 of 200
When does Grafana refresh variable values?
A) Variables never refresh — they're static after the dashboard loads
B) Variables refresh every 5 seconds — this interval cannot be changed
C) Only when the user manually clicks the refresh button next to the variable dropdown
D) Three options: On dashboard load (refresh once when the dashboard opens), On time range change (re-query when the user changes the time picker), and On change (re-query only when the variable's PARENT variable changes). These control how often Grafana re-queries the data source for updated variable values
✅ Correct Answer: D

Variable refresh behavior: On dashboard load — queries once when dashboard opens. On time range change — re-queries when time picker changes. On change — only refreshes when a parent variable changes (for chained variables).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Variable Refresh Options — Control When Values Update
  VARIABLE REFRESH BEHAVIOR:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  "On dashboard load"                         │
  │  ─ Query data source ONCE when dashboard     │
  │    opens. Best for: static lists (regions,   │
  │    environments, service names)              │
  │                                              │
  │  "On time range change"                      │
  │  ─ Re-query when user changes time picker.   │
  │    Best for: metrics-dependent variables     │
  │    (list of pods that had traffic recently)  │
  │                                              │
  │  "On change" (chained/dependent variables)   │
  │  ─ Re-query only when PARENT variable       │
  │    changes. $service → $namespace → $pod     │
  │    Best for: cascading dropdown chains       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Set $service to "On dashboard load", $pod to "On change" (depends on $service).

Dashboards Q110 of 200
How do you enable users to select multiple values in a dropdown variable?
A) Multi-select is automatic — all dropdown variables support multiple values by default
B) In variable settings, check "Multi-value" to allow selecting multiple options. Also check "Include All option" to add an "All" choice. Use ${variable:csv} in queries to pass comma-separated values for SQL IN clauses
C) Hold Ctrl while clicking — multi-select is a browser feature, not a Grafana setting
D) Multi-value requires the variable type to be "Text box" — dropdowns can only select one
✅ Correct Answer: B

Enable Multi-value in variable settings to allow selecting multiple dropdown options. Add Include All option for a convenient "select everything." Format multi-value variables with ${variable:csv} (Prometheus), ${variable:sqlstring} (SQL IN), or ${variable:pipe} (Loki).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Value Variables — Select Multiple Options
  MULTI-VALUE SETTINGS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Variable: $service                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ ☑ anihpj-api    ☑ anihpj-web      │      │
  │  │ ☐ anihpj-worker ☑ All             │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                              │
  │  Query formatting:                           │
  │  Prometheus: ${service:csv} → "api,web"      │
  │              {job=~"${service:regex}"}        │
  │  SQL:        ${service:sqlstring}             │
  │              WHERE service IN ('api','web')  │
  │  Loki:       ${service:pipe} → "api|web"     │
  │              {job=~"${service:pipe}"}         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Multi-value $service lets users compare anihpj-api and anihpj-web side-by-side in one dashboard view.

Dashboards Q111 of 200
What is a Custom variable and when should you use it?
A) Custom variables are created through the Grafana API — not through the UI
B) Custom variables are JavaScript scripts that dynamically generate variable values
C) A Custom variable is a manually-defined list of values (comma-separated). Use it when the options are fixed and don't come from a database — e.g., environments: "production, staging, development" or cloud regions: "us-east-1, eu-west-1"
D) Custom variables require Grafana Enterprise — OSS only supports Query variables
✅ Correct Answer: C

Custom variables are manually entered lists separated by commas. They're perfect for fixed-choice values that don't exist in any data source: environments, regions, severity levels, time windows.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Custom Variables — Fixed-Value Lists
  CUSTOM VARIABLE: $environment
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Values (comma-separated):                   │
  │  production, staging, development            │
  │                                              │
  │  ──VS──                                      │
  │                                              │
  │  QUERY VARIABLE: $service                    │
  │  query = label_values(job)                   │
  │  → Dynamically fetches from Prometheus       │
  │                                              │
  │  USE CUSTOM WHEN:                            │
  │  ✅ Values are fixed (environments, regions) │
  │  ✅ Values don't exist in any data source    │
  │  ✅ Simple lists (severity: crit,warn,info)  │
  │  ❌ NOT for: dynamic service/pod lists       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Create Custom variable $severity: critical, warning, info to filter alert-related panels by severity level.

Dashboards Q112 of 200
How do you create a Query variable that lists all jobs from Prometheus?
A) Variable type: Query → Data source: Prometheus → Query: label_values(job). This returns all unique values of the "job" label. Use label_values(metric, label) to filter by a specific metric, or label_names() to list all label names
B) Query variables only work with SQL data sources — Prometheus must use Custom variables
C) Use SELECT DISTINCT job FROM metrics — Prometheus query variables use SQL syntax
D) Query variables from Prometheus require enabling the "experimental" flag in grafana.ini
✅ Correct Answer: A

Prometheus query variables use label_values() and label_names() functions: label_values(job) — all values of the job label. label_values(up{env="prod"}, job) — job values only where env=prod. label_names() — all label names.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Query Variables — Dynamic Values from Data Sources
  PROMETHEUS QUERY VARIABLES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  label_values(label)                         │
  │  → All unique values of a label              │
  │  label_values(job) → [api, web, worker, db]  │
  │                                              │
  │  label_values(metric{}, label)               │
  │  → Filter by metric first                    │
  │  label_values(up{env="prod"}, job)            │
  │  → Only jobs from production                 │
  │                                              │
  │  label_names()                               │
  │  → All label names (for Ad hoc filters)      │
  │  → [job, instance, env, app, ...]            │
  │                                              │
  │  💡 Query variables auto-update! New services │
  │     appear in dropdowns without editing.      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: label_values(django_http_requests_total, job) lists all instrumented Django services — auto-updating.

Dashboards Q113 of 200
What is a Constant variable and when would anihpj/jobpost use one?
A) Constants are read-only variables auto-generated by Grafana from the data source
B) A Constant variable has a fixed value that is NOT shown as a dropdown in the UI — it's hidden from users but available in all queries. Use it for values that should never change per-user: cloud region, account ID, cluster name, or Grafana instance name
C) Constants are variables that can only be set once when the dashboard is created — never changed
D) Constant variables require Grafana Enterprise — not available in OSS
✅ Correct Answer: B

Constant variables store fixed values that are invisible to dashboard viewers but available in every panel query. Use them for values that are the same for everyone: $cluster_name = "kubeadm-prod", $region = "us-east-1", $environment = "production". Consistent values across all panels without cluttering the UI.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Constant Variables — Hidden, Fixed Values
  CONSTANT vs CUSTOM vs QUERY VARIABLES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONSTANT (hidden):                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ $cluster = "prod-us-east"          │      │
  │  │ $app     = "jobpost"              │      │
  │  │ → NOT shown in UI dropdowns        │      │
  │  │ → Available in ALL queries         │      │
  │  │ → Users can't change them          │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                              │
  │  CUSTOM (visible dropdown):                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ $env: [production ▼]              │      │
  │  │ → Visible to users                 │      │
  │  │ → Users can change selection       │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                              │
  │  Best for: cluster name, region, account ID  │
  │  Every query shares these values seamlessly  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: $app = "jobpost" — every query uses it, no one needs to see or change it.

Dashboards Q114 of 200
What are Ad hoc filters and how do they work?
A) Ad hoc filters are temporary text notes you add to dashboards during incidents
B) Ad hoc filters are Grafana's term for alert silences — temporary alert muting
C) Ad hoc filters are dynamic key=value filters added to the dashboard toolbar. Users pick a label/key from a dropdown, then pick values — and Grafana applies the filter to ALL queries on the dashboard automatically. No query modification needed
D) Ad hoc filters require manual query editing — they're not automatic
✅ Correct Answer: C

Ad hoc filters add a dynamic filter bar to the dashboard. Users select a label (e.g., "pod") and value (e.g., "anihpj-api-7d8f9"), and Grafana automatically applies the filter to every query — adding {pod="anihpj-api-7d8f9"} to Prometheus queries, or equivalent WHERE clauses to SQL.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Ad Hoc Filters — Dynamic Dashboard-Wide Filtering
  AD HOC FILTERS (dashboard toolbar):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Dashboard: anihpj Overview                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ Filter: [pod ▼] = [anihpj-api-7d8 ▼]│ +  │
  │  │ Filter: [env ▼]  = [production  ▼]  │ +  │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                              │
  │  ALL panels automatically filtered!          │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐     │
  │  │ Requests │ │ Errors   │ │ Latency  │     │
  │  │ (filtered)│ │ (filtered)│ │ (filtered)│     │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘     │
  │                                              │
  │  No query editing needed — Grafana injects  │
  │  filters into every query automatically     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

During an anihpj incident: add ad hoc filter for the misbehaving pod → instantly every panel shows only that pod's data.

3.6 Library Panels & Dashboard-as-CodeQ115-Q120

Dashboards Q115 of 200
What is a Library Panel in Grafana?
A) A reusable panel that can be shared across multiple dashboards. Edit the Library Panel once, and ALL dashboards using it update automatically. Perfect for consistent "footer" panels, company-branded headers, or standard monitoring panels used everywhere
B) A panel template downloaded from the Grafana plugin library on grafana.com
C) A read-only panel that only Admins can edit — viewers see it but can't modify it
D) Library Panels are panels stored in a Git repository for version control — the "library" is Git
✅ Correct Answer: A

Library Panels are shared, reusable panel definitions. Create once → save to Library → use on any dashboard. Edit the Library Panel → ALL referencing dashboards update automatically. Ensures consistency across dashboards.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Library Panels — Shared, Reusable Components
  LIBRARY PANEL WORKFLOW:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Create Panel → Save to Library → "Health"   │
  │                                              │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐     │
  │  │ Dashboard│ │ Dashboard│ │ Dashboard│     │
  │  │    1     │ │    2     │ │    3     │     │
  │  │  ┌────┐  │ │  ┌────┐  │ │  ┌────┐  │     │
  │  │  │Health│ │ │  │Health│ │ │  │Health│ │     │
  │  │  └──┬───┘ │ │  └──┬───┘ │ │  └──┬───┘ │     │
  │  └────┼──────┘ └────┼──────┘ └────┼──────┘     │
  │       └─────────────┼─────────────┘           │
  │                     ▼                          │
  │              LIBRARY (single source)           │
  │                                              │
  │  Update once → all dashboards get the change │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: "Health Status" Library Panel used on all team dashboards for consistency.

Dashboards Q116 of 200
What happens when you edit a Library Panel that's used on 5 dashboards?
A) Each dashboard gets its own independent copy — changes don't propagate
B) ALL 5 dashboards update automatically. Grafana shows a warning: "This Library Panel is used on 5 dashboards. Changes will affect all of them." You can also "Unlink" a Library Panel on a specific dashboard to make it independent
C) Only the dashboard where you initiated the edit updates — others stay unchanged
D) Library Panels are read-only after creation — you must delete and recreate to change them
✅ Correct Answer: B

Editing a Library Panel updates ALL dashboards using it — that's the power (and responsibility) of Library Panels. Grafana warns you with a count of affected dashboards before saving. If you need a dashboard-specific variation, use Unlink — the panel becomes an independent copy.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Library Panel Edit — Cascade vs Unlink
  EDITING A LIBRARY PANEL:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ⚠️ "This Library Panel is used on 5         │
  │      dashboards. Changes will affect all."   │
  │                                              │
  │  SAVE → Updates all 5 dashboards             │
  │  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐    │
  │  │ D1 ✓│ │ D2 ✓│ │ D3 ✓│ │ D4 ✓│ │ D5 ✓│    │
  │  └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘    │
  │                                              │
  │  UNLINK (on a specific dashboard):           │
  │  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐    │
  │  │ D1 ✓│ │ D2 ✓│ │ D3 ✓│ │ D4 ✗│ │ D5 ✓│    │
  │  └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘ └──┬──┘ └─────┘    │
  │                             │                │
  │                    Independent copy          │
  │                    (no longer linked)        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Update the "anihpj Footer" Library Panel when on-call changes → all 10 dashboards reflect new contact instantly. Unlink for one-off experimental dashboards.

Dashboards Q117 of 200
How does dashboard provisioning work with Kubernetes ConfigMaps?

B) Dashboards are embedded in the Grafana Docker image — they can't be provisioned externally
C) Store dashboard JSON in a Kubernetes ConfigMap, mount it as a volume at /etc/grafana/provisioning/dashboards/ in the Grafana pod. Configure a dashboard provider YAML that tells Grafana to watch that path. Grafana auto-imports all dashboards from the ConfigMap
D) Use kubectl create dashboard — Grafana has native Kubernetes CRDs for dashboards

Dashboards Q118 of 200
What are __inputs and __requires in a dashboard JSON?
A) __inputs define variables that the importer must fill in (usually data source mappings). __requires lists required Grafana version, panel plugins, and data source types. Both are used during import to ensure the dashboard works in the target environment
B) __inputs are the user's text entries; __requires are Grafana's system dependencies
C) They're deprecated fields replaced by the Variables system in Grafana 10
D) __inputs are for Prometheus; __requires are for Loki — data source-specific fields
✅ Correct Answer: A

When you export a dashboard, Grafana adds __inputs (data source placeholders) and __requires (minimum Grafana version, required plugins). During import, the user maps each __input to an actual data source in their instance.

📝 Detailed Explanation
__inputs and __requires — Dashboard Portability
  DASHBOARD JSON (exported):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  {                                           │
  │    "__inputs": [                             │
  │      {                                       │
  │        "name": "DS_PROMETHEUS",              │
  │        "label": "Prometheus",                │
  │        "type": "datasource"                  │
  │      }                                       │
  │    ],                                        │
  │    "__requires": [                           │
  │      { "type": "grafana", "version": ">=9" },│
  │      { "type": "panel", "id": "timeseries" },│
  │      { "type": "datasource",                 │
  │        "id": "prometheus" }                  │
  │    ]                                         │
  │  }                                           │
  │                                              │
  │  Import prompt: Map DS_PROMETHEUS →          │
  │  your actual Prometheus data source name     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: When sharing dashboards, __inputs lets anyone import them regardless of their data source naming convention.

Dashboards Q119 of 200
Why do Grafana dashboards use data source UIDs instead of names?
A) UIDs are shorter than names — it's a performance optimization for dashboard loading
B) UIDs and names are interchangeable — there's no practical difference
C) Only Prometheus supports UIDs; other data sources must use names
D) UIDs are globally unique and STABLE — a data source can be renamed without breaking every dashboard that references it. Names can change, UIDs cannot. This is why provisioning files use UIDs: they survive renames
✅ Correct Answer: D

UIDs (unique identifiers) are assigned when a data source is created and never change. Names can be changed anytime by an admin. If dashboards referenced data sources by name, renaming "prometheus-prod" to "prometheus-production" would break every dashboard. UIDs solve this.

📝 Detailed Explanation
UIDs vs Names — Stability Matters
  DATA SOURCE REFERENCING:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BY NAME (fragile):                          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ Dashboard: "use prometheus-prod"   │      │
  │  │ Admin renames → "prometheus-main"  │      │
  │  │ Dashboard: ❌ BROKEN!              │      │
  │  │ "Data source not found"           │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                              │
  │  BY UID (stable):                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ Dashboard: "use uid: P8D3F7A2"    │      │
  │  │ Admin renames → "prometheus-main"  │      │
  │  │ Dashboard: ✅ STILL WORKS!          │      │
  │  │ Grafana resolves UID → finds DS    │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                              │
  │  UID = permanent, generated at creation      │
  │  Name = mutable, can change anytime          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Always use UIDs in provisioned dashboards — they survive any renaming in the Grafana UI.

Dashboards Q120 of 200
Which of these is a Grafana dashboard best practice?

C) Use 20+ different colors per graph — more colors make dashboards more informative
D) Avoid variables — create separate dashboards for each environment for better isolation

Grafana dashboard best practices: One question per row, F-pattern layout (KPIs top-left), Consistent colors (green=healthy, red=problem), Descriptions on every panel, Variables for multi-environment, 5-7 panels max per row, Thresholds for visual severity.

  F-PATTERN LAYOUT (eyes scan left→right, top→bottom):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │  ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐        │
  │  │🟢247 │ │🟡2.3%│ │🟢99.9│ │🟢45ms│        │

  │ ╱  ╲__╱  ╲__╱  ╲___                         │
  │                                              │

  │  │ Recent Errors │ Count │ Time     │        │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────┘        │
  │                                              │
  │  5 RULES: 1) KPIs first  2) Consistent color │

🔔 Part 1 — Category 4: Alerting (15%)

Moving from "looking at dashboards" to "Grafana tells YOU when something is wrong." Alerting closes the loop — it watches your data 24/7 and notifies the right person when thresholds are crossed. 30 questions covering alert rules, contact points, notification policies, and silences.

30Questions
15%Exam Weight
4Sub-Topics

🔔 Category 4: Alerting (15%)Q121-Q150

🟡 Intermediate: When Grafana Watches So You Don't Have To

Alerting closes the observability loop. Dashboards are reactive (you look at them), alerts are proactive (they tell YOU). For anihpj/jobpost, this means: if error rate > 5%, Grafana sends a Slack message to #on-call and pages the SRE via PagerDuty. Alerts turn "I noticed the problem when users complained" into "I knew about the problem before users noticed."

Q121-Q150 30 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

4.1 Alerting Architecture & ConceptsQ121-Q126

Alerting Q121 of 200
What is the difference between Grafana-managed alerts and Data source-managed alerts?
A) They're the same — "Grafana-managed" and "data source-managed" are different names for the same feature
B) Grafana-managed alerts use Grafana's database; data source-managed alerts use etcd
C) Grafana-managed alerts are evaluated by Grafana's internal alerting engine (supports ANY data source, multi-dimensional alerting). Data source-managed alerts are evaluated inside the data source itself (e.g., Prometheus Alertmanager rules, Loki ruler) — Grafana only displays and routes them
D) Grafana-managed alerts work with dashboards; data source-managed alerts only work with Explore
✅ Correct Answer: C

Grafana-managed alerts: Grafana's own engine evaluates conditions by querying data sources on a schedule. Works with ANY data source. Supports multi-dimensional alerting. Data source-managed alerts: The data source evaluates internally (e.g., Prometheus Alertmanager). Grafana displays them but doesn't evaluate.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Grafana-Managed vs Data Source-Managed Alerts
  ALERT TYPES COMPARISON:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GRAFANA-MANAGED:                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ Grafana evaluates (scheduled query)│      │
  │  │ → ANY data source (PG, Loki, etc) │      │
  │  │ → Multi-dimensional (per-label)    │      │
  │  │ → Grafana sends notifications      │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                              │
  │  DATA SOURCE-MANAGED:                        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ Data source evaluates internally   │      │
  │  │ → Only that DS's native format     │      │
  │  │ → Prometheus rules, Loki rules     │      │
  │  │ → Grafana DISPLAYS, doesn't eval   │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                              │
  │  Unified Alerting page shows BOTH types!    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Grafana-managed for PG business alerts, data source-managed for existing Prometheus Alertmanager rules.

Alerting Q122 of 200
What is multi-dimensional alerting in Grafana?
A) Alerts that trigger on multiple metrics simultaneously — a compound alert condition
B) One alert rule can generate MULTIPLE alert instances — one per unique label combination. If rate(errors{job="api"}[5m]) > 5 matches 3 pods, you get 3 separate alert instances (one per pod), each tracked and notified independently
C) Alerts that span multiple Grafana organizations — cross-org alerting
D) Multi-dimensional alerting requires Grafana Enterprise — OSS only supports single alerts
✅ Correct Answer: B

Multi-dimensional alerting: One alert rule generates MULTIPLE alert instances — one per unique label combination. If rate(errors{job="api"}[5m]) > 5 matches 3 pods, you get 3 separate alert instances, each tracked and notified independently.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Dimensional Alerting — One Rule, Many Instances
  SINGLE ALERT RULE → MULTIPLE INSTANCES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Rule: rate(errors{job="api"}[5m]) > 5       │
  │                                              │
  │  Matches:                                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ {pod="api-1"} → Instance #1 → 🔴   │      │
  │  │ {pod="api-2"} → Instance #2 → 🟢   │      │
  │  │ {pod="api-3"} → Instance #3 → 🔴   │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                              │
  │  Each instance:                              │
  │  ✅ Independent state (Normal/Pending/Fire)  │
  │  ✅ Independent notification                 │
  │  ✅ Resolves independently                   │
  │                                              │
  │  Benefit: Only the broken pod alerts,        │
  │  not the entire service!                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: If pod api-1 has high errors but api-2 is fine, only api-1 fires — not a blanket "API is down" page.

Alerting Q123 of 200
What are Alert Evaluation Groups in Grafana?
A) Evaluation groups organize alert rules for notification routing. Alerts in the same group share evaluation timing and can be batched into a single notification. Think of them as "these alerts are related — notify about them together"
B) Evaluation groups are Grafana Teams — they group users who receive alert notifications
C) Evaluation groups control which data source an alert queries — they're data source selectors
D) Evaluation groups only exist in Grafana Enterprise — OSS doesn't support alert grouping

Evaluation groups organize alert rules and control notification grouping. Rules in the same group share evaluation timing and firing alerts can be batched into a single notification — instead of 10 separate Slack messages, you get one message listing all 10.

Evaluation Groups — Batch Alerts & Notifications
  EVALUATION GROUPS (organize + batch):

  │  ├── Alert: High Error Rate (>5%)            │

Alerting Q124 of 200
How often does Grafana evaluate alert rules?

B) Once per minute — this interval is fixed and cannot be changed
C) Configurable per evaluation group — set the evaluation interval (e.g., every 1m, 5m, or 15m) in the evaluation group settings. Shorter intervals = faster detection but more data source load
D) Alerts are evaluated by the data source, not Grafana — the interval is out of Grafana's control

Alerting Q125 of 200
What are the five alert states in Grafana's lifecycle?
A) Active, Inactive, Acknowledged, Resolved, Closed — like an incident ticket system
B) Green, Yellow, Orange, Red, Black — tiered severity levels
C) Created, Evaluating, Triggered, Notified, Archived — the alert processing pipeline
D) Normal (condition not met), Pending (condition met, waiting "For" duration), Firing (condition met for >= "For" duration — notification sent), NoData (data source returned no data), Error (evaluation failed)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Grafana's alert state machine has 5 states: Normal (condition false), Pending (true but within "For" window), Firing (true for >= "For" — notification sent), NoData (no data returned), Error (evaluation failed).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Alert State Machine — 5 States
  ALERT LIFECYCLE (state transitions):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                              │
  │  Normal ──condition true──▶ Pending          │
  │    🟢                          🟡             │
  │    ▲                           │             │
  │    │ condition false           │ "For" time  │
  │    │ (resolved)                │ elapsed     │
  │    │                           ▼             │
  │    └─────────────────────── Firing           │
  │                               🔴             │
  │                               │              │
  │                    ┌──────────┼──────────┐   │
  │                    ▼          ▼          ▼   │
  │                 NoData     Error    Resolved │
  │                  ⬜         ❌         🟢     │
  │                                              │
  │  "For" duration = debounce: prevents        │
  │  momentary spikes from firing alerts         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Set "For: 3m" on error rate alert — transient spikes during deployments don't wake anyone up.

4.2 Creating Alert RulesQ127-Q134

Alerting Q127 of 200
How do you create an alert rule from an existing dashboard panel?
A) Alerts must be created from scratch in the Alerting section — dashboard panels can't create alerts
B) Edit the panel → Alert tab → "Create alert rule from this panel." Grafana pre-fills the query from your panel and you set the condition (Reduce + Threshold expressions). The alert rule appears in the Alerting section alongside the dashboard
C) Right-click the panel → "Alert on this" — it's a context menu option in the dashboard view
D) Export the panel JSON and import it into the Alerting section — alerts and panels are separate
✅ Correct Answer: B

Creating an alert from a panel: Edit panel → Alert tab → "Create alert rule from this panel." Grafana copies the query. Configure: Reduce (aggregate result), Threshold (IS ABOVE/BELOW), evaluation interval, "For" duration. The rule appears in Alerting → Alert rules.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Alert Rule Creation — Panel → Alert Workflow
  CREATE ALERT FROM PANEL:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Panel Edit → Alert tab → Create alert rule  │
  │                                              │
  │  Query: rate(errors[5m])                     │
  │     ↓                                        │
  │  Reduce: Avg (aggregate time series)         │
  │     ↓                                        │
  │  Math: $A / $B  (optional — error ratio)     │
  │     ↓                                        │
  │  Threshold: IS ABOVE 5                       │
  │     ↓                                        │
  │  For: 3m (debounce period)                   │
  │     ↓                                        │
  │  Evaluation group: "anihpj Critical" (1m)    │
  │                                              │
  │  Result: Alert fires when avg error rate     │
  │  > 5 for 3+ minutes → notification sent     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Edit "Error Rate" panel → Alert tab → rule fires when avg error rate IS ABOVE 5 for 3 minutes.

Alerting Q128 of 200
What alert conditions can you set in Grafana's Threshold expression?
A) IS ABOVE (value exceeds threshold), IS BELOW (value drops below threshold), IS OUTSIDE RANGE (value is outside a min-max band), and HAS NO VALUE (data source returned null/empty — detects missing data)
B) Only IS ABOVE and IS BELOW — Grafana doesn't support range or missing data conditions
C) IS EQUAL TO, IS NOT EQUAL TO, IS GREATER THAN, IS LESS THAN — four numeric comparisons
D) Grafana doesn't have built-in threshold expressions — you use PromQL comparison operators
✅ Correct Answer: A

Grafana's Threshold expression supports: IS ABOVE (value > threshold), IS BELOW (value < threshold), IS OUTSIDE RANGE (outside min-max band), HAS NO VALUE (null/empty — detects missing data).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Threshold Conditions — Four Comparison Types
  THRESHOLD EXPRESSIONS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  IS ABOVE (>):        IS BELOW (<):          │
  │  ┌────────────────┐   ┌────────────────┐     │
  │  │ 🔴 errors > 5  │   │ 🔴 jobs < 10   │     │
  │  │ ▄▄▄▄▄▄▄████    │   │ ████▄▄▄▄▄▄▄    │     │
  │  │         5────   │   │   ────10       │     │
  │  └────────────────┘   └────────────────┘     │
  │                                              │
  │  IS OUTSIDE RANGE:    HAS NO VALUE:          │
  │  ┌────────────────┐   ┌────────────────┐     │
  │  │ 🔴 outside     │   │ 🔴 no data     │     │
  │  │    10ms-500ms  │   │ returned from  │     │
  │  │ ╱╲    ╱╲       │   │ data source    │     │
  │  │╱  ╲──╱  ╲────  │   │ → metrics      │     │
  │  │ 10ms    500ms  │   │ stopped?!     │     │
  │  └────────────────┘   └────────────────┘     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: IS ABOVE for errors, IS BELOW for active jobs, HAS NO VALUE for Prometheus scrape failure detection.

Alerting Q129 of 200
What does the Reduce expression do in an alert rule?
A) It reduces the number of alerts to prevent notification fatigue
B) It compresses alert notification messages to fit within Slack's character limit
C) It aggregates the query result into a single value per series using functions like Avg, Min, Max, Sum, Count, Last — this reduced value is then compared against the threshold. Without Reduce, you can't compare a time series of values to a single threshold
D) Reduce expressions are only used for multi-dimensional alerts — single alerts don't need them
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Reduce expression converts a time series (many values over time) into a single value for threshold comparison. Functions: Avg, Min, Max, Sum, Count, Last, First, PercentDiff. Without Reduce, you can't compare a time series to a single threshold number.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Reduce Expression — Aggregate Time Series → Single Value
  REDUCE IN ALERT RULES (mandatory step):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Query returns TIME SERIES:                  │
  │  Time     Value                              │
  │  10:00    4.2                                │
  │  10:01    6.1                                │
  │  10:02    5.8                                │
  │  10:03    7.3                                │
  │  10:04    8.1                                │
  │                                              │
  │  REDUCE: Avg  →  6.3 (single number)         │
  │  REDUCE: Max  →  8.1                         │
  │  REDUCE: Min  →  4.2                         │
  │  REDUCE: Last →  8.1                         │
  │                                              │
  │  Threshold: IS ABOVE 5 → TRUE (6.3 > 5)     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Query returns error rates over 5m → Reduce: Avg → 6.3 → Threshold: IS ABOVE 5 → alert fires.

Alerting Q130 of 200
What does the "For" duration do in a Grafana alert rule?
A) It sets how long the notification message will be — longer "For" = more detailed message
B) It determines how long an alert stays in the Firing state before auto-resolving
C) It controls how long Grafana waits before re-sending the same alert notification
D) It's the "debounce" period — the condition must be continuously true for this duration before the alert transitions from Pending to Firing (and sends a notification). Prevents alert flapping from momentary spikes
✅ Correct Answer: D

The "For" duration is the debounce period — the condition must be continuously true for this duration before the alert transitions from Pending to Firing and sends a notification. Prevents alert flapping from momentary spikes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
"For" Duration — Alert Debounce Mechanism
  "FOR" DURATION IN ACTION:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Error rate:                                 │
  │  10%┤     ╱╲                                 │
  │   5%┤────╱──╲────────────────────────────── │
  │   0%┤───╱    ╲─────────────────────────────  │
  │      │   │    │                              │
  │      │ Pending│ (5s spike — resolves before  │
  │      │        │  "For: 3m" elapsed)          │
  │      │        │                              │
  │      │ Pending ──────── Firing               │
  │      │ (3+ minute sustained spike)           │
  │                                              │
  │  Without "For": momentary spikes fire alerts │
  │  With "For: 3m": only sustained issues alert │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: "For: 3m" on error rate alert — a 10-second deployment spike won't trigger, but 3+ minutes of sustained errors will.

Alerting Q131 of 200
When would you use a Math expression in a Grafana alert rule?
A) Math expressions are for formatting notification messages with calculations
B) When you need to combine results from multiple queries — e.g., "errors / total_requests > 0.05" (error rate as a ratio). Math expressions can reference other queries by their RefID ($A, $B, $C) and perform arithmetic operations
C) Math expressions replace the Threshold expression — you use one or the other, not both
D) Math expressions are only available in Grafana Enterprise for complex alerting logic
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Math expression combines multiple queries using arithmetic: $A / $B (error rate = errors ÷ total), ($A + $B) / $C, $A > $B. Reference queries by RefID (A, B, C).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Math Expression — Combine Multiple Queries
  MATH EXPRESSION (combine query results):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Query A: rate(errors[5m])     →  6.3        │
  │  Query B: rate(requests[5m])  →  120.0      │
  │                                              │
  │  Math: $A / $B                              │
  │     = 6.3 / 120.0                           │
  │     = 0.0525 (5.25% error rate)              │
  │                                              │
  │  Threshold: IS ABOVE 0.05                   │
  │     → 0.0525 > 0.05 → TRUE → ALERT FIRES!   │
  │                                              │
  │  Other examples:                             │
  │  ($A + $B) / $C  → combined metrics          │
  │  $A > $B          → compare two metrics      │
  │  $A * 100          → scale to percentage     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Query A = errors, Query B = requests → Math: $A / $B → Threshold: IS ABOVE 0.05 → 5% error rate alert.

Alerting Q132 of 200
What replaced Classic Conditions in Grafana's Unified Alerting?
A) Nothing — Classic Conditions are still the recommended way to create alerts
B) Alert rules are now created directly in Prometheus Alertmanager — no Grafana equivalent
C) Classic Conditions were replaced by Webhook notifications — alerts are now event-driven
D) Reduce + Threshold + Math expressions replaced the single Classic Condition. The new system is more flexible: you chain expressions (Query → Reduce → Math → Threshold) to build complex alert logic from simple building blocks
✅ Correct Answer: D

Grafana's Unified Alerting replaced Classic Conditions with a flexible expression pipeline: Query → Reduce → Math (optional) → Threshold. Each step is a separate expression you can mix and match. More control than the old single Classic Condition.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Classic Conditions → Unified Alerting Pipeline
  OLD (Classic Condition):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Query + Condition (single combined step)    │
  │  → Limited to simple "avg > X" checks       │
  │  → No multi-query, no math expressions       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  NEW (Unified Alerting Pipeline):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌────────┐   ┌────────┐   ┌──────┐  ┌─────┐ │
  │  │ Query  │ → │ Reduce │ → │ Math │→│Thresh│ │
  │  │ A: err │   │ Avg    │   │$A/$B │  │>0.05│ │
  │  │ B: req │   │        │   │      │  │     │ │
  │  └────────┘   └────────┘   └──────┘  └─────┘ │
  │                                              │
  │  Benefits:                                   │
  │  ✅ Chainable building blocks                │
  │  ✅ Multi-query support                      │
  │  ✅ Math operations between queries          │
  │  ✅ Same pipeline for all data sources       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: New pipeline combines Prometheus errors + PostgreSQL thresholds in one alert — impossible before.

Alerting Q133 of 200
How does alert evaluation with "Evaluate every 1m for 3m" work?
A) Every 1 minute, Grafana queries the data source and evaluates the condition. If the condition is met, the alert enters Pending. After 3 consecutive evaluations where the condition is true, the alert transitions to Firing and a notification is sent. If at any point the condition becomes false, the counter resets
B) Grafana evaluates once every 3 minutes and waits 1 minute before checking again — the numbers are swapped
C) "Evaluate every 1m" sets the dashboard refresh rate; "for 3m" sets the notification delay
D) The 1m and 3m refer to data time ranges — query last 1m of data, wait 3m before alerting
✅ Correct Answer: A

Evaluation cycle: Every N minutes, Grafana runs the query. If condition is true for "For" duration of consecutive evaluations → Fire. If any evaluation is false, counter resets. This prevents alerts from transient spikes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Alert Evaluation Cycle — Consecutive Checks
  EVAL EVERY 1m, FOR 3m (needs 3 consecutive TRUEs):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Eval 1 (10:00): TRUE  → Pending (1 of 3)    │
  │  Eval 2 (10:01): TRUE  → Pending (2 of 3)    │
  │  Eval 3 (10:02): TRUE  → FIRING! 🔴          │
  │                                              │
  │  Vs:                                         │
  │  Eval 1 (10:00): FALSE → Normal              │
  │  Eval 2 (10:01): TRUE  → Pending (1 of 3)    │
  │  Eval 3 (10:02): FALSE → Counter RESET      │
  │  → Back to Normal, start over                │
  │                                              │
  │  Any FALSE in the "For" window = RESET      │
  │  Only sustained TRUEs trigger alerts         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: "Evaluate every 1m for 3m" — notified within 3-4 min of sustained problem, ignores blips.

Alerting Q134 of 200
What happens when an alert rule's data source returns no data?
A) Grafana silently ignores it — no action is taken for missing data
B) The alert immediately fires — missing data is treated as a critical condition
C) The alert enters NoData state, and you can configure what happens: keep last state, fire an alert (OK → Alerting), or treat as OK. This is critical for detecting when metrics STOP flowing — the absence of data is itself an alertable condition
D) Grafana retries the query 3 times before giving up and marking the alert as Error
✅ Correct Answer: C

When no data: alert enters NoData state. Configure behavior: Keep Last State, Alerting (fire — "metrics stopped!"), OK (resolve), NoData (info only). Critical for detecting when metrics stop flowing.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NoData State — When Metrics Stop Flowing
  NODATA CONFIGURATION OPTIONS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Keep Last State:                            │
  │  🔴→🔴  Alert was firing, stays firing       │
  │  🟢→🟢  Alert was OK, stays OK               │
  │  → Suppress nuisance alerts, status quo      │
  │                                              │
  │  Alerting:                                   │
  │  🟢→🔴  "Metrics stopped — investigate!"     │
  │  → Catch Prometheus scrape failures          │
  │  → Use for critical monitoring metrics       │
  │                                              │
  │  OK:                                         │
  │  🔴→🟢  "No errors = resolved"               │
  │  → Use for optional business metrics         │
  │                                              │
  │  NoData (info only):                         │
  │  ⬜  State stays NoData, no notification     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: NoData=Alerting for Prometheus scrape alert. NoData=OK for optional business metrics.

Alerting Q137 of 200
How does Grafana integrate with PagerDuty for on-call alerting?
A) Grafana can only send emails to PagerDuty — direct integration requires Enterprise
B) Grafana manages on-call schedules internally — PagerDuty is not supported as a notifier
C) Create a PagerDuty Contact Point → Integration Key (from PagerDuty service) → Severity mapping (critical/warning/info). When an alert fires, Grafana sends it to PagerDuty which routes it to the on-call person based on escalation policies
D) Use a Webhook contact point with PagerDuty's Events API v2 — there's no built-in PagerDuty support
✅ Correct Answer: C

PagerDuty is built-in. Configure with Integration Key (from PagerDuty service → Events API v2). Set Severity, Class, Component, Group. Grafana sends deduplication keys for auto-resolve.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PagerDuty Contact Point — On-Call Escalation
  GRAFANA → PAGERDUTY FLOW:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. PagerDuty: Create Service → Integrations │
  │     → Events API v2 → Copy Integration Key   │
  │                                              │
  │  2. Grafana: Contact Point → PagerDuty       │
  │     ┌──────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │     │ Integration Key: abc123def456    │     │
  │     │ Severity: critical               │     │
  │     │ Class: latency                   │     │
  │     │ Component: anihpj-api            │     │
  │     └──────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                              │
  │  3. Alert fires → PagerDuty receives →      │
  │     Routes per escalation policy →          │
  │     Phone call/SMS/Push to on-call engineer │
  │                                              │
  │  Dedup key: same alert → same incident,     │
  │  auto-resolves when Grafana alert resolves  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: PagerDuty for critical alerts only (error rate > 10%, DB down). Don't wake people for warnings.

Alerting Q138 of 200
What are Notification Policies and how do they route alerts?
A) Notification Policies are Grafana Teams that determine which users see which alerts
B) They're simple on/off switches — a Contact Point is either enabled or disabled for all alerts
C) Notification Policies are time-based rules — they control when alerts are sent (business hours only)
D) Notification Policies are a routing tree that matches alert labels to Contact Points. Root policy catches everything → child policies match specific labels (severity=critical → PagerDuty, team=platform → Slack #platform). Alerts cascade down the tree until they match a policy or hit the default
✅ Correct Answer: D

Notification Policies form a routing tree. Root policy is the default. Child policies match alert labels (=, !=, =~, !~) and route to different Contact Points. Alerts cascade until they match or hit default.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Notification Policy Routing Tree
  NOTIFICATION POLICY ROUTING TREE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ROOT POLICY (Default)                       │
  │  → Contact Point: Email (team@anihpj.com)    │
  │  ├─ CHILD: severity = critical               │
  │  │   → Contact Point: PagerDuty              │
  │  │   ├─ CHILD: team = platform               │
  │  │   │   → Slack #platform-critical          │
  │  │   └─ CHILD: team = data                   │
  │  │       → Slack #data-critical              │
  │  ├─ CHILD: severity = warning                │
  │  │   → Contact Point: Slack #on-call         │
  │  └─ CHILD: severity = info                   │
  │      → Contact Point: (none — silent)         │
  │                                              │
  │  Alert flow:                                 │
  │  {severity=critical, team=platform}          │
  │  → Hits root → matches "critical" child     │
  │  → matches "platform" child → Slack!        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Root→Email, Child(severity=critical)→PagerDuty, Child(team=platform)→Slack.

Alerting Q140 of 200
How often does Grafana re-send notifications for an alert that's still firing?
A) Configurable per notification policy — "Repeat every" setting (default: none). Set to 4h to re-notify every 4 hours that the alert is still firing. Set to 0 or leave empty to notify only once when the alert first fires
B) Every 5 minutes — Grafana re-notifies continuously until the alert is resolved
C) Only once — Grafana sends one notification per alert firing and never repeats
D) On every evaluation interval — if evaluating every 1m, you get a notification every 1m
✅ Correct Answer: A

The "Repeat every" setting controls re-notification. Default: notify only once when firing. Set to "4h" to re-send every 4 hours. Set empty/0 to notify once only. Prevents alert fatigue.

  REPEAT INTERVAL SETTINGS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Repeat = empty (default):                   │
  │  🔴 FIRE → 🔕 (one notification, then quiet) │
  │                                              │
  │  Repeat = "4h":                              │
  │  🔴 FIRE → 🔕4h→ 🔴 REMIND → 🔕4h→ 🔴 REMIND│

  │  Use cases:                                  │

  │  │                                    │      │

  │  │   Daily reminder for non-urgent    │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Warning alerts repeat every 4h. Critical PagerDuty: no repeat.

Alerting Q142 of 200
How does Grafana group multiple alerts into a single notification?
A) Grafana never groups alerts — each alert always sends its own separate notification
B) Alerts are automatically grouped by data source — all Prometheus alerts together, all SQL together
C) Grouping is done by the notification service (Slack/PagerDuty) — Grafana just sends individual alerts
D) Alerts in the same Evaluation Group + Notification Policy are batched. Grafana waits a configurable "Group wait" period, collects all firing alerts in that group, and sends ONE notification with all alerts listed — instead of 10 separate Slack messages
✅ Correct Answer: D

Evaluation Groups + Group wait batch alerts. Alerts in same evaluation group are collected over "Group wait" (default 30s). If multiple fire in that window → ONE notification listing all. Prevents notification storms.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Alert Grouping — One Notification, Many Alerts
  WITHOUT GROUPING:                  WITH GROUPING:
  ┌────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────┐
  │ 🔴 API-1 errors   │            │ 🔴 anihpj API Group │
  │ 🔴 API-2 errors   │            │ ├ API-1: 7.2% (>5) │
  │ 🔴 API-3 errors   │            │ ├ API-2: 6.8% (>5) │
  │ = 3 Slack messages │            │ └ API-3: 8.1% (>5) │
  └────────────────────┘            │ = 1 Slack message  │
                                    └────────────────────┘

  Group wait (30s): Grafana collects all firing
  alerts in the group, then sends ONE notification
  containing all of them.

For anihpj: Group all API alerts together — 3 failing pods = 1 Slack message, not 3.

4.4 Alert Management & MaintenanceQ143-Q150

Alerting Q143 of 200
What are Silences in Grafana alerting and when should you use them?
A) Silences permanently delete alert rules — use them to retire obsolete alerts
B) Silences temporarily suppress alert notifications for a specified duration. Use them during planned maintenance, deployments, or known issues when you don't want alerts firing. After the silence expires, alerting resumes normally
C) Silences mute specific users so they stop receiving alerts — other team members still get them
D) Silences are only available in Grafana Enterprise — OSS doesn't support alert suppression
✅ Correct Answer: B

Silences temporarily suppress notifications based on label matchers. Use during maintenance, deployments, or known issues. Set duration + comment. After expiry, alerting resumes normally.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Silences — Temporary Alert Suppression
  SILENCE LIFECYCLE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Before silence:                             │
  │  🔴 Alert FIRING → Notification SENT         │
  │                                              │
  │  During silence (e.g., 30m maintenance):     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ Match: environment=production      │      │
  │  │ Duration: 30 minutes               │      │
  │  │ Comment: "DB migration v2.4.0"     │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │  🔴 Alert STILL FIRING → 🔕 No notification  │
  │                                              │
  │  After silence expires:                      │
  │  🔴 Alert STILL FIRING → Notification SENT   │
  │  🟢 Alert RESOLVED → No notification needed  │
  │                                              │
  │  Silences suppress NOTIFICATIONS, not alerts │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: 30-min silence during DB migration — avoid false alarms while system is intentionally disrupted.

Alerting Q145 of 200
What is the difference between alert Labels and Annotations?
A) Labels are for the UI; Annotations are for notifications — they contain the same data
B) Labels are auto-generated; Annotations are manually added — you can't set labels
C) Labels identify and route alerts (used by Notification Policies to match "severity=critical" → PagerDuty). Annotations provide additional context (runbook URLs, descriptions, dashboard links) displayed in notifications but NOT used for routing
D) Labels and Annotations are the same feature — they were renamed in Grafana 10
✅ Correct Answer: C

Labels IDENTIFY and ROUTE alerts (severity=critical → PagerDuty). Annotations provide CONTEXT (runbook URL, description, dashboard link) displayed in notifications but NOT used for routing.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Labels vs Annotations — Route vs Context
  LABELS (route alerts):           ANNOTATIONS (add context):
  ┌────────────────────────┐      ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ severity = critical    │      │ runbook_url = wiki/... │
  │ team = platform        │      │ description = "Error   │
  │ env = production       │      │   rate > 5% for 3m"   │
  │                        │      │ dashboard_url = /d/... │
  │ Used by:               │      │                        │
  │ Notification Policies  │      │ Shown in:              │
  │ → Route to PagerDuty   │      │ Notification messages  │
  │ → Match rules          │      │ Alert detail page      │
  └────────────────────────┘      └────────────────────────┘

  Labels = WHERE should this go?   Annotations = WHAT should I say?

For anihpj: Labels=severity+team (routing). Annotations=runbook URL+dashboard link (notification content).

Alerting Q147 of 200
What happens to alert rules that reference a deleted dashboard or data source?
A) They're automatically deleted — Grafana cascades the deletion to dependent alert rules
B) They continue working normally — alert rules are independent of dashboard/data source lifecycle
C) They silently stop evaluating — no alert is fired and no error is shown
D) Alerts referencing deleted data sources enter the Error state. Alerts created from deleted dashboards continue to work (they're independent copies). You can pause or delete alert rules individually in Alerting → Alert Rules

  │        │                      │              │
  │    Delete dashboard     Alert still fires!   │
  │                                              │
  │  States:                                     │
  │  🟢 Active (evaluating)                      │

  │  ❌ Deleted (permanently removed)            │
  │                                              │
