Alerting Q149 of 200
What are Recording Rules in Prometheus and how do they help Grafana alerting?
A) Recording Rules are Grafana's feature for recording alert state history to a database
B) They're rules that record which user created each alert — for audit compliance purposes
C) Recording Rules pre-compute expensive PromQL queries and save the results as new time series. Instead of computing rate(errors[5m]) on every alert evaluation, pre-compute it every 1m as job:error_rate:5m and alert on that — faster evaluation, less Prometheus load
D) Recording Rules are Grafana's version of audit logs — they record who did what in the UI
✅ Correct Answer: C

Recording Rules pre-compute expensive PromQL queries as new time series. E.g., pre-compute rate(errors[5m]) as job:error_rate:5m. Alert on pre-computed metric → faster evaluation, less Prometheus load.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Recording Rules — Pre-Compute for Speed
  WITHOUT RECORDING RULES:          WITH RECORDING RULES:
  ┌────────────────────────┐       ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ Alert eval every 1m:   │       │ Record eval every 1m:  │
  │ compute rate(err[5m])  │       │ compute rate(err[5m])  │
  │ → scan 5m of raw data  │       │ → store as new metric  │
  │ → 300 data points      │       │                        │
  │ → heavy PromQL query   │       │ Alert eval:            │
  │ → 50ms per evaluation  │       │ read pre-computed value│
  └────────────────────────┘       │ → 1 data point         │
                                    │ → instant read         │
                                    │ → 5ms per evaluation   │
                                    └────────────────────────┘

  Rule: record=job:error_rate:5m
        expr=sum(rate(errors[5m])) by(job)
  Alert on: job:error_rate:5m > 5

For anihpj: Create recording rules for frequently-used metrics — faster dashboards AND faster alerts.

Alerting Q150 of 200
Which of these is a Grafana alerting best practice for anihpj/jobpost?
A) Alert on everything — more alerts means more visibility into your system
B) Alert on symptoms, not causes. Every alert has a runbook URL. Use appropriate severity (critical P1 → PagerDuty, warning P3 → Slack). Set meaningful "For" durations. Never alert on things you can't act on. Test alerts regularly with the "Test" button
C) Use the same severity for all alerts — simplifies notification policy configuration
D) Disable "For" duration — immediate notifications are always better for fast incident response
✅ Correct Answer: B

Best practices: Alert on symptoms (user-visible), not causes. Every alert has a runbook URL. Use severity levels (critical→PagerDuty, warning→Slack). Set appropriate "For" durations. Never alert on things you can't act on.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Alerting Best Practices — The Golden Rules
  GRAFANA ALERTING BEST PRACTICES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Alert on SYMPTOMS, not causes             │
  │     ✅ "Error rate > 5%" (users see it)      │
  │     ❌ "CPU > 80%" (might be fine)           │
  │                                              │
  │  ② Every alert has a RUNBOOK URL             │
  │     annotations: { runbook_url: wiki/... }   │
  │                                              │
  │  ③ Use SEVERITY LEVELS                       │
  │     Critical → PagerDuty (wake someone)      │
  │     Warning  → Slack (look soon)             │
  │     Info     → Email (no urgency)            │
  │                                              │
  │  ④ Set meaningful "FOR" durations            │
  │     1m critical, 5m warning, 15m info        │
  │                                              │
  │  ⑤ Don't alert on UNACTIONABLE things        │
  │     If there's nothing to do, don't alert    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Alert when error rate > 5% with runbook link. Don't alert when CPU > 80%.

5.1 Authentication — Who Are You?Q151-Q156

Admin Q151 of 200
What authentication options does Grafana support?
A) Only username/password — Grafana has no external authentication support
B) Grafana only supports OAuth2 — built-in auth and LDAP are deprecated
C) Grafana requires a separate authentication service like Keycloak — no built-in auth
D) Built-in (username/password with brute-force protection), LDAP, OAuth2 (Google, GitHub, GitLab, Azure AD, Okta, Generic), SAML, Auth Proxy (reverse proxy passes authenticated user via header), and JWT
✅ Correct Answer: D

Grafana supports 6 auth methods: Built-in, LDAP, OAuth2, SAML, Auth Proxy, and Anonymous. For anihpj: OAuth2 with GitHub/Google for SSO, or Auth Proxy when using nginx/oauth2-proxy in front of Grafana.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Grafana Authentication Methods — 6 Options
  AUTH METHODS (configured in grafana.ini):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Built-in    → Grafana's own user DB       │
  │  ② LDAP        → Corporate directory (AD)    │
  │  ③ OAuth2      → GitHub/Google/GitLab/Okta   │
  │  ④ SAML        → Enterprise SSO (Azure AD)   │
  │  ⑤ Auth Proxy  → nginx/oauth2-proxy passes   │
  │                   user via X-WEBAUTH-USER     │
  │  ⑥ Anonymous   → No login required (public)  │
  │                                              │
  │  For anihpj/k8s: OAuth2 GitHub SSO +         │
  │  Auth Proxy via nginx-ingress                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj on kubeadm: OAuth2 with GitHub/Google for SSO, or Auth Proxy via nginx/oauth2-proxy.

Admin Q152 of 200
How does Grafana's built-in authentication protect against brute-force attacks?
A) Grafana has no brute-force protection — you must implement it at the network/firewall level
B) After 5 consecutive failed login attempts for a user, Grafana locks that user for a configurable duration. The default admin password must be changed on first login. Password policies enforce minimum length and complexity
C) Grafana uses CAPTCHA after every 3 login attempts — this is the only protection mechanism
D) Brute-force protection is only available in Grafana Enterprise
✅ Correct Answer: B

After 5 consecutive failed logins, Grafana locks the user for a configurable duration. Default admin password must be changed on first login. Password policies enforce minimum length and complexity. Config in grafana.ini [security].

📝 Detailed Explanation
Brute-Force Protection — Login Security
  GRAFANA BUILT-IN SECURITY:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  [security] section in grafana.ini:          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ login_maximum_lifetime_days = 7   │      │
  │  │ login_maximum_inactive_lifetime   │      │
  │  │   _days = 30                       │      │
  │  │ password_policy:                   │      │
  │  │   min_length = 8                   │      │
  │  │   require_upper = true             │      │
  │  │   require_number = true            │      │
  │  │ disable_brute_force_login          │      │
  │  │   _protection = false              │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                              │
  │  Attempt 1-4: ❌ Wrong password              │
  │  Attempt 5:   ❌ User LOCKED (configurable)  │
  │                                              │
  │  Admin: Must change default password on      │
  │  first login — can't skip this step!         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Ensure brute-force protection is enabled and admin password changed on first deploy.

Admin Q153 of 200
Which OAuth2 providers does Grafana natively support?
A) Only Google — other OAuth2 providers require custom plugin development
B) Google and GitHub — these are the only OAuth2 providers built into Grafana
C) Google, GitHub, GitLab, Azure AD, Okta, Generic OAuth2 (any OAuth2-compliant provider), plus Grafana.com and Keycloak via Generic OAuth2. Each has its own configuration section in grafana.ini
D) OAuth2 requires Grafana Enterprise — OSS only supports built-in and LDAP authentication
✅ Correct Answer: C

Grafana has dedicated config sections: [auth.google], [auth.github], [auth.gitlab], [auth.azuread], [auth.okta], [auth.generic_oauth]. Each needs client_id, client_secret, allowed_domains, role mapping.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OAuth2 Providers — Native Support
  OAUTH2 PROVIDERS (grafana.ini sections):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  [auth.google]    → Google Workspace SSO     │
  │  [auth.github]    → GitHub OAuth App         │
  │  [auth.gitlab]    → GitLab Application       │
  │  [auth.azuread]   → Azure AD / Entra ID      │
  │  [auth.okta]      → Okta OIDC                │
  │  [auth.generic_oauth] → Any OAuth2 provider  │
  │                    (Keycloak, Auth0, etc.)    │
  │                                              │
  │  Common settings:                            │
  │  enabled = true                              │
  │  client_id = xxx                             │
  │  client_secret = xxx                         │
  │  allowed_domains = anihpj.com                │
  │  allow_sign_up = true                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: [auth.github] with allowed_organizations to restrict to your GitHub org members.

Admin Q154 of 200
How does LDAP authentication map directory groups to Grafana roles?
A) In the LDAP TOML config: define group_mappings that map LDAP group DNs to Grafana org roles. E.g., "CN=grafana-admins" → Admin role, "CN=grafana-editors" → Editor. Users are auto-assigned to Grafana orgs based on their LDAP group membership
B) LDAP groups are only for authentication — role assignment must be done manually in the Grafana UI
C) LDAP group mapping is automatic — any LDAP group with "grafana" in the name gets Editor access
D) LDAP group mapping requires a separate LDAP-to-Grafana sync service — not built into Grafana
✅ Correct Answer: A

LDAP uses a TOML file (/etc/grafana/ldap.toml) with [[servers]] and [[servers.group_mappings]]. Maps LDAP group DNs → Grafana roles (Admin/Editor/Viewer). Auto-assigns on login.

📝 Detailed Explanation
LDAP Group Mapping — Directory → Grafana Roles
  LDAP GROUP MAPPING (ldap.toml):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  [[servers.group_mappings]]                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ group_dn = "CN=grafana-admins,     │      │
  │  │            OU=Groups,DC=anihpj"   │      │
  │  │ org_role = Admin                   │      │
  │  ├────────────────────────────────────┤      │
  │  │ group_dn = "CN=grafana-editors,..."│      │
  │  │ org_role = Editor                  │      │
  │  ├────────────────────────────────────┤      │
  │  │ group_dn = "CN=grafana-viewers,..."│      │
  │  │ org_role = Viewer                  │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                              │
  │  User logs in → LDAP search for groups →    │
  │  highest-priority match → role assigned     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: "CN=platform-eng"→Admin, "CN=developers"→Editor, "CN=viewers"→Viewer.

Admin Q155 of 200
What is SAML authentication in Grafana and when would you use it?
A) SAML is Grafana's legacy authentication — replaced by OAuth2 in Grafana 10
B) SAML (Security Assertion Markup Language) is an XML-based SSO protocol commonly used in enterprise environments (Okta, Azure AD, OneLogin). Configure in grafana.ini [auth.saml] with IdP metadata URL or XML, assertion mapping (email, name, login), and optional single logout (SLO)
C) SAML is the same as OAuth2 but uses a different configuration section name
D) SAML only works with Grafana Enterprise — OSS doesn't support SAML authentication
✅ Correct Answer: B

SAML is the enterprise SSO standard (Okta, Azure AD, OneLogin). Configure [auth.saml] in grafana.ini with IdP metadata, assertion mappings (email, name, groups), and optional single logout (SLO).

📝 Detailed Explanation
SAML Authentication — Enterprise SSO
  SAML FLOW (Service Provider initiated):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① User visits Grafana → redirected to IdP   │
  │  ② IdP authenticates (password/MFA)          │
  │  ③ IdP sends SAML assertion to Grafana       │
  │  ④ Grafana validates assertion → logs in     │
  │                                              │
  │  Config in grafana.ini [auth.saml]:          │
  │  enabled = true                              │
  │  idp_metadata_url = https://idp/metadata     │
  │  assertion_attribute_email = email           │
  │  assertion_attribute_login = username        │
  │  assertion_attribute_name = display_name     │
  │  single_logout = true                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj enterprise: SAML with Azure AD for organization-wide SSO to Grafana dashboards.

Admin Q156 of 200
How does Auth Proxy authentication work in Grafana?
A) Auth Proxy requires the Grafana Agent running on every user's machine
B) It's a deprecated authentication method removed in Grafana 10
C) Grafana runs an internal proxy that authenticates users against multiple backends simultaneously
D) A reverse proxy (nginx, Apache, oauth2-proxy) handles authentication and passes the authenticated username to Grafana via an HTTP header (X-WEBAUTH-USER by default). Grafana trusts the proxy and creates a session for that user without further authentication
✅ Correct Answer: D

Auth Proxy delegates auth to nginx/oauth2-proxy. Proxy authenticates → passes username via X-WEBAUTH-USER header → Grafana trusts it. CRITICAL: proxy MUST strip client-supplied headers to prevent spoofing.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Auth Proxy — Delegate to Reverse Proxy
  AUTH PROXY FLOW (nginx + oauth2-proxy):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Browser → nginx (oauth2-proxy) → Grafana    │
  │                                              │
  │  ① Browser: GET /grafana                     │
  │  ② nginx: redirect to OAuth2 provider       │
  │  ③ User: authenticates with Google/GitHub    │
  │  ④ nginx: receives OAuth2 callback          │
  │  ⑤ nginx: sets X-WEBAUTH-USER: alice       │
  │  ⑥ nginx: forwards to Grafana               │
  │  ⑦ Grafana: reads header → logs in alice    │
  │                                              │
  │  ⚠️ CRITICAL: nginx MUST strip any           │
  │     X-WEBAUTH-USER sent by the client!       │
  │     Otherwise: header spoofing attack        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj on k8s: nginx-ingress + oauth2-proxy sidecar → Grafana trusts the authenticated header.

Admin Q157 of 200
What are the three built-in Organization roles in Grafana?
A) Admin (can manage users, org settings, data sources, plugins, and all dashboards), Editor (can create/edit/delete dashboards, panels, and alert rules but cannot manage users or data sources), Viewer (read-only — can view dashboards and Explore but cannot create or edit anything)
B) Super Admin, Admin, and User — three levels of system access
C) Owner, Contributor, and Reader — based on the GitLab permission model
D) Admin, Developer, and Guest — Guest accounts expire after 30 days of inactivity
✅ Correct Answer: A

Three org roles: Admin (full control), Editor (create/edit dashboards/alerts), Viewer (read-only). For anihpj: Platform engineers=Admin, Developers=Editor, Stakeholders=Viewer.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Organization Roles — Permission Levels
  THREE ORG ROLES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ADMIN:    🔑 Full control                   │
  │  ├ Manage users, teams, org settings         │
  │  ├ Manage data sources, plugins              │
  │  ├ Create/edit/delete ALL dashboards         │
  │  └ Full alerting management                  │
  │                                              │
  │  EDITOR:   ✏️ Create & edit content           │
  │  ├ Create/edit dashboards, alerts            │
  │  ├ Use Explore, create panels                │
  │  └ Cannot manage users/data sources/org      │
  │                                              │
  │  VIEWER:   👁️ Read-only                       │
  │  ├ View dashboards, use Explore              │
  │  └ Cannot create, edit, or delete anything   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Platform-Eng=Admin, Developers=Editor, Stakeholders=Viewer.

Admin Q158 of 200
How do Folder permissions work in Grafana?
A) Folders inherit permissions from the organization — individual folder permissions don't exist
B) Folder permissions are set globally in grafana.ini — they can't be managed in the UI
C) Each folder has its own permission settings: assign users/teams as Admin, Editor, or Viewer for THAT folder. A user can be Admin of one folder and Viewer of another. Folder permissions override the org role for dashboards within

✅ Correct Answer: C

Folder permissions provide granular access — assign users/teams as Admin/Editor/Viewer per folder. Overrides org role for dashboards within. Permission hierarchy: Dashboard > Folder > Org Role.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Permission Hierarchy — Dashboard > Folder > Org
  PERMISSION RESOLUTION (most specific wins):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Dashboard Permission (override)             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ "Business Overview" = Everyone:View │      │
  │  │ → Overrides folder + org role       │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │         ↓ if not set                          │
  │  Folder Permission                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ "Production" folder               │      │
  │  │   Platform-Eng: Admin              │      │
  │  │   Developers: Editor               │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │         ↓ if not set                          │
  │  Organization Role (base/default)             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ Alice: Viewer (default everywhere)  │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Devs=Viewer org role but Editor on "Dev" folder. Can't touch "Production" folder.

Admin Q160 of 200
What are Teams in Grafana and how do they simplify permission management?
A) Teams are Grafana's notification groups for alert routing — they have no relation to permissions
B) Teams are only cosmetic labels — they don't affect permissions or access control
C) Teams replace Organization roles entirely — when Teams are used, org roles are ignored
D) Teams group users together so you can assign permissions to the TEAM (e.g., "Platform-Engineering team has Editor access to Production folder") instead of assigning permissions to each user individually. When a new engineer joins, add them to the team and they automatically inherit all team permissions
✅ Correct Answer: D

Teams group users for permission management. Assign permissions to the TEAM, not individuals. New hire → add to team → auto-inherits all permissions. Offboarding → remove from team → access revoked.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Teams — Scalable Permission Management
  WITHOUT TEAMS (per-user):       WITH TEAMS (per-group):
  ┌──────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ Alice: Editor(Prod)  │       │ Team: Platform-Eng   │
  │ Bob:   Editor(Prod)  │       │ → Editor(Production) │
  │ Carol: Editor(Prod)  │       │ → Admin(Dev)         │
  │ Dave:  Admin(Dev)    │       │                      │
  │ Eve:   Admin(Dev)    │       │ Team: Developers     │
  │ ...(50 more users)...│       │ → Editor(Dev)        │
  └──────────────────────┘       │ → Viewer(Production) │
                                  └──────────────────────┘
  New hire → add Alice to        New hire → add to
  Platform-Eng team              6 folders, assign perms
  → instant access to all!

For anihpj: Teams for Platform-Eng (Admin), Developers (Editor), Viewers. Permissions to teams, not users.

Admin Q161 of 200
What are Service Accounts and how do they differ from API Keys?
A) Service Accounts and API Keys are the same thing — just different names in the Grafana UI
B) Service Accounts are for human users; API Keys are for machine-to-machine communication
C) Service Accounts are machine identities with RBAC permissions (like users but for automation). They can have multiple tokens (replacing API Keys) and you manage their permissions via org roles and folder permissions — just like human users. API Keys are the legacy approach, deprecated in favor of Service Accounts
D) Service Accounts require Grafana Enterprise — OSS only supports API Keys
✅ Correct Answer: C

Service Accounts (Grafana 8.5+) are machine identities with RBAC. Replace legacy API Keys. Assign org role, team membership, folder permissions. Can have multiple tokens. Modern replacement for API Keys.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Service Accounts vs API Keys — Modern vs Legacy
  SERVICE ACCOUNTS (modern):       API KEYS (legacy/deprecated):
  ┌────────────────────────┐      ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ Identity: anihpj-ci    │      │ Just a bearer token    │
  │ Role: Editor           │      │ Role: Admin (fixed)    │
  │ Teams: Platform-Eng    │      │ No RBAC integration    │
  │ Permissions: granular  │      │ No audit trail         │
  │ Tokens: multiple       │      │ One key = one token    │
  │   ├ token-ci-1 (30d)   │      │                        │
  │   ├ token-ci-2 (90d)   │      │                        │
  │   └ token-ci-3 (1y)    │      │                        │
  └────────────────────────┘      └────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Create "gitlab-ci" Service Account with Editor role → token → GitLab CI provisions dashboards.

Admin Q162 of 200
What does the allow_sign_up setting control in grafana.ini?

C) It enables/disables the "Sign Up" button on the Grafana login page for built-in auth
D) It's a Grafana Cloud-only setting — OSS doesn't have user sign-up controls

allow_sign_up ([users] section): When external auth (OAuth2/LDAP) authenticates a new user — true=auto-create account (with auto_assign_org_role), false=reject (admin must pre-create).

allow_sign_up — Auto-Provisioning Control
  allow_sign_up = true:                allow_sign_up = false:
  ┌────────────────────────────┐      ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ New GitHub user logs in    │      │ New GitHub user logs in    │
  │ → Grafana creates account  │      │ → Grafana checks DB        │
  │ → Role: Viewer (default)   │      │ → User NOT found           │
  │ → User can see dashboards  │      │ → ❌ Login rejected         │
  │ → Admin promotes later     │      │ → Admin must create first  │
  └────────────────────────────┘      └────────────────────────────┘

  Config in grafana.ini [users]:
  allow_sign_up = true/false
  auto_assign_org_role = Viewer

For anihpj: true=auto-provision GitHub org members as Viewers. false=require admin approval.

5.3 Organizations & Multi-TenancyQ163-Q166

Admin Q163 of 200
What is a Grafana Organization?
A) A complete tenant boundary — each Org has its OWN users, dashboards, data sources, teams, alert rules, and settings, completely isolated from other Orgs. One Grafana instance can host multiple Organizations
B) An Organization is the same as a Team — it's a group of users with shared permissions
C) An Organization is a dashboard folder at the root level — it's purely organizational
D) Organizations are only available in Grafana Cloud — self-hosted doesn't support multi-tenancy
✅ Correct Answer: A

An Organization is the highest isolation boundary — each Org has its OWN users, dashboards, data sources, teams, alerts, settings. One Grafana instance hosts multiple completely isolated Orgs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Organizations — Complete Tenant Isolation
  ONE GRAFANA INSTANCE → MULTIPLE ORGANIZATIONS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌─────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Org: Production │  │ Org: Staging    │    │
  │  │ ├ DS: prom-prod │  │ ├ DS: prom-stag │    │
  │  │ ├ DS: pg-prod   │  │ ├ DS: pg-stag   │    │
  │  │ ├ Dashboards    │  │ ├ Dashboards    │    │
  │  │ ├ Alerts        │  │ ├ Alerts        │    │
  │  │ ├ Teams         │  │ ├ Teams         │    │
  │  │ └ Users (Alice) │  │ └ Users (Alice) │    │
  │  └─────────────────┘  └─────────────────┘    │
  │         ↑ COMPLETELY ISOLATED ↑               │
  │  Alice can be Admin in Prod, Viewer in Staging│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: "Production" and "Staging" Orgs — isolated data sources, dashboards, and alerts per environment.

Can one user belong to multiple Grafana Organizations?

Admin Q165 of 200
What are common multi-tenant Grafana patterns?
A) Multi-tenancy requires a separate Grafana instance per tenant — Organizations can't provide true isolation
B) One Org per environment (prod/staging/dev), one Org per team, one Org per customer, or one Org per business unit. Organizations provide logical isolation; combine with separate data source instances for full isolation
C) Multi-tenancy is achieved through dashboard folders, not Organizations — Orgs are deprecated
D) Multi-tenancy is a Grafana Enterprise feature — OSS only supports single-tenant mode
✅ Correct Answer: B

Common patterns: Per environment (prod/staging/dev), Per team, Per customer, Per business unit. Orgs = logical isolation; pair with separate data sources for full isolation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Tenancy Patterns — Organization Strategies
  MULTI-TENANCY PATTERNS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Per Environment:                            │
  │  ├ Org: Production → DS: prom-prod, pg-prod  │
  │  ├ Org: Staging    → DS: prom-stag, pg-stag  │
  │  └ Org: Dev        → DS: prom-dev, pg-dev    │
  │                                              │
  │  Per Team:                                   │
  │  ├ Org: Platform-Eng → Infra dashboards      │
  │  ├ Org: Frontend     → FE metrics only       │
  │  └ Org: Backend      → API/DB metrics        │
  │                                              │
  │  Per Customer (SaaS):                        │
  │  ├ Org: Customer-A → Isolated dashboards     │
  │  └ Org: Customer-B → Isolated dashboards     │
  │                                              │
  │  Orgs = logical isolation | Full = + separate DS
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Production Org→kubeadm Prometheus, Staging Org→kind-cka-lab Prometheus.

Admin Q166 of 200
How do Grafana Cloud Stacks compare to self-hosted Organizations?
A) They're identical — Cloud Stacks and Orgs are the same feature with different names
B) Cloud Stacks are less isolated than Orgs — they share data sources across stacks
C) Grafana Cloud Stacks are fully isolated Grafana instances (separate URL, separate data plane), while Organizations are logical tenants within a single Grafana instance. Stacks offer stronger isolation; Orgs are more cost-effective for internal teams
D) Cloud Stacks are only for enterprise customers — self-hosted users must use Organizations
✅ Correct Answer: C

Cloud Stacks = physically separate instances (separate URL, DB, infra). Organizations = logical tenants within one instance. Stacks → stronger isolation, higher cost. Orgs → lighter, easier, shared infra.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cloud Stacks vs Organizations — Isolation Levels
  CLOUD STACKS (physical):         ORGANIZATIONS (logical):
  ┌────────────────────────┐      ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ Stack A                │      │ One Grafana Instance   │
  │ └ URL: a.grafana.net   │      │ ├ Org: Production      │
  │ └ DB: separate postgres│      │ ├ Org: Staging         │
  │ └ Resources: dedicated │      │ └ Org: Dev             │
  │                        │      │                        │
  │ Stack B                │      │ All share: same process│
  │ └ URL: b.grafana.net   │      │ same DB, same infra    │
  │ └ DB: separate postgres│      │                        │
  │ └ Resources: dedicated │      │ Isolation: logical only│
  └────────────────────────┘      └────────────────────────┘
  Stronger isolation, $$$        Lighter weight, $

For anihpj: Orgs for internal envs. Cloud Stacks (or separate instances) for per-customer SaaS.

5.4 grafana.ini Configuration Deep DiveQ167-Q172

Admin Q167 of 200
Where is the grafana.ini configuration file located?
A) Default: /etc/grafana/grafana.ini (Linux package install), $WORKING_DIR/conf/defaults.ini (Docker), or $WORKING_DIR/conf/custom.ini (custom). Override with --config=/path/to/grafana.ini flag. Environment variables override INI settings
B) Always at /etc/grafana/grafana.ini — the location cannot be changed
C) Grafana stores all configuration in its internal SQLite database — grafana.ini is only for Docker
D) Configuration is in /opt/grafana/config.json — grafana.ini was replaced in Grafana 8
✅ Correct Answer: A

Default: /etc/grafana/grafana.ini (Linux), $WORKING_DIR/conf/defaults.ini (Docker). Override with --config flag. Environment variables (GF_ prefix) override INI settings.

📝 Detailed Explanation
grafana.ini — Configuration File Locations
  CONFIG LOCATIONS & OVERRIDES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Linux:    /etc/grafana/grafana.ini          │
  │  Docker:   /etc/grafana/grafana.ini          │
  │            (or $GF_PATHS_CONFIG)             │
  │  Custom:   --config=/path/to/grafana.ini     │
  │                                              │
  │  OVERRIDE HIERARCHY:                         │
  │  defaults.ini → grafana.ini → ENV vars       │
  │  (lowest)          (middle)      (highest)   │
  │                                              │
  │  ENV VAR PATTERN:                            │
  │  [server] http_port = 3000                   │
  │  → GF_SERVER_HTTP_PORT=8080                  │
  │                                              │
  │  [auth.github] client_id = xxx               │
  │  → GF_AUTH_GITHUB_CLIENT_ID=xxx              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj on K8s: Mount as ConfigMap volume or use env vars in Deployment spec.

Admin Q168 of 200
What do the [server] settings in grafana.ini control?
A) Server settings only control the database connection for Grafana's internal storage
B) They control Grafana's alerting server — SMTP, notification intervals, and contact points
C) http_port (default 3000), domain (your Grafana domain for cookies), root_url (full URL including subpath for reverse proxy setups), serve_from_sub_path (for hosting Grafana at /grafana/ behind nginx), router_logging (log HTTP requests), and enforce_domain (cookie security)
D) Server settings are only available in Grafana Enterprise — OSS uses hardcoded defaults
✅ Correct Answer: C

The [server] section: http_port (3000), domain (cookie domain), root_url (full URL for OAuth callbacks & links), serve_from_sub_path (/grafana/ behind nginx), router_logging, enforce_domain.

📝 Detailed Explanation
[server] — Network & URL Configuration
  [server] IN grafana.ini:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  http_port = 3000          # Listen port     │
  │  domain = anihpj.com       # Cookie domain   │
  │  root_url = https://monitoring.anihpj.com/   │
  │             grafana/       # Full public URL  │
  │  serve_from_sub_path = true                  │
  │  router_logging = true     # Log HTTP        │
  │  enforce_domain = true     # Cookie security  │
  │                                              │
  │  root_url is CRITICAL for:                   │
  │  → OAuth2 redirect callbacks                 │
  │  → Notification links (dashboard URLs)       │
  │  → API responses (Location headers)          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: root_url=https://grafana.anihpj.com for correct OAuth2 redirects.

Admin Q170 of 200
What critical [security] settings should you configure in grafana.ini?
A) Security settings are only for Grafana Cloud — self-hosted has no security configuration
B) admin_user/admin_password (default admin credentials), secret_key (used for signing session cookies and data source auth tokens — MUST be set to a random string and NEVER changed after initial setup), disable_gravatar (privacy), cookie_secure/cookie_samesite (session security), and strict_transport_security (HSTS)
C) The only security setting is admin_password — everything else is hardcoded for security
D) Security settings are configured through environment variables only — not in grafana.ini
✅ Correct Answer: B

Critical [security] settings: secret_key (random 32+ chars — NEVER change after setup), admin_user/password, disable_gravatar (privacy), cookie_secure/samesite (HTTP security), strict_transport_security (HSTS).

📝 Detailed Explanation
[security] — Critical Security Settings
  [security] IN grafana.ini:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  secret_key = $(openssl rand -hex 32)        │
  │  → Signs cookies, encrypts DS passwords     │
  │  → ⚠️ NEVER CHANGE after initial setup!      │
  │  → Changing = invalidates ALL sessions       │
  │                                              │
  │  admin_user = admin                          │
  │  admin_password = changeme123                │
  │  → CHANGE on first login!                    │
  │                                              │
  │  disable_gravatar = true  # Privacy          │
  │  cookie_secure = true     # HTTPS only       │
  │  cookie_samesite = strict # CSRF protection  │
  │  strict_transport_security = true # HSTS     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Generate secret_key with openssl, store in K8s Secret.

Admin Q172 of 200
How do environment variables override grafana.ini settings?
A) Environment variables don't override grafana.ini — the file always takes precedence
B) Only specifically whitelisted settings can be overridden via environment variables
C) ANY grafana.ini setting can be overridden with the GF_ prefix: [section] key_name → GF_SECTION_KEY_NAME. Example: [server] http_port → GF_SERVER_HTTP_PORT=8080. This is the standard approach for Docker/K8s deployments
D) Environment variable overrides require Grafana Enterprise — OSS reads only from grafana.ini
✅ Correct Answer: C

ALL settings overridable via GF_SECTION_KEY pattern. [server] http_port → GF_SERVER_HTTP_PORT=8080. Standard for Docker/K8s: use env vars instead of mounting grafana.ini.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Env Var Overrides — GF_ Prefix Pattern
  ENVIRONMENT VARIABLE OVERRIDE PATTERN:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  grafana.ini          →  Environment Variable │
  │  ─────────────────────   ─────────────────── │
  │  [server]              →  GF_SERVER_         │
  │    http_port = 3000    →  HTTP_PORT=8080      │
  │    root_url = ...      →  ROOT_URL=...        │
  │                                              │
  │  [security]            →  GF_SECURITY_       │
  │    admin_password = x  →  ADMIN_PASSWORD=x    │
  │    secret_key = x      →  SECRET_KEY=x        │
  │                                              │
  │  [database]            →  GF_DATABASE_       │
  │    type = postgres     →  TYPE=postgres       │
  │    host = pg:5432      →  HOST=pg:5432        │
  │                                              │
  │  Rule: [section] key → GF_SECTION_KEY       │
  │  Dots → underscores, all UPPERCASE           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj K8s: Set GF_SERVER_ROOT_URL, GF_SECURITY_ADMIN_PASSWORD as env vars.

5.5 Provisioning & Infrastructure-as-CodeQ173-Q176

Admin Q173 of 200
What is Grafana Provisioning?
A) Provisioning is the process of installing Grafana on a new server using apt/yum
B) Provisioning lets you define data sources, dashboards, alert rules, and notification policies as YAML/JSON files. Grafana reads these files on startup (and watches for changes), creating/updating resources automatically. Enables GitOps — your entire Grafana config is version-controlled code
C) Provisioning is Grafana's term for creating user accounts in bulk via CSV import
D) Provisioning requires Grafana Enterprise — OSS must configure everything through the UI
✅ Correct Answer: B

Provisioning = Infrastructure-as-Code for Grafana. Define data sources, dashboards, alerts, notification policies as YAML/JSON files. Grafana reads on startup + watches for changes. GitOps: version-controlled Grafana config, zero manual UI.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Provisioning — Grafana as Code
  PROVISIONING DIRECTORIES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  /etc/grafana/provisioning/                  │
  │  ├── datasources/          (YAML files)       │
  │  ├── dashboards/           (JSON files)       │
  │  ├── alerting/             (YAML/JSON)        │
  │  ├── plugins/              (plugin list)      │
  │  └── notifiers/            (contact points)   │
  │                                              │
  │  Git → CI/CD → provisioning dir → Grafana    │
  │  Reads on startup, watches for changes        │
  │  Provisioned resources: read-only in UI       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Store all Grafana config in Git → CI/CD deploys → Grafana auto-loads. Zero manual UI.

5.6 Maintenance, Backup & SecurityQ177-Q180

Admin Q177 of 200
How do you backup Grafana's internal database?
A) Grafana auto-backups to /var/lib/grafana/backups/ — no manual backup is needed
B) For SQLite: copy /var/lib/grafana/grafana.db (stop Grafana first or use .backup). For PostgreSQL: pg_dump grafana > backup.sql. This backs up dashboards, users, orgs, alert rules, annotations, and settings. Also export dashboard JSON as an additional safety net
C) Export dashboards as JSON — that's the only backup needed. Users and settings are recreated
D) Grafana database backup is a Grafana Enterprise feature — OSS has no backup mechanism
✅ Correct Answer: B

SQLite: copy grafana.db (stop first or .backup). PostgreSQL: pg_dump grafana > backup.sql. Backs up dashboards, users, orgs, alerts, annotations, settings. Also export dashboard JSON as safety net.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Database Backup — Protect Your Grafana Data
  BACKUP METHODS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SQLite:                                     │
  │  sqlite3 /var/lib/grafana/grafana.db \       │
  │    ".backup backup.db"                       │
  │  or: cp grafana.db backup.db (stop first)    │
  │                                              │
  │  PostgreSQL:                                 │
  │  pg_dump -h pg-host -U grafana grafana \     │
  │    > grafana-backup-$(date +%Y%m%d).sql      │
  │                                              │
  │  What's backed up: dashboards, users, orgs,  │
  │  alert rules, annotations, API keys, prefs   │
  │                                              │
  │  💡 Also export dashboard JSON to Git!       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: K8s CronJob runs pg_dump nightly → uploads to S3. Dashboard JSON in Git as secondary.

Admin Q180 of 200
How do you enable HTTPS/TLS for Grafana?
A) Grafana automatically enables HTTPS on port 443 — no configuration needed
B) HTTPS is a Grafana Enterprise feature — OSS only supports HTTP
C) Two approaches: (1) Set cert_file and cert_key in [server] section of grafana.ini to point to your TLS certificate and key files. (2) RECOMMENDED: Run Grafana behind a reverse proxy (nginx/traefik) that handles TLS termination — simpler, more battle-tested, and supports Let's Encrypt auto-renewal
D) Install a Grafana TLS plugin from the plugin catalog — TLS is plugin-based
✅ Correct Answer: C

Option 1: Set cert_file + cert_key in [server]. Option 2 (RECOMMENDED): Reverse proxy (nginx/traefik) handles TLS termination. Grafana HTTP internally, proxy HTTPS externally. Supports Let's Encrypt auto-renewal.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TLS/HTTPS — Two Approaches
  APPROACH 1 (direct):            APPROACH 2 (proxy - RECOMMENDED):
  ┌────────────────────────┐      ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ [server]               │      │ Browser                     │
  │ cert_file = /etc/cert  │      │   │ HTTPS :443              │
  │ cert_key = /etc/key    │      │   ▼                         │
  │ protocol = https       │      │ nginx/traefik/Caddy        │
  │                        │      │   │ TLS termination         │
  │ Grafana :443 (HTTPS)   │      │   │ Let's Encrypt auto-renew │
  └────────────────────────┘      │   ▼                         │
                                   │ Grafana :3000 (HTTP)       │
                                   └────────────────────────────┘
  On K8s: cert-manager + nginx-ingress handles TLS automatically

For anihpj on K8s: cert-manager + nginx-ingress → TLS auto-terminated. Grafana only needs HTTP internally.

🔧 Part 1 — Category 6: Troubleshooting (10%)

What to do when something breaks — panels show "No Data," queries time out, dashboards won't load. The skill that separates Grafana users from Grafana experts. 20 questions.

20Questions
10%Exam Weight
4Sub-Topics

🔧 Category 6: Troubleshooting (10%)Q181-Q200

🟡 Intermediate: When Things Go Wrong

Troubleshooting is the skill that separates Grafana users from Grafana experts. When a panel shows "No Data," a query times out, or a dashboard won't load, you need a systematic approach. For anihpj/jobpost, this means quickly diagnosing whether the issue is in Grafana, the data source, the query, or the infrastructure.

Q181-Q200 20 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

6.1 Dashboard & Panel TroubleshootingQ181-Q186

Troubleshoot Q181 of 200
Your panel shows "No data." What is the systematic diagnostic checklist?
A) Immediately restart Grafana — "No data" is always a server-side caching issue
B) Delete and recreate the panel — "No data" means the panel configuration is corrupted
C) ① Is the data source healthy? (Save & Test) ② Is the time range correct? (check time picker) ③ Is the query valid? (test in Explore mode) ④ Does the data exist? (check raw data source) ⑤ Check panel field overrides and transformations ⑥ Check browser console for errors
D) "No data" always means the data source is down — go directly to checking the data source
✅ Correct Answer: C

Systematic checklist: ① DS healthy? (Save & Test) ② Time range correct? ③ Query valid? (test in Explore) ④ Data exist? (check raw source) ⑤ Field overrides/transformations filtering? ⑥ Browser console errors.

📝 Detailed Explanation
"No Data" — Systematic Diagnostic Flow
  "NO DATA" DIAGNOSTIC CHECKLIST:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Data Source: Save & Test → green? ✅       │
  │     ↓ NO → DS is down or misconfigured        │
  │  ② Time Range: Does data exist in this window?│
  │     ↓ Try "Last 7 days" to widen scope       │
  │  ③ Query: Test in Explore → returns data?     │
  │     ↓ NO → fix the query (labels, SQL, etc)  │
  │  ④ Raw Source: Query DS directly (promQL UI)  │
  │     ↓ Does the metric/table exist at all?     │
  │  ⑤ Panel Settings: Field overrides? Filters?  │
  │     ↓ Transformations removing all rows?     │
  │  ⑥ Browser: F12 → Console → JS errors?       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: "Active Jobs" shows NoData → check PostgreSQL DS health → test SQL in Explore.

Troubleshoot Q183 of 200
The graph renders but the values are clearly wrong. What should you check?
A) Wrong values always mean Grafana has a bug — report it to Grafana Labs
B) Check the query first (especially rate vs irate, incorrect label filters, wrong aggregation), then the unit (displaying bytes as seconds?), then panel field overrides (someone changed the unit or calculation), and finally test the same query in Explore to compare
C) Wrong values are always a timezone issue — change your user timezone setting
D) Restart Grafana — wrong values are caused by corrupted internal caches
✅ Correct Answer: B

Check: Query (rate vs irate, label filters), Unit (bytes as seconds?), Field overrides (wrong unit/calc), Test in Explore (compare raw vs panel).

Troubleshoot Q184 of 200

Troubleshoot Q185 of 200
Why is my dashboard loading slowly?
A) Slow dashboards are always caused by slow network — check your internet connection
B) Grafana has a fixed loading time of 5 seconds — anything slower is expected
C) Too many panels/queries (each panel spawns queries), too wide a time range (querying 30 days of data), expensive queries (complex PromQL aggregations), or data source bottlenecks. Check browser DevTools → Network tab to see which queries are slowest
D) Slow dashboards mean Grafana needs more RAM — the only fix is to add memory to the server
✅ Correct Answer: C

Causes: Too many panels/queries, wide time range (30d data), expensive queries (complex PromQL), data source overload. Diagnose: F12 → Network → sort by time. Fix: narrow range, recording rules, reduce panels.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Slow Dashboard — Performance Diagnosis
  DASHBOARD SLOW? DIAGNOSE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  F12 → Network tab → filter "api/ds/query"  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ Query 1: /api/ds/query → 3200ms 🔴 │      │
  │  │ Query 2: /api/ds/query →  150ms   │      │
  │  │ Query 3: /api/ds/query → 2800ms 🔴 │      │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                              │
  │  FIXES:                                      │
  │  ├ Narrow time range (30d → 1h)              │
  │  ├ Use recording rules (pre-compute)         │
  │  ├ Reduce panel count (30 → 10)              │
  │  ├ Add database indexes for SQL queries      │
  │  └ Scale data source (more Prometheus RAM)   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: 30 panels querying 30d → narrow to 1h, use recording rules for expensive queries.

Troubleshoot Q186 of 200
Panel shows a red error banner. What does it mean and where do you look?
A) Red error banners are always temporary — refresh the page and they'll disappear
B) The error banner contains the specific error message. Click "Details" or check the browser console (F12 → Console) for the full error. Common errors: "query error" (data source returned an error — check Explore), "plugin error" (panel plugin crashed — check grafana.log), "transformation error" (data transformation failed)
C) Red banners mean Grafana's license has expired — renew your Grafana Enterprise subscription
D) All red errors are the same — there's no need to read the specific error message

READ THE ERROR MESSAGE. Click Details. Check F12 Console. Common: "502" (DS unreachable), "Cannot read properties" (plugin bug), "Transformation failed" (check transform config).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Red Error Banner — Decode the Message
  PANEL ERROR BANNER — DIAGNOSE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🔴 "Request failed with status code 502"    │
  │     → Data source unreachable                │
  │     → Check: DS health, network, DNS         │
  │                                              │
  │  🔴 "Cannot read properties of undefined"    │
  │     → Panel plugin JavaScript error          │
  │     → Check: F12 Console, grafana.log        │
  │                                              │
  │  🔴 "Transformation failed: ..."             │
  │     → Data transformation error              │
  │     → Check: Panel Edit → Transform tab      │
  │                                              │
  │  Always: Click "Details" for full error!     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: "502" for Prometheus → check Prometheus pod, Save & Test the data source.

6.2 Data Source & Query TroubleshootingQ187-Q194

Troubleshoot Q187 of 200
"Data source connection refused" — what does this mean and how do you fix it?
A) Grafana can't establish a TCP connection to the data source URL. Check: is the data source running? Is the hostname/port correct? Can Grafana's network reach it (same cluster? firewall? NetworkPolicy?)? Test with curl from the Grafana container
B) This error means the data source credentials are wrong — reset the password and try again
C) Connection refused always means the data source is overloaded — reduce the number of queries
D) This is a Grafana bug — upgrade to the latest version to fix it
✅ Correct Answer: A

TCP-level error — Grafana can reach IP but nothing listening, OR can't reach IP at all. Check: DS running? URL correct? (prometheus:9090 vs localhost). From Grafana pod: curl the URL. Check K8s NetworkPolicies.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Connection Refused — Network Diagnostic Flow
  "CONNECTION REFUSED" — CHECKLIST:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Is the data source running?               │
  │     kubectl get pods | grep prometheus       │
  │                                              │
  │  ② Is the URL correct?                      │
  │     prometheus:9090 (svc) vs localhost:9090  │
  │                                              │
  │  ③ Can Grafana reach it?                     │
  │     kubectl exec grafana-pod -- \            │
  │       curl http://prometheus:9090/-/healthy  │
  │                                              │
  │  ④ Network policies blocking?               │
  │     kubectl get networkpolicies              │
  │     → Allow grafana→prometheus traffic       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Exec into Grafana pod, curl Prometheus health endpoint to test connectivity.

Troubleshoot Q188 of 200
"Authentication failed" when connecting to a data source. What are possible causes?
A) Authentication errors always mean the username is wrong — the password is rarely the issue
B) This error only occurs with cloud data sources — self-hosted sources don't require auth
C) Wrong username/password, expired API key/token, TLS certificate issue (expired or untrusted), wrong authentication method selected (trying Basic Auth on a Bearer token endpoint), or credentials were rotated but the data source config wasn't updated
D) Authentication failed means the data source has been deleted — recreate it from scratch
✅ Correct Answer: C

Wrong creds (typo), expired API key/token, TLS cert issue (expired/untrusted), wrong auth method (Basic vs Bearer), credentials rotated but DS config not updated.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Auth Failed — Credential Diagnostic Checklist
  "AUTHENTICATION FAILED" — CHECK:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ☐ Username/password: Typo? Rotated?        │
  │  ☐ API key/token: Expired? Revoked?          │
  │  ☐ TLS certificate: Expired? Self-signed     │
  │     not trusted? Wrong CA?                   │
  │  ☐ Auth method: Using Basic Auth but DS      │
  │     expects Bearer token?                    │
  │  ☐ DB permissions: User has CONNECT?        │
  │     GRANT CONNECT ON DATABASE TO user;      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: PG auth fails → check grafana_reader user exists with CONNECT on jobpost DB.

Troubleshoot Q189 of 200
"Query timeout" — what should you do?
A) Increase the Grafana server's CPU and memory — timeouts are always resource-related
B) Narrow the time range, simplify the query (reduce label cardinality, use recording rules for PromQL, add database indexes for SQL), increase the data source timeout setting in Grafana, or check if the data source itself is overloaded
C) Query timeouts cannot be fixed — switch to a different data source type
D) Restart Grafana — timeouts are caused by Grafana's internal query scheduler getting stuck
✅ Correct Answer: B

Narrow time range, simplify query (reduce cardinality, recording rules, DB indexes), increase DS timeout, check DS health. Use $__interval instead of hardcoded windows.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Query Timeout — Fixes in Priority Order
  QUERY TIMEOUT FIXES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Narrow time range: 30d → 1h               │
  │  ② Simplify: rate(http[30d]) → rate(http[$__interval])
  │  ③ Pre-compute: Use recording rules           │
  │  ④ Index: Add DB indexes for SQL queries     │
  │  ⑤ Increase timeout: DS settings → timeout   │
  │  ⑥ Scale: More resources for data source     │
  │                                              │
  │  $__interval = Grafana auto-calculates       │
  │  optimal step based on time range            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: rate(http[30d]) times out → use $__interval for auto-adjusted windows.

Troubleshoot Q190 of 200
"Bad Gateway / Proxy Error" from a data source. What does this mean?
A) Bad Gateway means the data source returned invalid data — fix the query
B) This error only occurs when Grafana is behind a load balancer — it's not a data source issue
C) Bad Gateway is always a temporary network blip — ignore it, it resolves automatically
D) Grafana's backend proxy can reach the data source host but the data source returned an HTTP error (502/503/504). Check: is the data source process running? Did it crash and restart? Is a reverse proxy between Grafana and the data source returning errors? Check data source logs
✅ Correct Answer: D

502/504 = Grafana reached DS host but got error response. DS might be running but unhealthy. Check DS logs, proxy between Grafana→DS, DS OOM/crashing.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Bad Gateway (502/504) — What It Means
  502/504 ERROR FLOW:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Grafana → [TCP OK] → Data Source Host       │
  │                         ↓                    │
  │                    Returns 502/504            │
  │                                              │
  │  Meaning: TCP works, HTTP fails              │
  │  → DS process running but unhealthy          │
  │  → DS in crash loop, WAL replay, compaction  │
  │  → Proxy between Grafana and DS failing      │
  │  → DS out of memory, returning errors        │
  │                                              │
  │  CHECK: DS logs, proxy logs, DS metrics      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Prometheus 502 → check Prometheus logs for WAL replay or compaction issues.

Troubleshoot Q191 of 200
Your PromQL query returns empty results but you know the metric exists. Why?
A) Label mismatch (typo in {job="api"} vs {job="anihpj-api"}), time range issue (metric only started 10 min ago but query covers 1h), stale data (metric hasn't been scraped recently), or counter reset (Prometheus counter reset causes rate() to return nothing briefly)
B) Empty results always mean the metric doesn't exist — check metric names in Explore
C) PromQL can't handle empty results — use SQL instead for reliability
D) Empty results mean Prometheus is down — restart the Prometheus service
✅ Correct Answer: A

Label mismatch (typo: {job="api"} vs {job="anihpj-api"}), time range (metric started after query window), stale data (>5min no scrape), counter reset (rate() needs 2 samples).

📝 Detailed Explanation
PromQL Empty Results — Diagnostic Checklist
  PROMQL RETURNS EMPTY? CHECK:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Label mismatch: {job="api"} but actual   │
  │     label is {job="anihpj-api"}             │
  │     → Test: {job=~".*api.*"}                │
  │                                              │
  │  ② Time range: Metric started 10min ago     │
  │     but you're querying last 1h              │
  │     → Try "Last 5 minutes"                  │
  │                                              │
  │  ③ Stale data: No scrape in >5min           │
  │     → Prometheus drops the metric            │
  │                                              │
  │  ④ Counter reset: rate() needs ≥2 samples   │
  │     → Reset causes gap, returns nothing      │
  │                                              │
  │  Debug: count({job=~".*"}) by (job)         │
  │  → Lists all job labels with data           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Explore → count({job=~".*"}) by (job) to see all labels and find typos.

Troubleshoot Q192 of 200
LogQL query returns logs from the wrong application. What went wrong?
A) Loki is returning random data — restart the Loki ingester to fix log routing
B) This is a Grafana Explore bug — switch to the dashboard Logs panel for accurate results
C) Label selector is too broad ({app=~".*"} matches everything) or wrong label key used ({service="api"} but the label is actually named "app"). Also check regex — |= "ERROR" is case-sensitive and matches substrings, which can pull unexpected logs
D) Wrong logs always mean Promtail is misconfigured — the LogQL query is correct
✅ Correct Answer: C

Label selector too broad ({app=~".*"}), wrong label key ({service="api"} but actual is "app"), case sensitivity (|= "error" ≠ "ERROR"), substring matching (|= "error" matches "terror").

📝 Detailed Explanation
LogQL Wrong Logs — Selector Debugging
  LOGQL RETURNING WRONG LOGS? CHECK:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Broad selector: {app=~".*"}→everything   │
  │     Fix: {app="anihpj-api"}                  │
  │  ② Wrong label: {service="api"} but is "app" │
  │     Check actual labels in Explore           │
  │  ③ Case: |= "error" won't match "ERROR"     │
  │     Fix: |= "(?i)error" case-insensitive    │
  │  ④ Substring: |= "err" matches "terror"     │
  │     Fix: |= "^err" or use !~ to exclude     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: {job=~".*anihpj.*"} to see all anihpj log streams, verify exact labels.

Troubleshoot Q193 of 200
SQL query returns "relation does not exist" in Grafana's PostgreSQL data source.
A) This error means PostgreSQL is down — restart the database service
B) Schema mismatch: the table name is wrong, the table is in a different schema (not in search_path), the database user doesn't have USAGE permission on the schema, or you're connected to the wrong database. Check: SELECT table_name FROM information_schema.tables WHERE table_schema='public'
C) "Relation does not exist" always means the PostgreSQL plugin is broken — reinstall it
D) This error means Grafana's PostgreSQL driver version is incompatible — upgrade Grafana
✅ Correct Answer: B

Table name typo, wrong schema (not in search_path), no USAGE permission, wrong database. Check: SELECT table_name FROM information_schema.tables.

📝 Detailed Explanation
"Relation Does Not Exist" — PostgreSQL Schema Issues
  "RELATION DOES NOT EXIST" — CHECK:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Table name: Typo? Case-sensitive?         │
  │     → "jobs" vs "Jobs" vs "job_list"         │
  │                                              │
  │  ② Schema: Table in "public" but user        │
  │     searches "anihpj" schema?                │
  │     → SET search_path TO anihpj, public;     │
  │     → GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA anihpj TO user;  │
  │                                              │
  │  ③ Database: Connected to "postgres" but    │
  │     tables are in "jobpost" database?        │
  │     → Check data source Database field       │
  │                                              │
  │  Debug: SELECT current_database(),           │
  │         current_schema();                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Verify with SELECT current_database(), current_schema() in Explore.

Troubleshoot Q194 of 200
Plugin not working after installation. What are the common causes?
A) Plugins always work immediately after installation — failures require reinstallation
B) The only cause is a corrupted download — reinstall the plugin to fix it
C) Plugins only fail on Grafana Cloud — self-hosted installations are immune to plugin issues
D) Unsigned plugin not allowed (add to allow_loading_unsigned_plugins), incompatible version (plugin requires newer Grafana), Grafana not restarted after installation, missing dependencies, or plugin binary not executable (Linux permissions)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Unsigned plugin (add to allow_loading_unsigned_plugins), incompatible version, no restart after install, missing dependencies, file permissions (binary not executable).

Plugin Not Working — Diagnostic Checklist
  PLUGIN ISSUES — CHECK IN ORDER:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Unsigned? → allow_loading_unsigned_plugins│
  │     in grafana.ini: plugin-id,other-plugin   │
  │                                              │
  │  ② Version mismatch? Plugin needs Grafana  │
  │     10+, you're on 9 → Upgrade or find older │
  │                                              │
  │  ③ Restarted? Grafana must restart after    │
  │     plugin install → kubectl rollout restart │
  │                                              │
  │  ④ Dependencies? Some plugins need system   │
  │     packages → check plugin README            │
  │                                              │
  │  ⑤ Permissions? Binary must be executable   │
  │     → chmod +x plugin-binary                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: After plugin install, restart Grafana pod and check grafana.log.

6.4 Performance & Log InvestigationQ195-Q200

Troubleshoot Q195 of 200
Grafana feels sluggish. How do you diagnose performance issues?
A) Grafana performance issues can only be diagnosed by Grafana Labs support — there are no self-service tools
B) Add more RAM to the Grafana server — performance issues are always memory-related
C) Browser DevTools → Network tab (identify slow API calls), Performance tab (JavaScript rendering bottlenecks), Grafana server logs (/var/log/grafana/grafana.log for backend slowness), database query analysis (slow SQL queries), and Grafana's own /metrics endpoint
D) Grafana has a built-in performance dashboard at /d/performance — it auto-detects issues
✅ Correct Answer: C

Diagnose from: Browser F12→Network (slow API calls), Performance tab (React render), grafana.log (backend), Grafana's /metrics (self-monitoring), DB query analysis.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Performance Diagnosis — Multi-Layer Approach
  DIAGNOSE SLOW GRAFANA:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FRONTEND: Browser DevTools (F12)            │
  │  ├ Network → sort by time → slow /api calls? │
  │  └ Performance → JS rendering bottlenecks?   │
  │                                              │
  │  BACKEND: Server-side                        │
  │  ├ grafana.log → "duration=" for slow ops    │
  │  ├ /metrics → grafana_api_request_duration   │
  │  └ DB → slow PostgreSQL queries on grafana DB│
  │                                              │
  │  DATA SOURCES:                               │
  │  └ Are Prometheus/PG slow? (not Grafana)     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Scrape Grafana's /metrics endpoint with Prometheus for self-monitoring.

Troubleshoot Q196 of 200
Where are Grafana server logs and how do you read them?
A) Default: /var/log/grafana/grafana.log (Linux) or stdout (Docker/K8s). Log levels: debug, info, warn, error, critical. Key patterns to look for: "Failed to connect to database", "Plugin unavailable", "Alert evaluation failed", "Request Completed" with status codes and durations
B) Grafana doesn't produce server logs — all diagnostics are in the browser console only
C) Logs are stored in the Grafana database, not in files — query the "logs" table via API
D) Grafana logs are encrypted and can only be read by Grafana Labs support engineers
✅ Correct Answer: A

/var/log/grafana/grafana.log (Linux) or stdout (Docker/K8s). Levels: debug, info, warn, error, critical. Key patterns: "Failed to connect", "Plugin unavailable", "Alert eval failed", "Request Completed" with durations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Grafana Logs — Location & Key Patterns
  LOG LOCATIONS:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Linux:  /var/log/grafana/grafana.log        │
  │  Docker: stdout (docker logs grafana)        │
  │  K8s:    kubectl logs deploy/grafana         │
  │                                              │
  │  LOG LEVELS (grafana.ini [log]):             │
  │  debug < info < warn < error < critical      │
  │                                              │
  │  KEY PATTERNS TO SEARCH:                     │
  │  "lvl=eror" → errors                        │
  │  "msg=Failed" → failures                     │
  │  "duration="  → slow operations              │
  │  "status=500" → server errors                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: kubectl logs deploy/grafana -n monitoring --tail=100 | grep lvl=eror

Troubleshoot Q197 of 200
Grafana is using too much memory. What are the likely causes?
A) High memory usage always indicates a memory leak — restart Grafana daily as a workaround
B) Too many dashboards with large time ranges (caching results), too many concurrent users (each session consumes memory), data source response caching (large query results kept in memory), or too many plugins loaded. Check Grafana's /metrics for grafana_memory_* metrics
C) Memory usage is fixed — Grafana allocates all memory at startup and never changes
D) High memory usage only occurs with Grafana Cloud — self-hosted uses minimal memory
✅ Correct Answer: B

Too many dashboards with large time ranges (query caching), many concurrent users (session memory), large query result caching, too many plugins. Monitor grafana_memory_* metrics via /metrics.

📝 Detailed Explanation
High Memory — Causes & Mitigations
  GRAFANA HIGH MEMORY — CHECK:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ☐ Query caching: Large time ranges × many   │
  │    dashboards = massive cached results       │
  │    → Reduce cache TTL, limit max queries     │
  │                                              │
  │  ☐ Concurrent users: Each session holds      │
  │    query results in memory                   │
  │    → External cache (Redis/memcached)         │
  │                                              │
  │  ☐ Plugin memory: Some plugins are heavy     │
  │    → Audit installed plugins                 │
  │                                              │
  │  ☐ DB connections: max_open_conn too high    │
  │    → Each connection consumes memory         │
  │                                              │
  │  K8s: Set resource limits + monitor metrics  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Set K8s resource limits, monitor grafana_memory_* via Prometheus.

Troubleshoot Q198 of 200
Grafana can't save dashboards — database errors. What's happening?
A) "Can't save" means the user doesn't have Editor permissions — it's always an RBAC issue
B) Restart Grafana — database errors are always resolved by a restart
C) SQLite: database is locked (multiple Grafana instances accessing the same grafana.db — switch to PostgreSQL for HA), or disk is full. PostgreSQL: connection pool exhausted (max_open_conn too low), disk full, or database is read-only
D) Database errors mean Grafana's license has expired — renew to unlock database access
✅ Correct Answer: C

SQLite: database locked (multiple instances sharing same file → use PostgreSQL for HA), disk full. PostgreSQL: connection pool exhausted (increase max_open_conn), disk full, DB read-only.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Can't Save — Database Error Diagnosis
  DATABASE SAVE ERRORS — BY DB TYPE:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SQLite:                                     │
  │  "database is locked" → Multiple Grafana     │
  │  instances sharing same grafana.db!          │
  │  → SWITCH TO PostgreSQL for HA setups        │
  │                                              │
  │  PostgreSQL:                                 │
  │  "too many clients" → Increase max_open_conn │
  │  "disk full" → Free space or extend volume   │
  │  "read-only" → Check replica/Fs mount        │
  │                                              │
  │  ALWAYS use PostgreSQL for multi-replica!    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Multiple Grafana replicas → PostgreSQL (not SQLite) as shared backend.

Troubleshoot Q199 of 200
What common misconfiguration causes an OAuth2 redirect loop?
A) OAuth2 redirect loops are caused by browser cache — clearing cookies always fixes it
B) root_url in [server] doesn't match the actual URL users access Grafana at (e.g., root_url is http://localhost:3000 but users access https://grafana.company.com). The OAuth provider redirects to the configured root_url, creating a mismatch loop
C) Redirect loops are always caused by an expired OAuth2 client_secret — regenerate it
D) OAuth2 redirect loops are a Grafana bug in version 10 — upgrade to fix
✅ Correct Answer: B

#1 cause: root_url mismatch — root_url says localhost:3000 but users access grafana.company.com. OAuth callback URL doesn't match → redirect loop. Also: domain, secret_key changed, allow_sign_up=false with no admin.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OAuth2 Redirect Loop — Root Cause
  OAUTH2 REDIRECT LOOP — THE MISMATCH:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  grafana.ini: root_url = http://localhost:3K │
  │  User accesses: https://grafana.anihpj.com   │
  │                                              │
  │  ① User → Grafana (grafana.anihpj.com)       │
  │  ② Grafana → OAuth: redirect_uri=localhost:3K│
  │  ③ OAuth: "That's wrong!" → redirect loop    │
  │                                              │
  │  FIX: root_url = https://grafana.anihpj.com  │
  │  root_url MUST match the accessed URL!        │
  │                                              │
  │  Other causes: domain mismatch, secret_key   │
  │  changed (all sessions invalidated)          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Always set root_url=https://grafana.anihpj.com exactly matching user URL.

Troubleshoot Q200 of 200
The Ultimate Grafana Troubleshooting Flowchart — what's the first step for ANY issue?
A) Immediately restart Grafana — it solves 90% of problems and takes only 30 seconds
B) Check the Grafana community forums — someone else has probably already solved your issue
C) Roll back to the last known good Grafana version — issues are usually caused by upgrades
D) NODE 1: Is Grafana itself running? → NODE 2: Is the data source healthy? → NODE 3: Does the query work in Explore? → NODE 4: Check server logs → NODE 5: Check browser console → NODE 6: Isolate (new dashboard, simple query) → NODE 7: Restart as last resort, check disk, check DB integrity
✅ Correct Answer: D

Systematic approach: ① Grafana running? ② DS healthy? (Save & Test) ③ Query works in Explore? ④ Server logs ⑤ Browser console (F12) ⑥ Isolate (new dashboard + simple panel) ⑦ Last resort: restart, disk, DB.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Universal Troubleshooting Flowchart — The 7-Step Method
  SYSTEMATIC GRAFANA TROUBLESHOOTING:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Grafana running? → port 3000, process    │
  │  ② Data source healthy? → Save & Test       │
  │  ③ Query works in Explore? → isolate query   │
  │  ④ Server logs → /var/log/grafana/grafana.log│
  │  ⑤ Browser → F12 Console → JS/CORS errors?  │
  │  ⑥ Isolate → new dashboard + simple panel    │
  │  ⑦ Last resort → restart, disk, DB integrity │
  │                                              │
  │  Follow in ORDER — saves hours of debugging  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj: Follow this flowchart systematically — saves hours vs random fixes.
